is a natural equivalence
                                      𝐺𝑏 𝑓! ≃ 𝑓! 𝐺𝑎 .

Schemes
0.11.15. Following the Grothendieck school [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, Exemples 1.22; SGA
4iii , Exposé XVII, 0.12; 67; 101], we say that scheme 𝑋 is coherent if and only if 𝑋 is
quasicompact and quasiseparated. All schemes considered in this book will be coherent.




                                           27
Part I
Stratified spaces
In Chapter 1, we recall the Alexandroff topology on a poset. Just as the category of profi-
nite sets can be identified with that of Stone topological spaces, the category of profinite
posets be identified with that of spectral topological spaces. These topological spaces
allow one to define stratifications of topological spaces over finite and profinite posets.
    The homotopy theory of spaces stratified over a poset 𝑃 is introduced in Chapter 2.
These can be described in two equivalent ways: as a ∞-category with a conservative
functor to a poset and as a spatial décollage – a diagram of spaces indexed by the sub-
division of 𝑃 that satifies a Segal condition. These descriptions are equivalent, and they
both permit one to study the Postnikov tower of stratified spaces and identify finiteness
conditions on them.




                                            28
1      Aide-mémoire on the topology of posets & profinite
       posets
In this short chapter we review the topologies on posets, and stratifications of topologi-
cal spaces by posets. We also recall Hochster’s Theorem classifying spectral topological
spaces in terms of proöbjects in finite posets (Theorem 1.3.5).

1.1      Alexandroff Duality
We start by reviewing the relationship between topological spaces and preorders. The
first thing to note is that every topological space gives rise to a preorder.

1.1.1 Definition. Let 𝑇 be a topological space. The specialization preorder on 𝑇 is the
preorder on the underlying set of 𝑇 with order relation 𝑥 ≤ 𝑦 if and only if 𝑥 ∈ {𝑦}. We
denote the specialization preorder on 𝑇 by S(𝑇).
    Every preorder also gives rise to a topological space.

1.1.2 Definition. Let 𝑃 be a preorder.
(1.1.2.1) We say that a subset 𝑈 ⊆ 𝑃 a cosieve if for any points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞,
          if 𝑝 ∈ 𝑈 then 𝑞 ∈ 𝑈.
(1.1.2.2) We say that a subset 𝑍 ⊆ 𝑃 is a sieve if for any points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞,
          if 𝑞 ∈ 𝑍 then 𝑝 ∈ 𝑍.

(1.1.2.3) We say that subset 𝑊 ⊆ 𝑃 is an interval if for any points 𝑝, 𝑞, 𝑟 ∈ 𝑃 such that
          𝑝 ≤ 𝑞 ≤ 𝑟, if 𝑝, 𝑟 ∈ 𝑊 then 𝑞 ∈ 𝑊.
    The Alexandroff topology on 𝑃 is the topology on the underlying set of 𝑃 in which a
subset 𝑈 ⊆ 𝑃 is open if and only if 𝑈 is a cosieve. We write Alex(𝑃) or simply 𝑃 for the
set 𝑃 equipped with the Alexandroff topology.
1.1.3. Note that, a subset 𝑍 ⊆ 𝑃 is closed if and only if 𝑍 is a sieve, and subset 𝑊 ⊆ 𝑃 is
locally closed if and only if 𝑊 is an interval.
    Alexandroff topologies admit a well-known characterization.

1.1.4 Proposition. The following are equivalent for a topological space 𝑇.
      → The space 𝑇 is finitely generated; that is, a subset 𝑈 ⊆ 𝑇 is open if for any finite
        topological space 𝐹 and continuous map 𝑓 ∶ 𝐹 → 𝑇, the inverse image 𝑓−1 (𝑈) is
        open.
      → The union of any collection of closed subsets of 𝑇 is again closed.

      → The topology on 𝑇 coincides with the Alexandroff topology attached to the special-
        ization preorder on 𝑇.




                                              29
1.1.5 (Alexandroff Duality). The formation of the Alexandroff topology defines an equiv-
alence of categories
                                Alex ∶ Pord ⥲ TSpcfg
from the category of preorders to the category of finitely generated topological spaces.
The inverse S ∶ TSpcfg ⥲ Pord given by taking the specialization preorder. In partic-
ular, the functors Alex and S restrict to an equivalence between the category of finite
preorders and the category of finite topological spaces.
    The functors Alex and S also restrict to an equivalence between:
    → the category of posets and the category of T0 finitely generated topological spaces,
    → the category of noetherian preorders (i.e., those for which every nonempty subset
      contains a maximal element) and the category of quasi-sober finitely generated
      topological spaces, and thus

    → the category of noetherian posets and the category of sober finitely generated topo-
      logical spaces.
1.1.6 Notation. Let 𝑃 be a preorder. For any subset 𝑊 ⊆ 𝑃, we write 𝑃≥𝑊 for the cosieve
generated by 𝑊, which is the smallest open neighborhood of 𝑊. Dually, we write 𝑃≤𝑊
for the sieve generated by 𝑊, which is the closure of 𝑊.
    In particular, we will refer to the sets of the form

                                  𝑃≥𝑝 = {𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 | 𝑞 ≥ 𝑝}

for 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, which are sometimes called the principal open sets, and the sets of the form

                                  𝑃≤𝑝 = {𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 | 𝑞 ≤ 𝑝} ,

which are sometimes called the principal ideals.
   Similarly, we write

                     𝑃>𝑝 ≔ 𝑃≥𝑝 ∖ {𝑝}       and       𝑃<𝑝 ≔ 𝑃≤𝑝 ∖ {𝑝} .

1.1.7. A poset is quasicompact in the Alexandroff topology if and only if its set of min-
imal elements is finite and limit-cofinal. A monotone map 𝑓 ∶ 𝑄 → 𝑃 of posets is quasi-
compact if and only if, for any 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, the poset 𝑓−1 (𝑃≥𝑝 ) is quasicompact.
1.1.8 Notation. Let 𝑃 be a poset. We call a nonempty linearly ordered finite subset 𝛴 ⊂ 𝑃
a chain in 𝑃. We write sd(𝑃) for the subdivision of 𝑃; that is, sd(𝑃) is the poset of chains
𝛴 ⊂ 𝑃 ordered by containment.
    Note that there is a natural forgetful functor sd(𝑃) → 𝚫.
    Let 𝛴 ⊂ 𝑃 be a chain. Then every closed subset 𝑍 ⊂ 𝛴 is again a chain, and we denote
the inclusion 𝑍 ⊂ 𝛴 by 𝑖𝑍⊂𝛴 (or simply 𝑖 if 𝑍 and 𝛴 are clear from the context). Dually,
every open subset 𝑈 ⊂ 𝛴 is also a chain, and we denote the inclusion 𝑈 ⊂ 𝛴 by 𝑗𝑍⊂𝛴 (or
simply 𝑗 if 𝑈 and 𝛴 are clear from the context).
    In more general situations, we write 𝑒𝑊⊂𝛴 ∶ 𝑊 ↪ 𝛴 for an inclusion 𝑊 ⊂ 𝛴 that is
not known to be be either closed or open.


                                            30
1.2     Stratifications of topological spaces
The theory of stratified topological spaces can now be neatly organized in terms of topo-
logical spaces equipped with a continuous map to a poset in the Alexandroff topology.

1.2.1 Definition. A stratification of a topological space 𝑇 by a poset 𝑃 is a continuous
map 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑃. For any point 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, we write

                                            𝑇≥𝑝 ≔ 𝑓−1 (𝑃≥𝑝 ) ,
                                            𝑇>𝑝 ≔ 𝑓−1 (𝑃>𝑝 ) ,
                                            𝑇≤𝑝 ≔ 𝑓−1 (𝑃≤𝑝 ) ,
                                            𝑇<𝑝 ≔ 𝑓−1 (𝑃<𝑝 ) ,
                                              𝑇𝑝 ≔ 𝑇≥𝑝 ∩ 𝑇≤𝑝 .

The subspaces 𝑇≥𝑝 and 𝑇>𝑝 are open in 𝑇, and 𝑇≤𝑝 and 𝑇<𝑝 are closed in 𝑇. The subspace
𝑇𝑝 ⊂ 𝑇 is locally closed; we call 𝑇𝑝 the 𝑝th stratum of 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑃.
    We say that the stratification 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑃 is nondegenerate if each stratum 𝑇𝑝 is
nonempty, and for all 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, we have 𝑇𝑝 ⊆ 𝑇𝑞 .
    We say that a stratification is finite if and only if its base poset is finite. We say that
the stratification 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑃 is constructible if and only if, for all 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, the open subset
𝑇≥𝑝 ⊂ 𝑇 is retrocompact – i.e., for every quasicompact open 𝑉 ⊂ 𝑇, the intersection
𝑉 ∩ 𝑇≥𝑝 is quasicompact.

1.3     Hochster Duality
In this section we recall Hochster’s characterization of topological spaces that arise as the
Zariski space of a coherent scheme in terms of proöbjects in the category of finite posets.
This characterization provides a convenient way to study stratifications of schemes.
1.3.1 Notation. For any topological space 𝑇, we write FC(𝑇) for the 1-category of finite,
nondegenerate, constructible stratifications 𝑇 → 𝑃. We abuse notation and write merely
𝑃 for an object 𝑇 → 𝑃 of this category, leaving the structure morphism implicit. The 1-
category FC(𝑇) is, up to equivalence, an inverse poset in which 𝑃 ≤ 𝑄 if and only if 𝑃
refines 𝑄. That is, 𝑃 ≤ 𝑄 if and only if the structure morphism 𝑇 → 𝑄 factors through
the structure morphism 𝑇 → 𝑃.

1.3.2 Definition. A topological space 𝑆 is spectral. 11 if and only if 𝑆 is the limit of its
finite, nondegenerate, constructible stratifications; that is, if and only if the natural map

                                              𝑆 → lim 𝑃
                                                     𝑃∈FC(𝑆)

is an isomorphism the 1-category of topological spaces. We write TSpcspec ⊂ TSpc for
the subcategory of spectral topological spaces and quasicompact continuous maps.
  11
     Others call such topological spaces coherent; see for example [SAG, A.1; 73, Chapter III §3.4 & p. 78]. We
use Hochster’s algebro-geometric terminology [57; 58].




                                                     31
1.3.3 Notation. We write the Pos for 1-category of posets, and Posﬁn for the 1-category
of finite posets. We regard the 1-category Pos as a full subcategory of Cat∞ ; indeed one
has Pos ≃ Cat0 .
     Passing to proöbjects, we obtain the 1-category Pro(Pos) of proposets and the full
subcategory Pro(Posﬁn ) of proöbjects in the category of finite posets – which we call
profinite posets.
1.3.4. The formation of the Alexandroff topology extends to an equivalence of 1-categor-
ies
                             Alex ∶ Pro(Posﬁn ) ⥲ TSpcspec .
We will therefore fail to distinguish between a spectral topological space and its corre-
sponding profinite poset.
   Hochster’s characterization of spectral topological spaces justifies their name:
1.3.5 Theorem (Hochster Duality [57; 58]). The following are equivalent for a topological
space 𝑆.
    → The topological space 𝑆 is spectral.
    → The topological space 𝑆 is sober, quasicompact, and quasiseparated; additionally, the
      set of quasicompact open subsets forms a basis for the topology of 𝑆.
    → The topological space 𝑆 is homeomorphic to the underlying Zariski topological space
      of Spec 𝑅 for some ring 𝑅.
    → The topological space 𝑆 is homeomorphic to the underlying Zariski topological space
      of some coherent scheme 𝑌.
1.3.6. On one hand, Alexandroff Duality characterizes posets as finitely generated topo-
logical spaces. On the other, Stone Duality characterizes profinite sets as Stone topologi-
cal spaces, i.e., totally separated quasicompact topological spaces. Hochster Duality pro-
vides a common extension of each of these forms of duality. The situation is summarized
in the cube
                                       Setﬁn      ∼      TSpcﬁn,disc


                       Pro(Setﬁn )           ∼        TSpcStn


                                     Posﬁn            ∼          TSpcﬁn


                      Pro(Posﬁn )       ∼             TSpcspec            ,

where TSpcﬁn denotes the 1-category of finite spectral topological spaces, and the hori-
zontal functors marked ‘∼’ are equivalences of 1-categories.
   One of the main technical results of this book – the ∞-Categorical Hochster Duality
Theorem (Theorem 0.4.6=Theorem 9.3.1) – is an extension of this cube of dualities to


                                                 32
one in which the 1-category of finite sets is replaced with the ∞-category of π-finite
∞-groupoids. Part of this extension is already established in the literature: Lurie proves
an ∞-categorical form of Stone Duality [SAG, §E.3]. This ∞-Categorical Stone Duality
Theorem identifies the ∞-category of profinite ∞-groupoids with the ∞-category of
what we call Stone ∞-topoi.12

1.4     Profinite stratifications
The theory of stratifications also works well for profinite stratifications.

1.4.1 Definition. A profinite stratification of a topological space 𝑇 is a spectral topo-
logical space 𝑆 and a continuous map 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑆. We say that 𝑓 is constructible if and
only if, for every quasicompact open subset 𝑈 ⊆ 𝑆, the inverse image 𝑓−1 (𝑈) ⊆ 𝑇 is
retrocompact.

1.4.2. A profinite stratification with base 𝑆 is the same as a compatible family of strati-
fications with base 𝑃 for each nondegenerate, finite, constructible stratification 𝑆 → 𝑃.
1.4.3 Notation. Let 𝑋 be a scheme. We write 𝑋zar for the underlying Zariski topological
space of 𝑋.
1.4.4 Example. Let 𝑋 be a scheme of finite type over the complex numbers. Write 𝑋an
for the set 𝑋(𝐂) of complex points of 𝑋 equipped with the complex analytic topology.
Then the natural continuous map 𝑋an → 𝑋zar is a profinite stratification of 𝑋an by 𝑋zar .




   12
      Lurie calls these profinite ∞-topoi. In Chapter 9 we introduce a more general class of ∞-topoi that could
also reasonably be called ‘profinite ∞-topoi’, so we use the distinct term ‘Stone ∞-topoi’ to avoid confusion.


                                                     33
2      The homotopy theory of stratified spaces
In this chapter we develop the homotopy theory of stratified spaces. To start, § 2.1 ex-
plains how to think about the homotopy theory of stratified spaces in terms of ∞-cate-
gories with a conservative functor to a poset. Section 2.2 explains how the ∞-categories
of stratified spaces relate as the poset varies. Section 2.3 explains the correct notions of
connectedness and truncatedness for stratified spaces. Section 2.4 explains the analogue
of π-finite spaces (i.e., truncated spaces with finite homotopy groups) in the stratified set-
ting. These π-finite stratified spaces are crucial in our formulation of one of the main re-
sults of this text: ∞-Categorical Hochster Duality (Theorem 0.4.6). Section 2.5 explains
the theory of profinite stratified spaces. Sections 2.6 and 2.7 explain a complete Segal
space style approach to stratified spaces that we’ll use again and again throughout the
text; the power of this approach is that it reduces many questions about stratifications
over a general poset 𝑃 to questions about strata and links (and essentially to the poset
𝑃 = [1]). Finally, § 2.8 explains how the complete Segal space approach works in the
profinite setting.

2.1     Stratified spaces as ∞-categories with a conservative functor to a
        poset
The equivalence between the homotopy theory of topological spaces and that of simpli-
cial sets – supplied in the 1950s and 1960s by the work of Kan and Quillen [78; 79; 80; 81;
82; 105] – justifies the treatment of the ∞-category of Kan complexes as ‘the’ homotopy
theory of spaces. Today, the theory of stratified spaces stands on similarly good footing.
Work of Ayala–Francis–Rozenblyum [10], Nand-Lal–Woolf [96; 97], Douteau [33], and
finally the third-named author [51] furnish an equivalence between the homotopy the-
ory of stratified topological spaces and that of ∞-categories with a conservative functor
to a poset.
     In this section we breifly recall this result and begin to develop the theory of stratified
spaces as ∞-categories with a conservative functor to a poset. To state the core result,
we need to fix a convenient category of topological spaces.

2.1.1 Notation. We write TSpcng ⊂ TSpc for the full subcategory spanned by the nu-
merically generated topological spaces. The category TSpcng is a convenient category of
topological spaces and is presentable; see [35; 38; 52; 53, §3; 124]. Moreover, every poset
in the Alexandroff topology is a numerically generated topological space.
2.1.2 Theorem ([51, §3]). Let 𝑃 be a poset. There is a class 𝑊 of stratified weak equiva-
                           ng
lences in the category TSpc/𝑃 with the following properties.
      → There is an equivalence of ∞-categories
                                           ng
        (2.1.3)                      TSpc/𝑃 [𝑊−1 ] ⥲ Catcons
                                                        ∞,/𝑃 ,

        where the target is the ∞-category of ∞-categories with a conservative functor to
        𝑃.



                                                34
     → Let 𝑇 be a 𝑃-stratified topological space whose stratification is conical in the sense
       of Lurie [HA, Definition A.5.5], e.g., 𝑇 is a topologically stratified in the sense of
       Goresky–MacPherson [44, §1.1]. Then the equivalence (2.1.3) sends 𝑇 to the exit
       path ∞-category of 𝑇.
     → If 𝑇 and 𝑇′ are conically 𝑃-stratified spaces, then a 𝑃-stratified map 𝑓 ∶ 𝑇 → 𝑇′ is
       a stratified weak equivalence if and only if 𝑓 induces a weak homotopy equivalence
       on strata and links.
2.1.4. An ∞-category 𝐶 admits a conservative functor to a poset if and only if every
endomorphism of an object of 𝐶 is an equivalence. In this case, the homotopy 0-category
h0 (𝐶) is a poset and the natural functor 𝐶 → h0 (𝐶) is conservative.
    We therefore give the following definition.
2.1.5 Definition. We define the ∞-category Str as the full subcategory of Fun([1], Cat∞ )
spanned by those functors 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱 → 𝑃 where 𝑃 is a poset and the functor 𝑓 is conserva-
tive. We call an object of Str a stratified space.
    Let 𝑃 be a poset. We write Str𝑃 for the fiber the target functor t ∶ Str → Pos over
𝑃. That is to say, Str𝑃 ≃ Catcons
                              ∞,/𝑃 is the ∞-category of ∞-categories with a conservative
functor to 𝑃. We call an object of Str𝑃 a 𝑃-stratified space.
2.1.6. The ∞-category Str𝑃 can also be described as the underlying ∞-category of the
third-named author’s Joyal–Kan model category sSet/𝑃 [51, Corollary 2.5.11].
2.1.7 Notation. Please observe that if 𝛱 and 𝛱′ are 𝑃-stratified spaces, then the ∞-cat-
egory Fun𝑃 (𝛱, 𝛱′ ) of functors 𝛱 → 𝛱′ over 𝑃 is an ∞-groupoid. Thus Fun𝑃 (𝛱, 𝛱′ )
coincides with the mapping space MapStr𝑃 (𝛱, 𝛱′ ). To simplify notation we write

                                  Map𝑃 (𝛱, 𝛱′ ) ≔ MapStr𝑃 (𝛱, 𝛱′ ) .

2.1.8 Definition. Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱 → 𝑃 be a 𝑃-stratified space. For each point 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, we call
the space
                           𝛱𝑝 ≔ Map𝑃 ({𝑝}, 𝛱) ≃ {𝑝} ×𝑃 𝛱
the 𝑝-th stratum of 𝛱. For each pair of points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 with 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, we call the space

                                N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} ≔ Map𝑃 ({𝑝 ≤ 𝑞}, 𝛱)

the link13 from the 𝑝-th stratum to the 𝑞-th stratum.
    Please observe that the link comes equipped with source and target maps

                                 (s, t) ∶ N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} → 𝛱𝑝 × 𝛱𝑞 ,

and the fiber of (s, t) over a point (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈ 𝛱𝑝 × 𝛱𝑞 is the mapping space Map𝛱 (𝑥, 𝑦).
When 𝑝 = 𝑞, each of s and t is an equivalence, whence (s, t) is equivalent to the diagonal

                                            𝛱𝑝 → 𝛱𝑝 × 𝛱𝑝 .
   13
      Our link corresponds to what Frank Quinn and others called the homotopy link or holink. The significance
of our chosen notation will become clear in Construction 2.7.1.


                                                     35
2.1.9. A morphism 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱′ → 𝛱 of Str𝑃 is an equivalence if and only if, for every pair
of points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 with 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, the map on links

                                 N𝑃 (𝛱′ ){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} → N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞}

is an equivalence (in particular, when 𝑝 = 𝑞, the map on strata 𝛱𝑝′ → 𝛱𝑝 is an equiva-
lence). That is, 𝑓 is an equivalence in Str𝑃 if and only if 𝑓 induces and equivalence on
all strata and links.

2.2     Functoriality in the poset
In this section we explain how the ∞-categories of stratified spaces relate as the poset
varies. Notice that if 𝜙 ∶ 𝑃′ → 𝑃 is a morphism of posets, then the functor

                                         Cat∞,/𝑃′ → Cat∞,/𝑃

given by postcomposition with 𝜙 does not generally send 𝑃′ -stratified spaces to 𝑃-strat-
ified spaces. However, we can easily repair this by inverting all morphisms that lie over
identities in 𝑃. To explain this point, let us first explain the left and right adjoints to the
inclusion Str𝑃 ⊂ Cat∞,/𝑃 .
     To begin with, we fix some notation.
2.2.1 Notation. We write ι ∶ Cat∞ → S for the right adjoint to the inclusion, given by
sending an ∞-category 𝐶 to the largest ∞-groupoid ι𝐶 ⊆ 𝐶 contained in 𝐶. We call ι𝐶
the interior of 𝐶.
    We write ε ∶ Cat∞ → S for the left adjoint to the inclusion. The functor ε is given
by sending an ∞-category 𝐶 to the ∞-groupoid ε(𝐶) obtained by inverting every mor-
phism of 𝐶. We call ε(𝐶) the classifying space of 𝐶.14 The ∞-groupoid ε(𝐶) can be com-
puted as the colimit ε(𝐶) ≃ colim𝐶 1S of the constant diagram 𝐶 → S at the terminal
object.
2.2.2 Construction. Let 𝑃 be a poset. Then inclusion Str𝑃 ↪ Cat∞,/𝑃 admits a right
adjoint ι𝑃 ∶ Str𝑃 → Cat∞,/𝑃 . Indeed, if 𝐶 is an ∞-category, and 𝑓 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝑃 is any functor,
we write ι𝑃 (𝐶) ⊂ 𝐶 for the largest subcategory of 𝐶 with the property that the composite

                                                             𝑓
                                         ι𝑃 (𝐶)         𝐶        𝑃

is conservative. Concretely, ι𝑃 (𝐶) ⊂ 𝐶 is the subcategory containing all objects such that
morphism 𝑒 of 𝐶 lies in ι𝑃 (𝐶) if and only if 𝑒 satisfies one of the following (disjoint)
conditions:
      → The morphism 𝑒 is an equivalence.
      → The morphism 𝑒 is not sent to an identity morphism in 𝑃.
   14
      In simplicial sets the functor ε can be modeled as Kan’s Ex∞ functor. The notation B𝐶 is often used for
the classifying space of 𝐶. We use the notation ε(𝐶) for three reasons: to avoid conflict with the notation B𝐺
for the 1-object groupoid with automorphism group 𝐺, to pay homage to Kan’s Ex∞ functor, and because ‘ε’
stands for everything in the phrase invert everything.


                                                     36
   Given a 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱, every functor 𝛱 → 𝐶 over 𝑃 factors through ι𝑃 (𝐶).
Hence the assignment 𝐶 ↦ ι𝑃 (𝐶) defines a right adjoint to the inclusion Str𝑃 ⊂ Cat∞,/𝑃 .
2.2.3 Construction. Let 𝑃 be a poset. Then the inclusion Str𝑃 ↪ Cat∞,/𝑃 admits a
left adjoint ε𝑃 ∶ Str𝑃 → Cat∞,/𝑃 . Indeed, if 𝐶 is an ∞-category, and 𝑓 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝑃 is any
functor, we can formally invert those morphisms of 𝐶 that are sent to identities in 𝑃 by
forming the pullback
                                  ε𝑃 (𝐶) ≔ ε(𝐶) ×ε(𝑃) 𝑃
in Cat∞ . Note that the second projection ε𝑃 (𝐶) → 𝑃 is conservative: for each 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃 we
have
                             ε𝑃 (𝐶) ×𝑃 {𝑝} ≃ ε(𝐶) ×ε(𝑃) {𝑝} ,
so that ε𝑃 (𝐶) ×𝑃 {𝑝} is the fiber of a map between ∞-groupoids. We regard ε𝑃 (𝐶) as a
𝑃-stratified space via the second projection ε𝑃 (𝐶) → 𝑃.
    The functor 𝑓 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝑃 and the unit 𝐶 → ε(𝐶) induce a natural functor 𝐶 → ε𝑃 (𝐶).
By construction, this functor exhibits ε𝑃 (𝐶) as the localization of 𝐶 at those morphisms
that lie over identities in 𝑃. Moreover, the natural functor 𝐶 → ε𝑃 (𝐶) is the unit of the
desired adjunction.
   Now we can describe the functionality of the construction 𝑃 ↦ Str𝑃 .
2.2.4. Let 𝜙 ∶ 𝑃′ → 𝑃 be a morphism of posets. Since the pullback of a conservative
functor is conservative, the pullback functor
                         𝜙∗ ≔ (−) ×𝑃 𝑃′ ∶ Cat∞,/𝑃 → Cat∞,/𝑃′
carries 𝑃-stratified spaces to 𝑃′ -stratfied spaces. The pullback functor 𝜙∗ ∶ Str𝑃 → Str𝑃′
admits a left adjoint 𝜙! given by the composite
                                                               ε𝑃
                              Str𝑃′             Cat∞,/𝑃                 Str𝑃

of postcomposition with 𝜙 followed by the left adjoint to the inclusion Str𝑃 ⊂ Cat∞,/𝑃 .
2.2.5 Proposition. The target functor t ∶ Str → Pos is a bicartesian fibration.
Proof. Let 𝜙 ∶ 𝑃′ → 𝑃 be a morphism of posets. If 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱 → 𝑃 is a 𝑃-stratified space, the
resulting pullback square
                                      𝑃′ ×𝑃 𝛱                   𝛱
                                            ⌟
                                      ∗                             𝑓
                                  𝜙 (𝑓)

                                           𝑃′          𝜙
                                                                𝑃

is a t-cartesian morphism lying over 𝜙.
     In the other direction, let 𝑓′ ∶ 𝛱′ → 𝑃′ be a 𝑃′ -stratified space. Then formally in-
verting those morphisms of 𝛱′ that are sent to identities by 𝜙 ∘ 𝑓′ , we see that the re-
sulting square
                                      𝛱′               ε𝑃 (𝛱′ )
                                  𝑓′                       𝜙! (𝑓′ )

                                      𝑃′         𝜙
                                                           𝑃


                                                  37
is a t-cocartesian morphism of Str lying over 𝜙.
       We can use the pullbacks to describe limits in Str:
2.2.6. To compute the limit of a diagram 𝛼 ↦ [𝛱𝛼 → 𝑃𝛼 ] in Str, we first form the limit
𝑃 ≔ lim𝛼 𝑃𝛼 ; then pulling back along the various projections 𝑝𝛼 ∶ 𝑃 → 𝑃𝛼 , we obtain
the diagram 𝛼 ↦ 𝑝∗𝛼 𝛱𝛼 of 𝑃-stratified spaces. We then form the limit 𝛱 ≔ lim𝛼 𝛱𝛼 in
Str𝑃 . If the diagram is connected, then the limit lim𝛼 𝛱𝛼 is computed in Cat∞ .

2.3        The stratified Postnikov tower
In this section we investigate a Postnikov tower for stratified spaces. Importantly, the
correct notion of ‘𝑛-truncatedness’ is not the notion of 𝑛-truncatedness interntal to the
∞-category Str𝑃 (in the sense of [HTT, §5.5.6]); rather it corresponds to the categorical
level of the stratified space.15 For this, recall that we write h𝑛 ∶ Cat∞ → Cat𝑛 for the left
adjoint to the inclusion Cat𝑛 ⊂ Cat∞ (0.11.4).
2.3.1 Definition. Let 𝑃 be a poset and 𝛱 a 𝑃-stratified space. We call the tower of 𝑃-
stratified spaces

                           𝛱 → ⋯ → h3 𝛱 → h2 𝛱 → h1 𝛱 → h0 𝛱 → 𝑃 ,

the stratified Postnikov tower of 𝛱.
    In particular, please observe that h0 𝛱 → 𝑃 is a morphism of posets.
2.3.2. If 𝑃 = {0}, then the stratified Postnikov tower coincides with the usual Postnikov
tower of spaces.
2.3.3. The following are equivalent for a poset 𝑃, a 𝑃-stratified space 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱 → 𝑃, and a
nonnegative integer 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍:
       → the ∞-category 𝛱 is an 𝑛-category;
       → the natural functor 𝛱 → h𝑛 𝛱 is an equivalence;
       → for all objects 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝛱, the space Map𝛱 (𝑥, 𝑦) is (𝑛 − 1)-truncated;
       → for all points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, the source and target map

                                         (s, t) ∶ N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} → 𝛱𝑝 × 𝛱𝑞

          is (𝑛 − 1)-truncated (in particular, when 𝑝 = 𝑞, the stratum 𝛱𝑝 is 𝑛-truncated).
2.3.4 Definition. Let 𝑃 be a poset and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. We say that a 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱 is 𝑛-
truncated if 𝛱 satisfies the equivalent conditions of (2.3.3). We write Str𝑃,≤𝑛 ⊂ Str𝑃 for
the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑛-truncated 𝑃-stratified spaces.
    We caution that an 𝑛-truncated 𝑃-stratified space is generally not the same thing as
an 𝑛-truncated object of the ∞-category Str𝑃 in the sense of [HTT, Definition 5.5.6.1].
Nor is it the same thing as a 𝑃-stratified space whose strata are 𝑛-truncated; truncated-
ness in our sense involves a condition on the links as well.
  15
       Recall that an 𝑛-category is an 𝑛-truncated object of Cat∞ , but the converse is false.


                                                         38
2.3.5. Dually, the following are equivalent for a poset 𝑃, a 𝑃-stratified space 𝑓 ∶ 𝛱 → 𝑃,
and a nonnegative integer 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍:
       → the natural functor h𝑛 𝛱 → 𝑃 is an equivalence;
       → for all objects 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝛱 such that 𝑓(𝑥) ≤ 𝑓(𝑦), the space Map𝛱 (𝑥, 𝑦) is (𝑛 − 1)-
         connected;
       → the strata of 𝛱 are nonempty and for all points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that 𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, the map

                                        (s, t) ∶ N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} → 𝛱𝑝 × 𝛱𝑞

          is (𝑛 − 1)-connected (in particular, when 𝑝 = 𝑞, the stratum 𝛱𝑝 is 𝑛-connected).

2.3.6 Definition. Let 𝑃 be a poset and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. We say that a 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱 is 𝑛-
connected if 𝛱 satisfies the equivalent conditions of (2.3.5). We write Str𝑃,≥𝑛 ⊂ Str𝑃 for
the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑛-connected 𝑃-stratified spaces.
       We can easily identify the 0-connected stratified spaces.

2.3.7 Definition. We say that a 1-category 𝐶 is layered16 if and only if every endomor-
phism of an object of 𝐶 is an isomorphism. We say that an ∞-category 𝛱 is layered if and
only if its homotopy category h1 (𝛱) is a layered 1-category. This holds if and only if the
natural functor 𝛱 → h0 (𝛱) is conservative. Thus a layered ∞-category 𝛱 is naturally
an h0 (𝛱)-stratified space.
    We write Lay∞ for the full subcategory of Cat∞ spanned by the layered ∞-cate-
gories.
2.3.8. The assignment [𝛱 → 𝑃] ↦ 𝛱 defines a functor Str → Lay∞ with a fully faithful
left adjoint that carries 𝛱 to the h0 (𝛱)-stratified space 𝛱. Consequently, we obtain an
identification
                                       Lay∞ ≃ Str≥0 .
Here Str≥0 ⊂ Str is the full subcategory spanned by the 0-connected stratified spaces.

2.4        π-finite stratified spaces
In this section we introduce the key finiteness condition that we impose on almost all of
the stratified spaces we consider in this book. This finiteness condition is the stratified
version of π-finiteness for spaces.
2.4.1 Recollection ([SAG, Definition E.0.7.8]). An ∞-groupoid 𝐾 is π-finite if and only
if the following conditions are satisfied.
       → The set π0 (𝐾) is finite.

       → For every point 𝑥 ∈ 𝐾 and any 𝑖 ≥ 1, the group π𝑖 (𝐾, 𝑥) is finite.
       → The ∞-groupoid 𝐾 is 𝑛-truncated for some 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍.
  16
       Layered categories are often called EI categories.


                                                        39
We write Sπ ⊂ S for the full subcategory spanned by the π-finite ∞-groupoids.
2.4.2 Warning. We caution that a π-finite space is not the same thing as what is nor-
mally called a finite space – one obtained via finite colimits from the point. In fact, the
overlap between these two classes of spaces is essentially trivial: the spaces satisfying
both of these conditions are exactly the discrete spaces with finitely many connected
components.
    We now define the analogous condition for a stratified space.
2.4.3 Definition. We say that a stratified space 𝛱 → 𝑃 is π-finite if and only if the
following conditions are satisfied.

      → The poset 𝑃 is finite.
      → For every point 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, the set π0 (𝛱𝑝 ) is finite.
      → For all 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝛱, the mapping space Map𝛱 (𝑥, 𝑦) is a π-finite space.
      → The ∞-category 𝛱 is an 𝑛-category for some 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍.

In particular, a nondegenerate stratified space 𝛱 → 𝑃 is π-finite if and only if 𝛱 has
finitely many objects up to equivalence and is locally π-finite in the sense that each map-
ping space Map𝛱 (𝑥, 𝑦) is π-finite.
     We write Strπ ⊂ Str for the full subcategory spanned by the π-finite stratified spaces.
Given a finite poset 𝑃, we write Strπ,𝑃 ⊂ Str𝑃 for the full subcategory spanned by the
π-finite 𝑃-stratified spaces.
2.4.4. The target functor t ∶ Strπ → Posﬁn is a cartesian fibration. However, it is not
a cocartesian fibration because the pullback functor doesn’t admit a left adjoint when
restricted to π-finite stratified spaces. To see this, note that the free pair of parallel arrows
0 ⇉ 1 is π-finite as a [1]-stratified space, but its classifying space is equivalent to B𝐙,
which is not π-finite.
    However, the pullback does preserve finite limits, hence has a proëxistent left adjoint;
we will discuss this in §2.5.
2.4.5 Lemma. The full subcategory Strπ ⊂ Str is an accessible subcategory that is closed
under finite limits.

Proof. Finite limits of finite posets are finite, pullbacks of π-finite stratified spaces along
maps of finite posets are π-finite, and finite limits of locally π-finite ∞-categories are
locally π-finite. Finally, Strπ is δ0 -small and idempotent complete.

2.5      Profinite stratified spaces
In light of (0.11.9), Lemma 2.4.5 allows us to speak of profinite stratified spaces in terms
of left exact accessible functors. We now turn to stratifications over profinite posets (i.e.,
spectral topological spaces).




                                               40
2.5.1 Definition. We call objects of the ∞-category Pro(Str) stratified prospaces; the
target functor t ∶ Str → Pos from stratified spaces to posets extends to a target functor

                                   t ∶ Pro(Str) → Pro(Pos) .

Let 𝑃 be a poset, regarded as a constant proposet. The fiber Pro(Str)𝑃 of t over 𝑃 can be
identified with the ∞-category Pro(Str𝑃 ) of proöbjects in Str𝑃 . We refer to objects of
Pro(Str)𝑃 as 𝑃-stratified prospaces.
    Similarly, if 𝑷 is a proposet, then we refer to the fiber Pro(Str)𝑷 of t over 𝑷 as the
∞-category of 𝑷-stratified prospaces.
2.5.2. A stratified prospace can be exhibited as an inverse system {𝛱𝛼 → 𝑃𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴 of strat-
ified spaces. The target functor t carries this stratified prospace to the proposet {𝑃𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴 .
2.5.3 Example. Our primary interest is in stratified prospaces stratified by a spectral
topological space regarded as a profinite poset. Still, in order to reason effectively with
these, it is occasionally necessary to deal with more general stratified prospaces.
    We now turn to the functoriality of the assignment 𝑷 ↦ Pro(Str)𝑷 . The key point
is that the adjunctions relating the ∞-categories Str𝑃 as 𝑃 varies extend to stratified
prospaces.
2.5.4 Construction (pullbacks of stratified prospaces). Please observe that the target
functor
                              t ∶ Pro(Str) → Pro(Pos)
is a cartesian fibration. Indeed, let 𝜙 ∶ 𝑷′ → 𝑷 be a morphism of proposets, and exhibit
𝑷′ and 𝑷 as inverse systems of proposets {𝑃𝛼′ }𝛼∈𝐴 and {𝑃𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴 , respectively. Given a 𝑷-
stratified prospace 𝜫 = {𝛱𝛼 → 𝑃𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴 , we define a 𝑷′ -stratified prospace 𝜙∗ (𝜫) as the
inverse system
                              𝜙∗ (𝜫) ≔ {𝛱𝛼 ×𝑃𝛼 𝑃𝛼′ → 𝑃𝛼′ }𝛼∈𝐴 .
The morphism 𝜙∗ (𝜫) → 𝜫 is a t-cartesian morphism lying over 𝜙 and the assignment
𝜫 ↦ 𝜙∗ (𝜫) defines a functor 𝜙∗ ∶ Pro(Str)𝑷 → Pro(Str)𝑷′ .
    We now describe the left adjoint to this functor.
2.5.5 Construction (Pushforwards of stratified prospaces). Let 𝜂 ∶ 𝑷′ → 𝑃 a morphism
of proposets where 𝑃 is constant. Regarding 𝑷′ as a left exact accessible functor

                                         Pos → Set ,

the morphism 𝜂 defines an element 𝜂 ∈ 𝑷′ (𝑃). For a 𝑷′ -stratified prospace 𝜫′ , there
exists a t-cocartesian edge 𝜫′ → 𝜂! 𝜫′ covering 𝜂; indeed, for any 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱,
we have an equivalence
                              (𝜂! 𝜫′ )(𝛱) ≃ 𝜫′ (𝛱) ′× {𝜂} .
                                                       𝑷 (𝑃)

Equivalently, if we exhibit 𝜫 as an inverse system {𝛱𝛼′ → 𝑃𝛼′ }𝛼∈𝐴 in Str, then the 𝑃-
                               ′

stratified prospace 𝜂! 𝜫′ can be exhibited as the inverse system 𝐴 ×Pos Pos/𝑄 → Str𝑃
given by
                                (𝛼, 𝑃𝛼′ → 𝑃) ↦ ε𝑃 (𝛱𝛼′ ) .


                                             41
Note in particular that if 𝑷′ and 𝜫′ are constant, then so is 𝜂! 𝜫′ .
    In the ∞-category Pro(Str), the inverse system Pos𝑷′ / → Str given by 𝜂 ↦ 𝜂! 𝜫′ is
identified with 𝜫′ itself.
    Given any morphism of proposets 𝜙 ∶ 𝑷′ → 𝑷 and 𝑷′ -stratified prospace 𝜫′ , the
inverse system Pos𝑷/ → Str given by the assignment

                                      𝜂 ↦ (𝜂 ∘ 𝜙)! 𝜫′

defines a 𝑷-stratified prospace 𝜙! 𝜫′ . As this notation suggests, the morphism

                                        𝜫′ → 𝜙 ! 𝜫′

is a t-cocartesian edge over 𝜙. Thus t ∶ Pro(Str) → Pro(Pos) is a cocartesian fibration.
    We thus combine the previous two points:
2.5.6 Proposition. The target functor t ∶ Pro(Str) → Pro(Pos) is a bicartesian fibration.
    We now turn to proöbjects in π-finite stratified spaces.

2.5.7 Definition. A profinite stratified space is a proöbject of the ∞-category Strπ . We
write Str∧π ≔ Pro(Strπ ) for the ∞-category of profinite stratified spaces.
    The target functor

                            t ∶ Str∧π → Pro(Posﬁn ) ≃ TSpcspec

is a cartesian fibration. Given a spectral topological space 𝑆, we write Str∧π,𝑆 for the fiber
of t over 𝑆. We call Str∧π,𝑆 the ∞-category of profinite 𝑆-stratified spaces.
     The inclusion Strπ ↪ Str extends to a fully faithful functor Str∧π ↪ Pro(Str), which
admits a left adjoint 𝜫 ↦ 𝜫π∧ given by restriction of left exact accessible functors. We
call the profinite stratified space 𝜫π∧ the profinite completion of 𝜫.

2.5.8. The profinite completion functor 𝜫 ↦ 𝜫π∧ is not a relative left adjunction over
Pro(Pos); however, the inclusion Strπ ↪ Str does induce a fully faithful functor

                             Str∧π ↪ Pro(Str)      ×       TSpcspec ,
                                                Pro(Pos)

and profinite completion does define a relative left adjoint over TSpcspec . In particular, if
𝑆 is a spectral topological space and 𝜫 is an 𝑆-stratified prospace, then 𝜫π∧ is a profinite
𝑆-stratified space, and the morphism 𝜫 → 𝜫π∧ lies over 𝑆.
2.5.9 Construction (Pushforwards of profinite stratified spaces). Let 𝜙 ∶ 𝑆′ → 𝑆 be a
quasicompact continuous map of spectral topological spaces, and let 𝜫′ → 𝑆′ be a
profinite 𝑆′ -stratified space. Then following Construction 2.5.5, we obtain an 𝑆-stratified
prospace 𝜙! 𝜫′ → 𝑆. Forming the profinite completion (𝜙! 𝜫′ )∧π → 𝑆 of 𝜙! 𝜫′ , we see that
the map
                                        𝜫′ → (𝜙! 𝜫′ )∧π
is a cocartesian edge over 𝜙 for the target functor t ∶ Str∧π → TSpcspec .


                                             42
We thus obtain:
2.5.10 Proposition. The target functor t ∶ Str∧π → TSpcspec is a bicartesian fibration.
2.5.11 Proposition. Let 𝑆 be a spectral topological space. Then the natural functor
                                  Str∧π,𝑆 → lim Str∧π,𝑃
                                                   𝑃∈FC(𝑆)

is an equivalence.
Proof. The formation of the limit in Str∧π is an inverse.

2.6     Complete Segal spaces & spatial décollages
2.6.1 Recollection. An ∞-category can be modeled as a simplicial space. In effect, if 𝐶
is an ∞-category, then one may extract a functor N(𝐶) ∶ 𝚫op → S in which N(𝐶)𝑚 is
the ∞-groupoid of functors [𝑚] → 𝐶 (the ‘moduli space of sequences of arrows in 𝐶’).
The simplicial space N(𝐶) is what Charles Rezk [107] called a complete Segal space – i.e.,
a functor 𝐷 ∶ 𝚫op → S satisfying the following conditions.
(1) For all 𝑚 ∈ 𝐍∗ , the natural map
                  𝐷𝑚 → 𝐷{0 < 1} × 𝐷{1 < 2} × ⋯                   ×      𝐷{𝑚 − 1 < 𝑚}
                                   𝐷{1}                 𝐷{2}   𝐷{𝑚−1}

      is an equivalence.
(2) Let 𝐼 denote the unique contractible 1-groupoid with two objects. Then the map
                                𝐷0 → MapFun(𝚫op ,S) (N(𝐼), 𝐷)
      induced by the projection 𝐼 → {0} is an equivalence.
(This same formalism can be deployed to define category objects in any ∞-category with
finite limits; we exploit this in Section 13.5.)
     Joyal and Tierney [77] showed that the assignment 𝐶 ↦ N(𝐶) defines an equiva-
lence from the ∞-category Cat∞ of ∞-categories to the ∞-category CSS of complete
Segal spaces.
     We can isolate the ∞-groupoids in CSS: an ∞-category 𝐶 is an ∞-groupoid if and
only if N(𝐶) ∶ 𝚫op → S is constant.
   In the remainder of this section and the next, we shall demonstrate that the homo-
topy theory of stratified spaces admits an analogous description.
2.6.2 Notation. For a poset 𝑃, we write sdop (𝑃) ≔ sd(𝑃)op .
2.6.3 Definition. Let 𝑃 be a poset. A functor 𝐷 ∶ sdop (𝑃) → S is a spatial décollage (over
𝑃) if and only if, for every string {𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑚 } ⊆ 𝑃, the map
   𝐷{𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑚 } → 𝐷{𝑝0 < 𝑝1 } × 𝐷{𝑝1 < 𝑝2 } × ⋯                          ×       𝐷{𝑝𝑚−1 < 𝑝𝑚 }
                                          𝐷{𝑝1 }               𝐷{𝑝2 }    𝐷{𝑝𝑚−1 }

is an equivalence. We write
                               Déc𝑃 (S) ⊆ Fun(sdop (𝑃), S)
for the full subcategory spanned by the spatial décollages.


                                                   43
2.6.4 Example. Let 𝑃 be a poset of rank ≤ 1. Then every functor sdop (𝑃) → S automat-
ically satisfies the décollage condition. So in this case,

                               Déc𝑃 (S) = Fun(sdop (𝑃), S) .

    Now we turn to the functoriality of the assignment 𝑃 ↦ Déc𝑃 (S):

2.6.5 Construction (functoriality of spatial décollages). Write

(2.6.6)                         ∫Pos Fun(sdop , S) → Pos

for the cartesian fibration classified by the functor Posop → Cat∞ given by the assign-
ment
                                    𝑃 ↦ Fun(sdop (𝑃), S)
with functoriality given by right Kan extension [HTT, Corollary 3.2.2.13]. Thus the ob-
jects of ∫Pos Fun(sdop , S) consist of a pair (𝑃, 𝐹) of a poset 𝑃 and a functor

                                    𝐹 ∶ sdop (𝑃) → S .

The fiber of (2.6.6) over a poset 𝑃 is the ∞-category Fun(sdop (𝑃), S).
   Let
                                Déc(S) ⊂ ∫Pos Fun(sdop , S)
denote the full subcategory spanned by the pairs (𝑃, 𝐷) in which 𝐷 is a spatial décollage.
Since Déc(S) contains all the cartesian edges, the functor Déc(S) → Pos is a cartesian
fibration.

2.7       The nerve of a stratified space
We now show that the ∞-category Str of stratified spaces and the ∞-category Déc(S)
of décollages are equivalent over Pos.

2.7.1 Construction (nerve of a stratified space). Let 𝑃 be a poset. Any chain contained in
𝑃 can be regarded as a 𝑃-stratified space via the inclusion map. This assignment defines
a functor sd(𝑃) → Str𝑃 . For any 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱, we define the nerve of 𝛱 to be the
functor
                                  N𝑃 (𝛱) ∶ sdop (𝑃) → S
given by the assignment 𝛴 ↦ Map𝑃 (𝛴, 𝛱). (This is the moduli space of sections over 𝛴.)
An equivalence of 𝑃-stratified spaces is carried to an objectwise equivalence of functors;
hence the nerve defines a functor

                              N𝑃 ∶ Str𝑃 → Fun(sdop (𝑃), S) .

Furthermore, the assignment [𝛱 → 𝑃] ↦ (𝑃, N𝑃 (𝛱)) defines a functor

                              N ∶ Str → ∫Pos Fun(sdop , S) .




                                           44
2.7.2 Example. For any poset 𝑃, 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱, and points 𝑝, 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 such that
𝑝 ≤ 𝑞, the space
                       N𝑃 (𝛱){𝑝 ≤ 𝑞} ≃ Map𝑃 ({𝑝 ≤ 𝑞}, 𝛱)
is the link between the 𝑝-th and 𝑞-th strata of 𝛱.
    Let us demonstrate that the functor N lands in the full subcategory

                                 Déc(S) ⊂ ∫Pos Fun(sdop , S) .

2.7.3 Lemma. For any poset 𝑃 and 𝑃-stratified space 𝛱, the functor N𝑃 (𝛱) is a spatial
décollage.
Proof. In Cat∞,/𝑃 , for any chain {𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑛 } ⊆ 𝑃, there is an equivalence

                             {𝑝1 }   {𝑝𝑛−1 }
                  {𝑝0 < 𝑝1 } ∪ ⋯ ∪ {𝑝𝑛−1 < 𝑝𝑛 } ⥲ {𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑛 } ,

which induces an equivalence

 Map𝑃 ({𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑛 }, 𝛱) ⥲ Map𝑃 ({𝑝0 < 𝑝1 }, 𝛱) × ⋯ × Map𝑃 ({𝑝𝑛−1 < 𝑝𝑛 }, 𝛱) ,
                                                       𝛱𝑝1       𝛱𝑝𝑛−1


as desired.

2.7.4 Theorem. The functor N ∶ Str → Déc(S) is an equivalence of ∞-categories over
Pos.
Proof. Let 𝑃 be a poset and write 𝚫/𝑃 for the category of simplices of 𝑃. The Joyal–
Tierney theorem [77] implies that the functor
                                                                    op
                     N ∶ Cat∞,/𝑃 → Fun(𝚫op , S)/ N(𝑃) ≃ Fun(𝚫/𝑃 , S)
                                 𝐶 ↦ [𝛴 ↦ ι Fun/𝑃 (𝛴, 𝐶)]
                                                                                  op
is fully faithful, and the essential image CSS/ N(𝑃) consists of those functors 𝚫/𝑃 → S
that satisfy both the Segal condition and the completeness condition. Now notice that
                                                       op
left Kan extension along the inclusion sdop (𝑃) ↪ 𝚫/𝑃 defines a fully faithful functor
Déc𝑃 (S) ↪ CSS/ N(𝑃) whose essential image consists of those complete Segal spaces
𝐶 → N(𝑃) such that for any 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, the complete Segal space 𝐶𝑝 is an ∞-groupoid.
2.7.5. Write Déc(Sπ ) ⊂ Déc(S) for the full subcategory spanned by those pairs (𝑃, 𝐷)
where 𝑃 is a finite poset and 𝐷 is a spatial décollage over 𝑃 whose values are all π-finite.
Then nerve restricts to an equivalence of ∞-categories N ∶ Strπ ⥲ Déc(Sπ ).

2.8    Profinite spatial décollages
In this section we extend the theory of décollages to proöbjects and explain how to un-
derstand profinite 𝑃-stratified spaces in terms of décollages valued in the ∞-category
of profinite spaces (Construction 2.8.8).



                                               45
2.8.1. We extend the nerve N ∶ Str ⥲ Déc(S) to proöbjects to obtain an equivalence of
∞-categories
                            N ∶ Pro(Str) ⥲ Pro(Déc(S))
over Pro(Pos).
   In order to understand profinite décollages, we recall some basic facts about profinite
spaces and the product in the ∞-category of profinite spaces.
2.8.2 Recollection. We write S∧π ≔ Pro(Sπ ) for the ∞-category of profinite spaces. We
regard the S∧π as a full subcategory of the ∞-category Pro(S). Precomposition with the
inclusion Sπ ↪ S defines a functor (−)∧π ∶ Pro(S) → S∧π that exhibits S∧π as a localization
of Pro(S). Given a prospace 𝑋, we call 𝑋π∧ the profinite completion of 𝑋.
2.8.3 Recollection (monoidal structures on Pro(S) [SAG, Remark E.2.1.2]). In addition
to the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure on Pro(S), there is a related ‘composition’
monoidal structure: since the composition of two left exact accessible functors S → S
is again left exact and accessible, the composition monodial structure on Fun(S, S)op
restricts to a monoidal structure (𝑋, 𝑌) ↦ 𝑋 ∘ 𝑌 on Pro(S). The identity functor is both
the unit for ∘ and terminal object of Pro(S). Hence the universal property of the product
provides is a comparison morphism

                                            𝑐𝑋,𝑌 ∶ 𝑋 ∘ 𝑌 → 𝑋 × 𝑌

that is natural in 𝑋 and 𝑌. However, this morphism is not generally an equivalence.
    Since the subcategory S∧π ⊂ Pro(S) is closed under products, if 𝑋, 𝑌 ∈ S∧π , then the
morphism 𝑋 ∘ 𝑌 → 𝑋 × 𝑌 induces a morphism

(2.8.4)                                       (𝑋 ∘ 𝑌)∧π → 𝑋 × 𝑌 .

We claim that the morphism (2.8.4) is an equivalence. To see this, we show that the
comparison morphism 𝑋 ∘ 𝑌 → 𝑋 × 𝑌 becomes an equivalence after evaluation at any
truncated space.17
2.8.5 Lemma. Let 𝑋 be a profinite space and 𝑌 a prospace. Then for every truncated space
𝐾, the morphism
                          𝑐𝑋,𝑌 ∶ (𝑋 ∘ 𝑌)(𝐾) → (𝑋 × 𝑌)(𝐾)
is an equivalence in Sop .
Proof. Exhibit 𝑋 an inverse system {𝑋𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴 of π-finite spaces and 𝑌 an inverse system
{𝑌𝛽 }𝛽∈𝐵 of spaces. For each 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴, the fact that the space 𝑋𝛼 is π-finite for implies that
the functor corepresented by 𝑋𝛼 preserves colimits of filtered diagrams of uniformly
truncated spaces [SAG, Corollary A.2.3.2]. Since the space 𝐾 is truncated, the filtered
diagram
                                   𝛽 ↦ MapS (𝑌𝛽 , 𝐾)
  17
       We are grateful to Jacob Lurie for this observation.




                                                        46
is uniformly truncated. Hence we have the following equivalences in S:

                   (𝑋 × 𝑌)(𝐾) ≃          colim         MapS (𝑋𝛼 × 𝑌𝛽 , 𝐾)
                                      (𝛼,𝛽)∈𝐴op ×𝐵op

                                  ≃      colim         MapS (𝑋𝛼 , MapS (𝑌𝛽 , 𝐾))
                                      (𝛼,𝛽)∈𝐴op ×𝐵op

                                  ⥲ colim
                                        op
                                           MapS (𝑋𝛼 , colim
                                                          op
                                                             MapS (𝑌𝛽 , 𝐾))
                                       𝛼∈𝐴                    𝛽∈𝐵

                                  ≃ (𝑋 ∘ 𝑌)(𝐾) .

2.8.6 Corollary. Let 𝑋 and 𝑌 be profinite spaces. Then the natural morphism

                                        (𝑋 ∘ 𝑌)∧π → 𝑋 × 𝑌

is an equivalence in S∧π .
2.8.7. Corollary 2.8.6 is helpful for describing fiber products in S∧π as well: given mor-
phisms of profinite spaces 𝑝 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑍 and 𝑞 ∶ 𝑌 → 𝑍, the pullback 𝑋 ×𝑍 𝑌 of 𝑝 along 𝑞
is given by a cobar construction:

                                𝑋 ×𝑍 𝑌 ≃ lim (𝑋 ∘ 𝑍∘𝑚 ∘ 𝑌)∧π .
                                              [𝑚]∈𝚫

    We are now ready to describe Str∧π,𝑃 in terms of profinite décollages.
2.8.8 Construction (profinite décollages). For any finite poset 𝑃, write Déc𝑃 (S∧π ) for the
full subcategory of Fun(sdop (𝑃), S∧π ) spanned by those functors

                                         𝐷 ∶ sdop (𝑃) → S∧π

such that for any chain {𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑛 } ⊆ 𝑃, the natural map

              𝐷{𝑝0 < ⋯ < 𝑝𝑛 } → 𝐷{𝑝0 < 𝑝1 } × ⋯                        ×       𝐷{𝑝𝑛−1 < 𝑝𝑛 }
                                                         𝐷{𝑝1 }     𝐷{𝑝𝑛−1 }


is an equivalence of profinite spaces. We call objects of Déc𝑃 (S∧π ) profinite décollages over
𝑃.
    Combining the equivalence

                         Pro(Fun(sdop (𝑃), Sπ )) ⥲ Fun(sdop (𝑃), S∧π )

furnished by [HTT, Proposition 5.3.5.15] with the equivalences (2.7.5) and (2.8.1), we
obtain equivalences of ∞-categories

(2.8.9)                      Str∧π,𝑃 ⥲ Pro(Déc𝑃 (Sπ )) ⥲ Déc𝑃 (S∧π ) .

Specifically, the composite equivalence (2.8.9) is given by extending the nerve

                             N𝑃 ∶ Strπ,𝑃 ⥲ Déc𝑃 (Sπ ) ⊂ Déc𝑃 (S∧π )

along inverse limits.


                                                  47
Part II
Elements of higher topos theory
In this part we develop the higher-toposic tools that we’ll need to state and prove our
∞-Categorical Hochster Duality Theorem (Theorem 0.4.6=Theorem 9.3.1). In Chap-
ter 3 we recall a number of important results from higher topos theory and develop
the basic frameworks of (bounded) coherent ∞-topoi and (bounded) ∞-pretopoi. The
theories of coherent ∞-topoi and bounded ∞-pretopoi will be used heavily in the re-
mainder of the text. In Chapter 4, we describe the basic theorems of shape theory for
∞-topoi; the generalization of shape theory to stratified ∞-topoi forms the foundation
of our work. Chapter 5 develops the basics of Deligne’s oriented fiber product; this plays
a fundamental role in our approach to stratified higher topos theory in Part III. In Chap-
ter 6 we introduce the analogue of local rings in the context of higher topos theory. In
Chapter 7 we generalize work of Moerdijk–Vermeulen and Illusie–Gabber by proving a
key basechange theorem for oriented fiber products of bounded coherent ∞-topoi. As
with the proof of the proper basechange theorem in algebraic geometry, reduction to
the local case plays a key role in our proof.




                                           48
3     Aide-mémoire on higher topoi
In this chapter we recall a number of important results from higher topos theory (mostly
from Lurie’s [SAG, Appendices A & E]), and develop some basic results that we’ll use
throughout the rest of the text. This chapter is here mostly for ease of reference; much
of the material is expository, and the original material mostly serves to fill small gaps in
the existing foundations.
     Section 3.1 sets our notational conventions for ∞-topoi. Section 3.2 introduces the
first of two finiteness conditions that we impose on almost all of the ∞-topoi we consider
in this text: boundedness. Section 3.3 introduces the second finiteness condition: coher-
ence. Section 3.4 studies the relationship between coherence and 𝑛-topoi. Section 3.5
provides a convenient reformulation of coherence for 𝑛-localic ∞-topoi. Section 3.6
shows that a morphism of finitary ∞-sites induces a coherent geometric morphism on
corresponding ∞-topoi; along with the material from § 3.5, this implies that the the-
ory of 1-localic coherent ∞-topoi recovers Grothendieck’s theory of coherent ordinary
topoi [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, Definition 2.3]. Section 3.7 uses the material from § 3.6 to
provide examples of coherent ∞-topoi and geometric morphisms coming from alge-
braic geometry. Section 3.8 explains how bounded coherent ∞-topoi are classified by
their truncated coherent objects. This perspective will be used extensively throughout
our work. Section 3.9 recalls the fact that inverse limits of bounded coherent ∞-topoi
are again bounded coherent. Section 3.10 shows that the pushforward in a coherent
geometric morphism commutes with filtered colimits of uniformly truncated diagrams.
Section 3.11 discusses points of ∞-topoi and hypercompletness, Deligne Completeness,
and Lurie’s Conceptual Completeness Theorem for bounded coherent ∞-topoi. Sec-
tion 3.12 explains how bases for Grothendieck topologies work for ∞-topoi (which is
more subtle than for ordinary topoi); the key example that we need is that the ∞-topos
of sheaves on a finite poset 𝑃 is the functor ∞-category Fun(𝑃, S).

3.1    Higher topoi
We begin by setting our basic notational conventions for higher topoi.
3.1.1 Notation. We use here the theory of 𝑛-topoi for 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍▹ ; see [HTT, Chapter 6]. We
write Top𝑛 ⊂ Cat∞,δ1 for the subcategory of δ1 -small 𝑛-topoi and geometric morphisms.
Many of the examples in this paper will have 𝑛 ∈ {0, 1, ∞}.
    For any δ0 -small ∞-category 𝐶, we write PSh(𝐶) ≔ Fun(𝐶op , S) for the ∞-topos of
presheaves of spaces on 𝐶.
3.1.2 Example. Recall that 0-topoi are (δ0 -small) locales [HTT, Proposition 6.4.2.5],
and 1-topoi are topoi in the classical sense of Grothendieck [HTT, Remark 6.4.1.3].
3.1.3 Example. Let 𝑚, 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍▹ with 𝑚 ≤ 𝑛. An 𝑚-site is a δ0 -small 𝑚-category 𝑋
equipped with a Grothendieck topology 𝜏. Given an 𝑚-site (𝑋, 𝜏), we write Sh𝜏 (𝑋)≤(𝑛−1)
for the 𝑛-topos of sheaves δ0 -small (𝑛 − 1)-groupoids on 𝑋; when 𝑛 = ∞, we will simply
write Sh𝜏 (𝑋).
    It is not expected that all ∞-topoi are of the form Sh𝜏 (𝑋) for an ∞-site (𝑋, 𝜏); how-
ever, if 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍, then every 𝑛-topos is of the form Sh𝜏 (𝑋)≤(𝑛−1) for some 𝑛-site (𝑋, 𝜏)
[HTT, Theorem 6.4.1.5].


                                            49
                                                     ̃ ≔ Sh(𝑊) for the 0-localic
3.1.4 Example. For any topological space 𝑊, we write 𝑊
∞-topos of sheaves of spaces on 𝑊.
3.1.5 Notation. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍▹ and let 𝑿 and 𝒀 be 𝑛-topoi. We write

                                         Fun∗ (𝑿, 𝒀) ⊆ Fun(𝑿, 𝒀)

for the full subcategory spanned by the geometric morphisms. We note that Fun∗ (𝑿, 𝒀)
is accessible [HTT, Proposition 6.3.1.13]. We write

                                         Fun∗ (𝒀, 𝑿) ⊆ Fun(𝒀, 𝑿)

for the full subcategory spanned by those functors that are left exact left adjoints, so that
Fun∗ (𝒀, 𝑿) ≃ Fun∗ (𝑿, 𝒀)op .
3.1.6 Notation. Recall that the ∞-topos S of spaces is terminal in Top∞ . If 𝑿 is an ∞-
topos, then we write Γ𝑿,∗ or Γ∗ for the unique geometric morphism 𝑿 → S; the functor
Γ𝑿,∗ is corepresented by the terminal object 1𝑿 ∈ 𝑿. The geometric morphism Γ𝑿,∗ is
called the global sections geometric morphism.
3.1.7 Definition. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos. A point of 𝑿 is a geometric morphism

                                                 𝑥∗ ∶ S → 𝑿 ,

which is necessarily a section of Γ∗ . We often write 𝑥̃ for this copy of S, regarded as lying
over 𝑿 via the geometric morphism 𝑥∗ .
3.1.8 Recollection (étale geometric morphisms). Let 𝑿 and 𝒀 be ∞-topoi. A geometric
morphism 𝑝∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 is étale if 𝑝∗ admits a further left adjoint 𝑝! ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 that exhibits
𝑿 as the slice ∞-topos 𝒀/𝑝! (1𝑿 ) . In this case 𝑝! is identified with the forgetful functor
𝒀/𝑝! (1𝑿 ) → 𝒀.
     By [HTT, Corollary 6.3.5.6], the functor

                                        Fun∗ (𝒁, 𝑿) → Fun∗ (𝒁, 𝒀)

is a right fibration whose fiber over a geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒁 → 𝒀 is the (δ0 -small)
∞-groupoid Map𝑿 (1𝑿 , 𝑓∗ 𝑝! (1𝑿 )).
3.1.9. If 𝑿 and 𝒀 are ∞-topoi, the product 𝑿 × 𝒀 in Top∞ is not the product of ∞-cate-
gories; rather, the ∞-topos 𝑿×𝒀 can be identified with the tensor product of presentable
∞-categories.18
    Similarly, if 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒁 and 𝑔∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝒁 are geometric morphisms, then the pull-
back 𝑿 ×𝒁 𝒀 in Top∞ exists [HTT, Proposition 6.3.4.6], but the ∞-topos 𝑿 ×𝒁 𝒀 is not
the pullback of ∞-categories.
    In Chapter 5 we also study an oriented fiber product of ∞-topoi. Again, this ori-
ented fiber product does not coincide with the oriented fiber product of ∞-categories
(0.11.10). We therefore endeavour to indicate clearly when a product, pullback, or ori-
ented fiber product is formed in Top∞ or Cat∞,δ1 .
  18
       For this reason, Lurie writes 𝑿 ⊗ 𝒀 for the product in Top∞ .


                                                       50
   We repeatedly make use of the fact that inverse limits in Top∞ are computed in
Cat∞,δ1 .
3.1.10 Theorem ([HTT, Theorem 6.3.3.1]). The forgetful functor Top∞ → Cat∞,δ1 pre-
serves inverse limits.

3.2    Boundedness
We now turn to the first of two finiteness conditions that we impose on almost all of the
∞-topoi we consider in this book.
3.2.1 Notation. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. For each integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, write
𝐶≤𝑛 ⊂ 𝐶 for the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑛-truncated objects, and
                                         τ≤𝑛 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝐶≤𝑛
for the 𝑛-truncation functor, which is left adjoint to the inclusion 𝐶≤𝑛 ⊂ 𝐶 [HTT, Propo-
sition 5.5.6.18]. Write 𝐶<∞ ⊂ 𝐶 for the full subcategory spanned by those objects which
are 𝑛-truncated for some integer 𝑛 ≥ −2.
3.2.2 Notation. If 𝑚, 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍▹ with 𝑚 < 𝑛, then passage to (𝑚 − 1)-truncated objects
defines a functor
                             (−)≤𝑚−1 ∶ Top𝑛 → Top𝑚 .
We call a (−1)-truncated object of an 𝑛-topos 𝑿 an open in 𝑿 and write Open(𝑿) ≔ 𝑿≤−1 .
For any open 𝑈 of 𝑿, the étale geometric morphism 𝑿/𝑈 ↪ 𝑿 then exhibits 𝑿/𝑈 as an
open subtopos of 𝑿.
3.2.3 Definition. If 𝑚, 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍▹ with 𝑚 < 𝑛, then the functor (−)≤𝑚−1 ∶ Top𝑛 → Top𝑚
admits a fully faithful right adjoint. Write Top𝑚
                                                𝑛 ⊆ Top𝑛 for the essential image of this
functor; this consists of those 𝑛-topoi 𝑿 such that, for every 𝑛-topos 𝒀, the functor
                               Fun∗ (𝒀, 𝑿) → Fun∗ (𝒀≤𝑚−1 , 𝑿≤𝑚−1 )
is an equivalence. We call such 𝑛-topoi 𝑚-localic [HTT, §6.4.5].
     The inclusion Top𝑛∞ ⊂ Top∞ of the full subcategory of 𝑛-localic ∞-topoi admits a
left adjoint
                                  L𝑛 ∶ Top∞ → Top𝑛∞
called 𝑛-localic reflection.
3.2.4. If 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍, then the proof of [HTT, Proposition 6.4.5.9] demonstrates that an ∞-
topos 𝑿 is 𝑛-localic if and only if 𝑿 ≃ Sh𝜏 (𝑋), where (𝑋, 𝜏) is a δ0 -small 𝑛-site with all
finite limits.
                                                             ̃ of sheaves on 𝑊 is
3.2.5 Example. If 𝑊 is a topological space, then the ∞-topos 𝑊
0-localic.
3.2.6 Warning. If (𝑋, 𝜏) is an 𝑛-site and the 𝑛-category 𝑋 does not have finite limits, then
the ∞-topos Sh𝜏 (𝑋) may not be 𝑁-localic for any 𝑁 ≥ 0. See [SAG, Counterexample
20.4.0.1] for a basis 𝐵 for the topology on the Hilbert cube ∏𝑖∈𝐙 [0, 1] for which the
∞-topos of sheaves on 𝐵 is not 𝑁-localic for any 𝑁 ≥ 0.
