3.2.7 Example. If 𝑋 is a scheme, then the ∞-topos 𝑋ét of étale sheaves on the 1-site of
étale 𝑋-schemes is 1-localic.
3.2.8 Example. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍 and let 𝑿 be an 𝑛-localic ∞-topos. Then [SAG, Lemma
1.4.7.7] demonstrates that for an object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿, the over ∞-topos 𝑿/𝑈 is 𝑛-localic if and
only if 𝑈 is 𝑛-truncated.

3.2.9 Definition. We write Top∧<∞ the inverse limit of ∞-categories

                                    Top∧<∞ ≔ limop Top𝑛
                                               𝑛∈𝐍

along the various truncation functors (−)≤𝑚−1 . This is the ∞-category of sequences
{𝑿𝑛 }𝑛∈𝐍 in which each 𝑿𝑛 is an 𝑛-topos, along with identifications (𝑿𝑛 )≤𝑚−1 ⥲ 𝑿𝑚
whenever 𝑚 ≤ 𝑛. The truncation functors provide a functor

                                    τ ∶ Top∞ → Top∧<∞ ,

which carries an ∞-topos 𝑿 to the sequence {𝑿≤𝑛−1 }𝑛∈𝐍 .
3.2.10 Construction. The functor τ ∶ Top∞ → Top∧<∞ admits a fully faithful right ad-
joint, which identifies Top∧<∞ with the full subcategory of Top∞ spanned by the bounded
∞-topoi [SAG, Proposition A.7.1.5]. These are the ∞-topoi that can be exhibited as in-
verse limits in Top∞ of a diagram of localic ∞-topoi. Equivalently, an ∞-topos 𝑿 is
bounded if and only if the natural geometric morphism

                                      𝑿 → limop L𝑛 (𝑿)
                                            𝑛∈𝐍

is an equivalence.
    Surprisingly, the functor the functor τ ∶ Top∞ → Top∧<∞ also admits a left adjoint;
to state what the essential image of this left adjoint is, we first recall a bit about Postnikov
completeness.
3.2.11 Definition. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. We say that:
(3.2.11.1) Postnikov towers converge in 𝐶, if for every object 𝑋 ∈ 𝐶, the natural mor-
           phism 𝑋 → lim𝑛∈𝐍op τ≤𝑛 𝑋 is an equivalence in 𝐶.

(3.2.11.2) The ∞-category 𝐶 is Postnikov complete if the natural functor

                                                     τ≤𝑛                 τ≤0
                      𝐶 → lim ( ⋯            𝐶≤𝑛+1         𝐶≤𝑛      ⋯          𝐶≤0 ) ,

           is an equivalence of ∞-categories. (Here the limit is formed in Cat∞,δ1 .)

3.2.12. Note that Postnikov completeness implies the convergence of Postnikov towers,
but not conversely.




                                              52
3.2.13 Construction. The functor τ ∶ Top∞ → Top∧<∞ also admits a left adjoint, which
is necessarily fully faithful. This identifies the ∞-category Top∧<∞ with the full subcate-
gory of Top∞ spanned by the Postnikov complete ∞-topoi [SAG, Corollary A.7.2.8].
    We write (−)post for the right adjoint to the inclusion of the full subcategory of Top∞
spanned by the Postnikov complete ∞-topoi, and write (−)b for the left adjoint to the
inclusion of the full subcategory of Top∞ spanned by the bounded ∞-topoi, so that

                     𝑿post ≔ limop 𝑿≤𝑛       and      𝑿b ≔ limop L𝑛 (𝑿) .
                              𝑛∈𝐍                           𝑛∈𝐍

For an ∞-topos 𝑿, we call 𝑿post the Postnikov completion of 𝑿 and call 𝑿b the bounded
reflection of 𝑿.
3.2.14. The relationship between bounded ∞-topoi and Postnikov complete ∞-topoi
is formally analogous to the relationship between 𝑝-nilpotent and 𝑝-complete abelian
groups. Of course 𝑝-nilpotent and 𝑝-complete abelian groups form equivalent categories,
but their embeddings into the category of all abelian groups differ.

3.3     Coherence
The second finiteness condition that we impose on almost all of the ∞-topoi we con-
sider is coherence. Coherence is the topos-theoretic analogue of being quasicompact
and quasiseparated in the world of schemes. Indeed, the étale ∞-topos of a scheme 𝑋
is coherent if and only if 𝑋 is quasicompact and quasiseparated (see Proposition 3.7.3).
3.3.1 Definition (coherence). Let 0 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ ∞, and let 𝑿 be an 𝑟-topos. We say that 𝑿
is 0-coherent if and only if the 0-topos (=locale) Open(𝑿) is quasicompact. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍∗ ,
and define 𝑛-coherence of 𝑟-topoi and their objects recursively as follows.
      → An object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent if and only if the 𝑟-topos 𝑿/𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent.
      → The 𝑟-topos 𝑿 is locally 𝑛-coherent if and only if every object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 admits a cover
        {𝑉𝑖 → 𝑈}𝑖∈𝐼 in which each 𝑉𝑖 is 𝑛-coherent.
      → The 𝑟-topos 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent if and only if 𝑿 is locally 𝑛-coherent, and the
        𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿 are closed under finite products.
   An 𝑟-topos 𝑿 is coherent if and only if 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent for every 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍, and an object
𝑈 of an 𝑟-topos 𝑿 is coherent if and only if 𝑿/𝑈 is a coherent 𝑟-topos. Finally, an 𝑟-topos
𝑿 is locally coherent if and only if every object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 admits a cover {𝑉𝑖 → 𝑈}𝑖∈𝐼 in
which each 𝑉𝑖 is coherent.
3.3.2. In particular, if 𝑿 is locally 𝑛-coherent, then 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent if and only
if 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent and for any pair 𝑈′ , 𝑉 ∈ 𝑿/𝑈 of 𝑛-coherent objects, the fiber product
𝑈′ ×𝑈 𝑉 is 𝑛-coherent.

3.3.3. We are mostly interested in coherence for ∞-topoi, however we have introduced
the notion for 𝑟-topoi in general because an ∞-topos 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent if and only if its
underlying 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is 𝑛-coherent (this is the content of §3.4).


                                             53
3.3.4 Notation. Let 𝑟 ∈ 𝐍▹ , and let 𝑿 be an 𝑟-topos. Write 𝑿coh ⊂ 𝑿 for the full subcat-
                                                   coh
egory of 𝑿 spanned by the coherent objects and 𝑿<∞     ⊂ 𝑿 for the full subcategory of 𝑿
spanned by the truncated coherent objects. For each integer 𝑛 ≥ 0, write 𝑿𝑛-coh ⊂ 𝑿 for
the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑛-coherent objects.
3.3.5 Example. A space 𝐾 ∈ S is truncated coherent if and only if 𝐾 is π-finite. That is
to say,
                                    Scoh
                                      <∞ = Sπ .

3.3.6 Example. By [SAG, Proposition A.7.5.1], if 𝑿 is a bounded coherent ∞-topos,
then 𝑿 is also locally coherent.
3.3.7. Let 0 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ ∞, let 𝑿 be an 𝑟-topos, and let 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿. Then for any integer 𝑛 ≥ 0, an
object 𝑈′ → 𝑈 of 𝑿/𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent if and only if 𝑈′ is 𝑛-coherent when viewed as an
object of 𝑿. Thus we have canonical identifications

               (𝑿𝑛-coh )/𝑈 = (𝑿/𝑈 )𝑛-coh     and       (𝑿coh )/𝑈 = (𝑿/𝑈 )coh

as full subcategories of 𝑿/𝑈 . If 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿<∞ is a truncated object, then we have a canonical
identification
                                      coh             coh
                                    (𝑿<∞  )/𝑈 = (𝑿/𝑈 )<∞
as full subcategories of 𝑿/𝑈 .
3.3.8 Definition. Let 𝑿 and 𝒀 be ∞-topoi. We say that a geometric morphism

                                       𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀

is coherent if and only if, for any coherent object 𝐹 ∈ 𝒀coh , the object 𝑓∗ (𝐹) ∈ 𝑿 is co-
herent as well. We write Topcoh∞ for the subcategory of Top∞ whose objects are coherent
∞-topoi and whose morphisms are coherent geometric morphisms.
    We defer examples of coherent ∞-topoi to § 3.7; we do this in order to put all of
our examples from algebraic geometry on the same footing after developing the basic
calculus of finitary sites in this section and in §3.6.
3.3.9 Definition. An ∞-site (𝑋, 𝜏) is finitary if and only if 𝑋 admits all fiber products,
and, for every object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑋 and every covering sieve 𝑆 ⊂ 𝑋/𝑈 , there is a finite subset
{𝑈𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 ⊂ 𝑆 that generates a covering sieve.
     Let (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 ) and (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) be finitary ∞-sites. A morphism of ∞-sites

                                  𝑓∗ ∶ (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) → (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 )

is a morphism of finitary ∞-sites if 𝑓∗ is preserves fiber products.
3.3.10 Proposition ([SAG, Proposition A.3.1.3]). Let (𝑋, 𝜏) be a finitary ∞-site and
write よ𝜏 ∶ 𝑋 → Sh𝜏 (𝑋) for the sheafified Yoneda embedding. Then:
(3.3.10.1) The ∞-topos Sh𝜏 (𝑋) locally coherent.
(3.3.10.2) For every object 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋, the sheaf よ𝜏 (𝑥) is a coherent object of Sh𝜏 (𝑋).


                                            54
(3.3.10.3) If, in addition, 𝑋 admits a terminal object, then Sh𝜏 (𝑋) is coherent.
   An elementary way to construct a finitary ∞-site is to make use of an ∞-categorical
analogue of the notion of pretopology on a 1-category.
3.3.11 Definition. An ∞-presite is a pair (𝑋, 𝐸) consisting of an ∞-category 𝑋 along
with a subcategory 𝐸 ⊆ 𝑋 satisfying the following conditions.
       → The subcategory 𝐸 contains all equivalences of 𝑋.
       → The ∞-category 𝑋 admits finite limits, and 𝐸 is stable under base change.
       → The ∞-category 𝑋 admits finite coproducts, finite coproducts are universal in 𝑋,
         and 𝐸 is closed under finite coproducts.
3.3.12 Construction ([SAG, Proposition A.3.2.1]). Let (𝑋, 𝐸) be an ∞-presite. Then
there exists a topology 𝜏𝐸 in which the 𝜏𝐸 -covering sieves are generated by finite families
{𝑦𝑖 → 𝑥}𝑖∈𝐼 such that ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑦𝑖 → 𝑥 lies in 𝐸. The ∞-site (𝑋, 𝜏𝐸 ) is finitary.

3.4        Coherence & 𝑛-topoi
In this section we prove that the property that an ∞-topos 𝑿 be 𝑛-coherent only depends
on its underlying 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 of (𝑛 − 1)-truncated objects (Corollary 3.4.10).19 We
begin with some preliminaries on the relationship between coherence and connectivity.
3.4.1 Proposition ([SAG, Proposition A.2.4.1]). Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos, let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑈 → 𝑉 be
a morphism in 𝑿, and let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. Then:
(3.4.1.1) If 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent and 𝑓 is 𝑛-connective, then 𝑉 is 𝑛-coherent.

(3.4.1.2) If 𝑉 is 𝑛-coherent and 𝑓 is (𝑛 + 1)-connective, then 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent.
Since the natural morphism from an object in an ∞-topos to its 𝑛-truncation is (𝑛 + 1)-
connective, we deduce:
3.4.2 Corollary. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. An object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent if and
only if τ≤𝑛−1 (𝑈) is an 𝑛-coherent object of 𝑿.
It is also easy to deduce the following.
3.4.3 Corollary ([SAG, Corollary A.2.4.4]). Let 𝑿 be a coherent ∞-topos and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍.
Then for any 𝑛-coherent object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿, the (𝑛 − 1)-truncation τ≤𝑛−1 (𝑈) of 𝑈 is a coherent
object of 𝑿.

3.4.4 Corollary. Let 𝑿 be a coherent ∞-topos. Then an object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is coherent if and
only if for every 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍, the (𝑛 − 1)-truncation τ≤𝑛−1 (𝑈) of 𝑋 is a coherent object of 𝑿.
3.4.5 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism between coherent ∞-topoi.
Then 𝑓∗ is coherent if and only if 𝑓∗ carries 𝒀<∞
                                               coh     coh
                                                   to 𝑿<∞  .
  19
       We are grateful to Jacob Lurie for conveying this observation.



                                                       55
    We also deduce that coherence of a geometric morphism between coherent ∞-topoi
is equivalent to the a priori stronger condition that the pullback functor preserve 𝑛-
coherent objects for all 𝑛 ≥ 0:20
3.4.6 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism between coherent ∞-topoi.
Then 𝑓∗ is coherent if and only if 𝑓∗ carries 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝒀 to 𝑛-coherent objects
of 𝑿 for all 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍.
Proof. It is immediate from the definition that if 𝑓∗ preserves 𝑛-coherence for all 𝑛 ≥ 0,
then 𝑓∗ is coherent. Conversely, assume that 𝑓∗ is coherent, and let 𝑈 ∈ 𝒀 be an 𝑛-
coherent object. Since 𝒀 is coherent, Corollary 3.4.3=[SAG, Corollary A.2.4.4] shows
      𝒀
that τ≤𝑛−1 (𝑈) is an 𝑛-coherent object of 𝒀. Since 𝑓∗ is coherent, we see that

                                  𝑓∗ τ≤𝑛−1
                                      𝒀           𝑿
                                           (𝑈) ≃ τ≤𝑛−1 (𝑓∗ (𝑈))

is a coherent object of 𝑿. Corollary 3.4.2 then shows that 𝑓∗ (𝑈) is an 𝑛-coherent object
of 𝑿.
   Before showing that 𝑛-coherence only depends on the underlying 𝑛-topos, we need
two preliminary facts on 𝑚-connective morphisms in an ∞-topos.
3.4.7 Lemma. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑚 ≥ 0 an integer. Let 𝑊 ∈ 𝑿 and let 𝑢 ∶ 𝑈′ → 𝑈
and 𝑣 ∶ 𝑉′ → 𝑉 be morphisms in 𝑿/𝑊 . If 𝑢 and 𝑣 are 𝑚-connective morphisms of 𝑿, then
the induced morphism 𝑢 ×𝑊 𝑣 ∶ 𝑈′ ×𝑊 𝑉′ → 𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉 is 𝑚-connective.
Proof. First we treat the case where 𝑊 = 1𝑿 is the terminal object of 𝑿. In this case,
since τ≤𝑚−1 ∶ 𝑿 → 𝑿 preserves finite products [HTT, Lemma 6.5.1.2] and τ≤𝑚−1 (𝑢) and
τ≤𝑚−1 (𝑣) are equivalences by assumption, we see that

                              τ≤𝑚−1 (𝑢 × 𝑣) ≃ τ≤𝑚−1 (𝑢) × τ≤𝑚−1 (𝑣)

is an equivalence.
    Now we treat the general case. In the diagram
                                      𝑢×𝑊 𝑣
                     𝑈′ ×𝑊 𝑉′                  𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉                     𝑊
                           ⌟                        ⌟
                                                                             Δ𝑊


                      𝑈′ × 𝑉′         𝑢×𝑣        𝑈×𝑉                   𝑊×𝑊

both squares are pullbacks and 𝑢 × 𝑣 is 𝑚-connective (by the preceding paragraph). This
completes the proof since the class of 𝑚-connective morphisms in an ∞-topos is stable
under pullback [HTT, Proposition 6.5.1.16].

   The following is a useful strenthening of the fact that 𝑛-truncation commutes with
basechange along a morphism between 𝑛-truncated objects [41, Lemma 1.8]:
  20
     This second notion is how Grothendieck and Verdier originally defined coherence for geometric mor-
phisms between ordinary topoi [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, Définition 3.1].



                                                 56
3.4.8 Lemma. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. Let 𝑊 ∈ 𝑿 and let 𝑈 → 𝑊 and 𝑉 → 𝑊
be morphisms in 𝑿. If 𝑊 is 𝑛-truncated, then the natural morphism

                           τ≤𝑛 (𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉) → τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉)

is an equivalence.
Proof. Since the natural morphisms 𝑈 ↠ τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) and 𝑉 ↠ τ≤𝑛 (𝑉) are (𝑛+1)-connective,
by Lemma 3.4.7 the natural morphism

                            𝜙 ∶ 𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉 → τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉)

is (𝑛 + 1)-connective. Since 𝑊 is 𝑛-truncated and the 𝑛-truncated objects of an ∞-topos
are closed under limits, the object τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉) is 𝑛-truncated. By the uniqueness
of the factorization of a morphism in an ∞-topos into an (𝑛 + 1)-connective morphism
followed by an 𝑛-truncated morphism, we see that 𝜙 exhibits τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉) as the
𝑛-truncation of 𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉.
3.4.9 Proposition. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The following are equivalent for an
(𝑛 − 1)-truncated object 𝑊 ∈ 𝑿≤𝑛−1 :
(3.4.9.1) As an object of the ∞-topos 𝑿, the object 𝑊 is 𝑛-coherent.
(3.4.9.2) As an object of the 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 , the object 𝑊 is 𝑛-coherent.
Proof. Clearly (3.4.9.1) implies (3.4.9.2). We prove that (3.4.9.2) implies (3.4.9.1) by in-
duction on 𝑛. The base case 𝑛 = 0 is immediate from the definition of 0-coherence.
    For the induction step assume we have shown that an (𝑛−1)-truncated object of 𝑿 is
𝑛-coherent if it is 𝑛-coherent as an object of the 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 . Let 𝑊 be an 𝑛-truncated
object of 𝑿 that is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent as an object of the (𝑛 + 1)-topos 𝑿≤𝑛 ; we prove that
𝑊 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent as an object of the ∞-topos 𝑿. First we show that 𝑿/𝑊 is locally
𝑛-coherent. Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑈 → 𝑊 be a morphism in 𝑿. Since 𝑊 is 𝑛-truncated, 𝑓 factors as a
composite
                                    𝑈 ↠ τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) → 𝑊 .
Since 𝑿≤𝑛,/𝑊 is locally 𝑛-coherent by the inductive hypothesis, there exists a cover

                                    {𝑈𝑖 → τ≤𝑛 (𝑈)}𝑖∈𝐼

of τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) such that for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼, the object 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝑿≤𝑛,/𝑊 is an 𝑛-coherent object of
𝑿≤𝑛,/𝑊 . Equivalently, each 𝑈𝑖 is an 𝑛-coherent object of 𝑿≤𝑛 (3.3.7). Since the morphism
𝑈 ↠ τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) is (𝑛+1)-connective, Proposition 3.4.1=[SAG, Proposition A.2.4.1] and the
fact that (𝑛 + 1)-connective morphisms in an ∞-topos are stable under pullback [HTT,
Proposition 6.5.1.16] show that the family

                                  {𝑈𝑖 ×τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) 𝑈 → 𝑈}𝑖∈𝐼

is a cover of 𝑈 in 𝑿/𝑊 by 𝑛-coherent objects. That is, 𝑿/𝑊 is locally 𝑛-coherent.
     Now let us show that the 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿/𝑊 are stable under finite products.
Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑈 → 𝑊 and 𝑔 ∶ 𝑉 → 𝑊 be morphisms in 𝑿/𝑊 , where 𝑈 and 𝑉 are 𝑛-coherent.


                                            57
Then since the 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿≤𝑛,/𝑊 are stable under finite products by the in-
ductive hypothesis, we see that τ≤𝑛 (𝑈)×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉) is an 𝑛-coherent object of 𝑿≤𝑛,𝑊 . By the
inductive hypothesis and Corollary 3.4.2, the object τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉) is an 𝑛-coherent
object of 𝑿/𝑊 . The claim now follows from the fact that the natural morphism
                              𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉 → τ≤𝑛 (𝑈) ×𝑊 τ≤𝑛 (𝑉)
is (𝑛 + 1)-connective (Lemma 3.4.8) and Proposition 3.4.1=[SAG, Proposition A.2.4.1].


Setting 𝑊 = 1𝑿 in Proposition 3.4.9 we deduce:
3.4.10 Corollary. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The following are equivalent for an ∞-topos 𝑿:
(3.4.10.1) The ∞-topos 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
(3.4.10.2) The 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is 𝑛-coherent.
    For the next few results, please recall the notations of Construction 3.2.10.
3.4.11 Corollary. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍 and let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi.
If 𝑓∗ induces an equivalence 𝑿≤𝑛−1 ⥲ 𝒀≤𝑛−1 , then 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent if and only if 𝒀 is
𝑛-coherent. Equivalently, if 𝑓∗ induces an equivalence L𝑛 (𝑿) ⥲ L𝑛 (𝒀) on 𝑛-localic reflec-
tions, then 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent if and only if 𝒀 is 𝑛-coherent.
Corollary 3.4.11 shows that there are many different ways to check the 𝑛-coherence of
an ∞-topos.
3.4.12 Lemma. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The following are equivalent for an ∞-topos 𝑿:
(3.4.12.1) The ∞-topos 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
(3.4.12.2) The 𝑛-localic reflection L𝑛 (𝑿) of 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
(3.4.12.3) The hypercompletion 𝑿hyp of 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent (see Definition 3.11.5).
(3.4.12.4) The Postnikov completion 𝑿post of 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
(3.4.12.5) The bounded reflection 𝑿b of 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
Proof. The equivalence of these statements follows from repeated application of Corol-
lary 3.4.11. The equivalence of (3.4.12.1) and (3.4.12.2) follows immediately from Corol-
lary 3.4.11.
    To see that (3.4.12.1)⇔(3.4.12.3), note that since truncated objects are hypercom-
plete, the natural fully faithful geometric morphism 𝑿hyp ↪ 𝑿 induces an equivalence
on (𝑛 − 1)-truncated objects.
    To see that (3.4.12.1)⇔(3.4.12.4), note that by [SAG, Proposition A.7.3.7] the natural
geometric morphism 𝑿post → 𝑿 is an equivalence when restricted to (𝑛 − 1)-truncated
objects.
    To see that (3.4.12.1)⇔(3.4.12.5), note that since the 𝑛-localic reflection functor
L𝑛 ∶ Top∞ → Top∞ preserves inverse limits [SAG, Lemma A.7.1.4], the natural geo-
metric morphism
                                   𝑿 → 𝑿b ≃ limop L𝑘 (𝑿)
                                              𝑘∈𝐍
induces an equivalence on 𝑛-localic reflections.


                                            58
3.4.13 (equivalent conditions for coherence of geometric morphisms). Let
                                            𝑥∗
                                     𝑿′          𝑿
                                    𝑓∗′            𝑓∗

                                      𝒀′    𝑦∗   𝒀

be a commutative square of coherent ∞-topoi in Top∞ . Lemma 3.4.12 and Corollary 3.4.5
show that if 𝑥∗ and 𝑦∗ induce equivalences
                         ′                               ′
                        𝑿<∞ ⥲ 𝑿<∞          and          𝒀<∞ ⥲ 𝒀<∞

on truncated objects, then 𝑓∗ is coherent if and only if 𝑓∗′ is coherent.
    In particular, the following are equivalent for a geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀
between coherent ∞-topoi:

(3.4.13.1) The geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 is coherent.
                                                 hyp
(3.4.13.2) The induced geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿hyp → 𝒀hyp on hypercompletions
           is coherent.
                                                 post
(3.4.13.3) The induced geometric morphism 𝑓∗            ∶ 𝑿post → 𝒀post on Postnikov com-
           pletions is coherent.
(3.4.13.4) The induced geometric morphism 𝑓∗b ∶ 𝑿b → 𝒀b on bounded refelections is
           coherent.
   Thus the equivalence between Postnikov complete ∞-topoi and bounded ∞-topoi
(Construction 3.2.10) restricts to an equivalence between the subcategory of Postnikov
complete coherent ∞-topoi and coherent geometric morphisms and the subcategory of
bounded coherent ∞-topoi and coherent geometric morphisms.

3.5    Coherence of morphisms & 𝑛-localic ∞-topoi
In this section we prove that coherence for an 𝑛-localic ∞-topos is equivalent to (𝑛 + 1)-
coherence, and may be checked on its underling 𝑛-topos (Proposition 3.5.6). First we’ll
need ∞-toposic versions of a number of points from [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, §§1–3]; these
follow easily from [SAG, §A.2.1].

3.5.1 Definition. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍 and let 𝑿 be a locally 𝑛-coherent ∞-topos. A morphism
𝑈 → 𝑉 in 𝑿 is called relatively 𝑛-coherent if for every 𝑛-coherent object 𝑉′ ∈ 𝑿 and
every morphism 𝑉′ → 𝑉, the fiber product 𝑈 ×𝑉 𝑉′ is also 𝑛-coherent.
3.5.2 Example ([SAG, Example A.2.1.2]). Let 𝑿 be a locally 𝑛-coherent ∞-topos and
𝑓 ∶ 𝑈 → 𝑉 a morphism in 𝑿. If 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent and 𝑉 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent, then 𝑓 is
relatively 𝑛-coherent.




                                           59
3.5.3 Example. As a consequence of Proposition 3.4.1=[SAG, Proposition A.2.4.1] and
the fact that the class of (𝑛 + 1)-connective morphisms in an ∞-topos is stable under
pullback [HTT, Proposition 6.5.1.16], the (𝑛 + 1)-connective morphism of an ∞-topos
are ‘relatively 𝑛-coherent’ in a very strong sense: they satisfy the condition of relative
𝑛-coherence without the need of local 𝑛-coherence assumptions on the ∞-topos.
3.5.4 Lemma. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍 and let 𝑿 be a locally 𝑛-coherent ∞-topos. Let 𝑢 ∶ 𝑈′ → 𝑈 and
𝑣 ∶ 𝑉′ → 𝑉 be relatively 𝑛-coherent morphisms in 𝑿, 𝑊 ∈ 𝑿 an object, and 𝑈 → 𝑊
and 𝑉 → 𝑊 be any morphisms. Then the induced morphism 𝑈′ ×𝑊 𝑉′ → 𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉 is
relatively 𝑛-coherent.
Proof. Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑈 ×𝑊 𝑉 be a morphism in 𝑿 where 𝑋 is 𝑛-coherent. Note that we
have equivalences of iterated fiber products

                    𝑋 × (𝑈′ ×𝑊 𝑉′ ) ≃ (𝑋 ×𝑈 𝑈′ ) ×𝑋 (𝑋 ×𝑉 𝑉′ )
                       𝑈×𝑊 𝑉

                                         ≃ (𝑋 ×𝑈 𝑈′ ) ×𝑉 𝑉′ .

First, since 𝑋 ×𝑈 𝑈′ is the pullback of pr1 ∘𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑈 along the relatively 𝑛-coherent
morphism 𝑢, the object 𝑋×𝑈 𝑈′ is 𝑛-coherent. Second, (𝑋×𝑈 𝑈′ )×𝑉 𝑉′ is the pullback of
the morphism 𝑋 ×𝑈 𝑈′ → 𝑉 induced by pr2 ∘𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑉 along the relatively 𝑛-coherent
morphism 𝑣. Hence (𝑋 ×𝑈 𝑈′ ) ×𝑉 𝑉′ is an 𝑛-coherent object of 𝑿, as desired.
3.5.5 Lemma. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑚 ∈ 𝐍. Let 𝑿0 ⊂ 𝑿 be a full subcategory
satisfying the following conditions:
(3.5.5.1) The full subcategory 𝑿0 ⊂ 𝑿 is closed under finite products.
(3.5.5.2) Every object of 𝑿0 is 𝑚-coherent.
(3.5.5.3) For every object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿, there exists an effective epimorphism ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈
          where 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝑿0 for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼.
Then the 𝑚-coherent objects of 𝑿 are closed under finite products.
Proof. Let 𝑿0′ ⊂ 𝑿 denote the closure of 𝑿0 under finite coproducts; then every object
of 𝑿0′ is 𝑚-coherent. Since colimits in 𝑿 are universal and 𝑿0 is closed under finite
products, 𝑿0′ ⊂ 𝑿 is closed under finite products.
    Let 𝑈, 𝑉 ∈ 𝑿 be 𝑚-coherent objects; we show that 𝑈 × 𝑉 is 𝑚-coherent. Since 𝑈 and
𝑉 are quasicompact, there exist effective epimorphisms 𝑢 ∶ 𝑈′ ↠ 𝑈 and 𝑣 ∶ 𝑉′ ↠ 𝑉
where 𝑈′ , 𝑉′ ∈ 𝑿0′ . By Example 3.5.2=[SAG, Example A.2.1.2] both 𝑢 and 𝑣 are relatively
(𝑚 − 1)-coherent. Lemma 3.5.4 shows that

                               𝑢 × 𝑣 ∶ 𝑈′ × 𝑉′ ↠ 𝑈 × 𝑉

is a relatively (𝑚 − 1)-coherent effective epimorphism. Since 𝑈′ × 𝑉′ ∈ 𝑿0′ is 𝑚-coherent
and 𝑿 is locally 𝑚-coherent, [SAG, Proposition A.2.1.3] shows that 𝑈×𝑉 is 𝑚-coherent,
as desired.
3.5.6 Proposition. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The following are equivalent for an 𝑛-localic ∞-topos 𝑿:


                                              60
(3.5.6.1) The 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent.
(3.5.6.2) The ∞-topos 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent.
(3.5.6.3) The ∞-topos 𝑿 is coherent.
(3.5.6.4) The 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is coherent.
Proof. Clearly (3.5.6.3)⇒(3.5.6.4) and (3.5.6.4)⇒(3.5.6.1).
     First we show that (3.5.6.1)⇒(3.5.6.2). Corollary 3.4.10 shows that 𝑿 is 𝑛-coherent.
First notice that every object of 𝑿 admits a cover by (𝑛−1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent objects
(so, in particular, 𝑿 is locally 𝑛-coherent). This follows from the following observations:
      → Since ∞-topos 𝑿 is 𝑛-localic, every object of 𝑿 admits a cover by (𝑛−1)-truncated
        objects.
      → Since the 𝑛-topos 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is locally 𝑛-coherent, Proposition 3.4.9 shows that every
        (𝑛 − 1)-truncated object of 𝑿 admits a cover by (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent ob-
        jects.
Moreover, since the (𝑛 − 1)-truncated objects of an ∞-topos are closed under limits
and 𝑿≤𝑛−1 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent, Proposition 3.4.9 shows that the (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-
coherent objects of 𝑿 are closed under finite products. Lemma 3.5.5 applied to the full
subcategory 𝑿0 ⊂ 𝑿 spanned by the (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent objects (so that 𝑚 = 𝑛
in the notation of Lemma 3.5.5) now shows that the 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿 are closed
under finite products.
    Since an 𝑛-localic ∞-topos is 𝑁-localic for all 𝑁 ≥ 𝑛, to prove the implication
(3.5.6.2)⇒(3.5.6.3), it suffices to prove that if 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent, then 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 2)-
coherent. First we show that 𝑿 is locally (𝑛 + 1)-coherent. We have already seen that
every object of 𝑿 admits a cover by a (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent objects, and that
the subcategory 𝑿0 of (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent objects is closed under finite prod-
ucts. Since 𝑿 is (𝑛 + 1)-coherent, [SAG, Corollary A.2.4.3] shows that (𝑛 − 1)-truncated
𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿 are automatically (𝑛 + 1)-coherent, immediately implying that
𝑿 is locally (𝑛 + 1)-coherent. Lemma 3.5.5 applied to the subcategory 𝑿0 of (𝑛 − 1)-
truncated (𝑛 + 1)-coherent objects (so that 𝑚 = 𝑛 + 1 in the notation of Lemma 3.5.5)
shows that the (𝑛 + 1)-coherent objects of 𝑿 are closed under finite products.

3.6     Coherent geometric morphisms via sites & coherent ordinary
        topoi
In this section we explain the relationship between coherent ordinary topoi and their
corresponding 1-localic ∞-topoi.21 (See [85; 86, Appendix C, §§5–6] for an excellent
accounts of coherent ordinary topoi.) We show that the ∞-category of coherent 1-localic
∞-topoi is equivalent to the 2-category of coherent ordinary topoi. In fact, the results of
§3.4 allow us to show that the ∞-category of coherent 𝑛-localic ∞-topoi is equivalent
to the (𝑛 + 1)-category of coherent 𝑛-topoi (Proposition 3.6.11).
  21
     The contents of this section originally appeared in a (partially expository) preprint of the third-named
author [50].


                                                    61
3.6.1 Recollection. A 1-topos 𝑿 is coherent in the sense of [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, Defini-
tion 2.3] if and only if 𝑿 is 2-coherent in the sense of Definition 3.3.1. This is true if and
only if 𝑿 is equivalent to the 1-topos of sheaves of sets on a finitary 1-site (𝑋, 𝜏) with a
terminal object. Proposition 3.5.6 shows that 𝑿 is coherent if and only if its correspond-
ing 1-localic ∞-topos is coherent.
      A geometric morphism morphism of coherent 1-topoi 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 is coherent [SGA
4ii , Exposé VI, Definition 3.1] if and only if 𝑓∗ is induced by a morphism of finitary 1-
sites 𝑓∗ ∶ (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) → (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 ).
    The content of the equivalence between coherent 𝑛-topoi and coherent 𝑛-localic ∞-
topoi reduces to showing that a coherent morphism of coherent 𝑛-topoi induces a co-
herent morphism of corresponding 𝑛-localic ∞-topoi. This follows from the fact that
coherence of a geometric morphism between locally coherent ∞-topoi can be checked
on a generating set of coherent objects (Corollary 3.6.6). A particularly useful conse-
quence is that morphisms of finitary ∞-sites induce coherent geometric morphisms
(Corollary 3.6.8).
    First we need a few preliminary results. For this, please recall the notion of relative
𝑛-coherence (Definition 3.5.1) introduced in §3.5.

3.6.2 Lemma. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos. If 𝑒 ∶ 𝑈 ↠ 𝑉 is an effective epimorphism in 𝑿 and
𝑈 is quasicompact, then 𝑉 is quasicompact.
Proof. This is a special case of [SAG, Proposition A.2.1.3], or, alternatively, Proposi-
tion 3.4.1=[SAG, Proposition A.2.4.1].

3.6.3 Lemma. Let 𝑛 ≥ 1 be an integer and 𝑿 a locally (𝑛 − 1)-coherent ∞-topos. Let
𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 and let 𝑒 ∶ ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈 be a cover of 𝑈 where 𝐼 is finite and 𝑈𝑖 is 𝑛-coherent for
each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼. The following are equivalent:
(3.6.3.1) The effective epimorphism 𝑒 is relatively (𝑛 − 1)-coherent.

(3.6.3.2) For all 𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼, the object 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent.
(3.6.3.3) The object 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent.
Proof. If 𝑒 is relatively (𝑛 − 1)-coherent, then since coproducts in 𝑿 are universal, the
fiber product
                           (∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ) × (∐𝑗∈𝐼 𝑈𝑗 ) ≃ ∐ 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗
                                      𝑈
                                                       𝑖,𝑗∈𝐼

is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent. Thus 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent for all 𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼 [SAG, Remark
A.2.0.16].
    If each 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent, then since each 𝑈𝑖 is 𝑛-coherent we see that the
pullback of 𝑒 along itself
                                  ∐ 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 ↠ ∐ 𝑈𝑖
                                   𝑖,𝑗∈𝐼             𝑖∈𝐼

is relatively (𝑛 − 1)-coherent (Example 3.5.2=[SAG, Example A.2.1.2]). Applying [SAG,
Corollary A.2.1.5] we deduce that 𝑒 ∶ ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈 is relatively (𝑛 − 1)-coherent.



                                              62
   To conclude, note that if 𝑒 ∶ ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈 is relatively (𝑛 − 1)-coherent, then [SAG,
Proposition A.2.1.3] shows that 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent. On the other hand, if 𝑈 is 𝑛-coherent,
then 𝑒 is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent by Example 3.5.2=[SAG, Example A.2.1.2].
3.6.4 Proposition. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi and 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍.
Assume that:

(3.6.4.1) There exists a collection of 𝑛-coherent objects 𝒀0 ⊂ Obj(𝒀) of 𝒀 such that for
          every 𝑛-coherent object 𝑈 ∈ 𝒀 there exists a cover ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈 where 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝒀0
          for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼.
(3.6.4.2) The pullback functor 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝑿 takes objects of 𝒀0 to 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿.

(3.6.4.3) If 𝑛 ≥ 1, the ∞-topoi 𝑿 and 𝒀 are locally (𝑛 − 1)-coherent and 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝑿
          takes (𝑛 − 1)-coherent objects of 𝒀 to (𝑛 − 1)-coherent objects of 𝑿.
Then 𝑓∗ takes 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝒀 to 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿.
Proof. Let 𝑈 ∈ 𝒀 be an 𝑛-coherent object; we show that 𝑓∗ (𝑈) is 𝑛-coherent. Since 𝑈 is
0-coherent, by (3.6.4.1) there exists a cover

                                         𝑒 ∶ ∐ 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈
                                            𝑖∈𝐼

where 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝒀0 for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 and 𝐼 is finite. By (3.6.4.2), for all 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 the object 𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑖 ) is 𝑛-
coherent, so since 𝑛-coherent objects are closed under finite coproducts [SAG, Remark
A.2.0.16], the object
                               𝑓∗ (∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ) ≃ ∐ 𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑖 )
                                                       𝑖∈𝐼

is 𝑛-coherent.
    Note that
                                 𝑓∗ (𝑒) ∶ ∐ 𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑖 ) ↠ 𝑓∗ (𝑈)
                                          𝑖∈𝐼

is an effective epimorphism in 𝑿. If 𝑛 = 0, this proves the claim (Lemma 3.6.2). If 𝑛 ≥ 1,
then Lemma 3.6.3 shows that it suffices to show that for all 𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼, the object

                            𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑖 ) ×𝑓∗ (𝑈) 𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑗 ) ≃ 𝑓∗ (𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 )

is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent. This follows from the fact that 𝑈𝑖 ×𝑈 𝑈𝑗 is (𝑛 − 1)-coherent (by
Lemma 3.6.3) and the assumption that 𝑓∗ sends (𝑛 − 1)-coherent objects of 𝒀 to (𝑛 − 1)-
coherent objects of 𝑿.

    Proposition 3.6.4 shows that coherence of a geometric morphism between locally co-
herent ∞-topoi is equivalent to the a priori stronger condition that the pullback functor
preserve 𝑛-coherent objects for all 𝑛 ≥ 0; see also Corollary 3.4.6.
3.6.5 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism between locally coherent ∞-
topoi. Then 𝑓∗ is coherent if and only if 𝑓∗ takes 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝒀 to 𝑛-coherent
objects of 𝑿 for all 𝑛 ≥ 0.


                                                  63
    Proposition 3.6.4 also shows that coherence of a geometric morphism can be checked
on a generating set of coherent objects.
3.6.6 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism between locally coherent ∞-
topoi. Let 𝒀0 ⊂ Obj(𝒀coh ) be a collection of coherent objects such that for every object
𝑈 ∈ 𝒀 there exists a cover ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 𝑈 where 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝒀0 for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼. If for all 𝑈 ∈ 𝒀0 the
object 𝑓∗ (𝑈) is coherent, the geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 is coherent.
    For the next result, we need the following lemma.
3.6.7 Lemma. Let
                                 𝑓∗ ∶ (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) → (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 )
be a morphism of ∞-sites, and write よ𝜏𝑌 ∶ 𝑌 → Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌) for the sheafified Yoneda embed-
ding. If the topology 𝜏𝑋 is finitary, then

                                 𝑓∗ よ𝜏𝑌 ∶ 𝑌 → Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋)

factors through Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋)coh ⊂ Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋).
Proof. We have a commutative square

                                           𝑓∗
                                      𝑌             𝑋
                                ょ𝜏𝑌                   ょ𝜏𝑋

                                Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌)         Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋)
                                           𝑓∗


where the vertical functors are sheafified Yoneda embeddings. Since the topology 𝜏𝑋 is
finitary, the sheafified Yoneda embedding よ𝜏𝑋 ∶ 𝑋 → Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋) factors through Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋)coh
(Proposition 3.3.10=[SAG, Proposition A.3.1.3]).
3.6.8 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) → (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 ) be a morphism of finitary ∞-sites. Then the
induced geometric morphism

                                𝑓∗ ∶ Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋) → Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌)

is coherent.
Proof. By Proposition 3.3.10, both Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋) and Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌) are locally coherent. The image
よ𝜏 (𝑌) of 𝑌 under the sheafified Yoneda embedding generates Sh𝜏 (𝑌) under colimits,
   𝑌                                                                 𝑌
so by Corollary 3.6.6 it suffices to check that 𝑓∗ carries objects in よ𝜏 (𝑌) to coherent
                                                                        𝑌
objects of 𝑿; this the content of Lemma 3.6.7.
3.6.9. Proposition 3.5.6 and Corollaries 3.6.6 and 3.6.8 together show that a geometric
morphism of coherent 1-topoi is coherent in the sense of [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI, Definition
3.1] if and only if the geometric morphism of corresponding of 1-localic ∞-topoi is
coherent if and only if the geometric morphism of coherent 1-topoi is coherent in the
sense of Definition 3.3.8.



                                           64
  We now turn to the equivalence between coherent 𝑛-topoi and coherent 𝑛-localic
∞-topoi.
3.6.10 Notation. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. Write

                                    Top𝑛,coh
                                       ∞     ⊂ Topcoh
                                                  ∞

for the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑛-localic coherent ∞-topoi. Write

                                     Topcoh
                                        𝑛 ⊂ Top𝑛

for the subcategory of the (𝑛 + 1)-category of 𝑛-topoi with objects coherent 𝑛-topoi and
morphisms coherent geometric morphisms. When 𝑛 = 1, the 2-category Topcoh       1 is the
2-category of ordinary coherent topoi and coherent geometric morphisms (both in the
sense of [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI]).
Proposition 3.5.6 and Corollary 3.6.6 immediately imply the following:

3.6.11 Proposition. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The equivalence of ∞-categories (−)≤𝑛−1 ∶ Top𝑛∞ ⥲ Top𝑛
restricts to an equivalence

                             (−)≤𝑛−1 ∶ Top𝑛,coh
                                          ∞     ⥲ Topcoh
                                                     𝑛

3.6.12 Corollary. Let 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍. The following are equivalent for a geometric morphism
𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 between 𝑛-localic coherent ∞-topoi:
(3.6.12.1) The geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 is coherent.
(3.6.12.2) The pullback functor 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝑿 carries (𝑛 − 1)-truncated 𝑛-coherent objects
           of 𝒀 to 𝑛-coherent objects of 𝑿.

3.7    Examples of coherent ∞-topoi from algebraic geometry
In this section we use Corollary 3.6.8 to provide a few examples of coherent ∞-topoi
arising from algebraic geometry.
3.7.1 Example. For a spectral topological space 𝑆, write Openqc (𝑆) ⊂ Open(𝑆) for the
locale of quasicompact opens in 𝑆. Since the quasicompact opens of 𝑆 form a basis for
the topology on 𝑆 that is closed under finite intersections, the ∞-topos Sh(Openqc (𝑆))
is 0-localic (3.2.4). Applying [86, Proposition B.6.4] we see that the inclusion

                                Openqc (𝑆) ⊂ Open(𝑆)

induces an equivalence of 0-localic ∞-topoi

                                  𝑆̃ ≃ Sh(Openqc (𝑆))

(see also Corollary 3.12.14). Since Grothendieck topology on Openqc (𝑆) is finitary, the
∞-topos 𝑆̃ of sheaves on 𝑆 is a coherent ∞-topos. (Cf. [SAG, Lemma 2.3.4.1]).


                                           65
    If 𝑓 ∶ 𝑆 → 𝑇 is a quasicompact continuous map of spectral topological spaces, the
inverse image map 𝑓−1 ∶ Open(𝑇) → Open(𝑆) restricts to a map

                                    𝑓−1 ∶ Openqc (𝑇) → Openqc (𝑆) .

Corollary 3.6.8 shows that the induced geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑆̃ → 𝑇 ̃ is coherent.
Since spectral topological spaces are sober, a continuous map 𝑓 ∶ 𝑆 → 𝑇 of spectral
topological spaces induces a coherent geometric morphism on the level of ∞-topoi if
and only if 𝑓 is quasicompact.
3.7.2. Note that if 𝑿 is a coherent ∞-topos, then the underlying topological space of 𝑿
is spectral (Corollary 3.4.10).

    Combining the fact that the Zariski, Nisnevich22 , étale, and proétale23 topoi of a
scheme all have the same underlying topological space with the fact that if a scheme 𝑋
is quasicompact and quasiseparated, then the 1-topoi of sheaves on 𝑋 in each of these
topologies is coherent [SAG, Proposition 2.3.4.2 & Remark 3.7.4.2; 11, Appendix A; 86,
Example 7.1.7], we deduce the following:

3.7.3 Proposition. The following are equivalent for a scheme 𝑋:
(3.7.3.1) The scheme 𝑋 is coherent (i.e., quasicompact and quasiseparated).
(3.7.3.2) The Zariski ∞-topos 𝑋zar of 𝑋 is a coherent ∞-topos.
(3.7.3.3) The Nisnevich ∞-topos 𝑋nis of 𝑋 is a coherent ∞-topos.

(3.7.3.4) The étale ∞-topos 𝑋ét of 𝑋 is a coherent ∞-topos.
(3.7.3.5) The proétale ∞-topos 𝑋proét of 𝑋 is a coherent ∞-topos.
3.7.4. In the case of the étale topology, see also [SAG, Proposition 2.3.4.2].

3.7.5 Example. Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑌 be a morphism of coherent schemes and let

                                         𝜏 ∈ {zar, nis, ét, proét} .

Then the induced geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑋𝜏 → 𝑌𝜏 on ∞-topoi of 𝜏-sheaves is a
coherent geometric morphism of coherent ∞-topoi. (Cf. [SAG, Proposition 2.3.5.1])

3.7.6 Example. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. Then the natural geometric morphisms

                     𝑋proét → 𝑋ét ,        𝑋ét → 𝑋nis ,        and       𝑋nis → 𝑋zar

are all coherent geometric morphisms of coherent ∞-topoi.
  22
       For background on the Nisnevich topology, see [SAG, §3.7; 65; 61; 100].
  23
       For background on the proétale topology, see [STK, Tags 0988 & 099R; 17].




                                                     66
3.8     Classification of bounded coherent ∞-topoi via ∞-pretopoi
In this section we explain how an ∞-topos that is both bounded and coherent is deter-
mined by its truncated coherent objects.

3.8.1 Notation. Write Topbc        coh
                           ∞ ⊂ Top∞ for the full subcategory spanned by those coher-
ent ∞-topoi that are also bounded, that is, the bounded coherent ∞-topoi.
    To a large extent, bounded coherent ∞-topoi function in much the same way as co-
herent 1-topoi. In particular, any bounded coherent ∞-topos is, in a canonical fashion,
the ∞-category of sheaves on an ∞-site with excellent formal properties.
3.8.2 Definition. An ∞-category 𝑋 called an ∞-pretopos if and only if the following
conditions are satisfied.
      → The ∞-category 𝑋 admits finite limits.
      → The ∞-category 𝑋 admits finite coproducts, which are universal and disjoint.
      → Groupoid objects in 𝑋 are effective, and their geometric realizations are universal.
    If 𝑋 and 𝑌 are ∞-pretopoi, then a functor 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑌 → 𝑋 is a morphism of ∞-pretopoi
   ∗
if 𝑓 preserves finite limits, finite coproducts, and effective epimorphisms. We write

                                    Pretop∞ ⊂ Cat∞,δ1

for the subcategory consisting of ∞-pretopoi and morphisms of ∞-pretopoi.
3.8.3 Example. If 𝑿 is a coherent ∞-topos, then the full subcategory 𝑿coh ⊆ 𝑿 spanned
by the coherent objects is an ∞-pretopos [SAG, Corollary A.6.1.7].
The following two useful facts are immediate from the definitions.
3.8.4 Lemma. Let {𝑋𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 be a collection of ∞-pretopoi. Then the product ∏𝑖∈𝐼 𝑋𝑖 in
Cat∞,δ1 is an ∞-pretopos and for each 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼 the projection

                                     pr𝑗 ∶ ∏ 𝑋𝑖 → 𝑋𝑗
                                           𝑖∈𝐼

is a morphism of ∞-pretopoi.
3.8.5 Lemma. Given morphisms of ∞-pretopoi 𝑋 → 𝑍 and 𝑌 → 𝑍, the pullback 𝑋 ×𝑍 𝑌
in Cat∞,δ1 is an ∞-pretopos, and the projections

                      pr1 ∶ 𝑋 ×𝑍 𝑌 → 𝑋      and    pr2 ∶ 𝑋 ×𝑍 𝑌 → 𝑋

are morphisms of ∞-pretopoi.
3.8.6 Notation. Let 𝑋 be an ∞-pretopos, and write 𝐸 ⊆ 𝑋 for the collection of effective
epimorphisms in 𝑋. Then (𝑋, 𝐸) is an ∞-presite, and we write eff ≔ 𝜏𝐸 for the resulting
finitary topology on 𝑋. We call this topology the effective epimorphism topology on 𝑋
[SAG, §A.6.2].


                                             67
3.8.7. The effective epimorphism topology on an ∞-pretopos is a subcanonical topol-
ogy [SAG, Corollary A.6.2.6].
3.8.8 Definition. An ∞-pretopos 𝑋 is bounded if and only if 𝑋 is δ0 -small and every
object of 𝑋 is truncated. We write
                                   Pretopb∞ ⊂ Pretop∞
for the full subcategory spanned by the bounded ∞-pretopoi.
3.8.9 Theorem ([SAG, Theorem A.7.5.3]). The constructions
                               coh
                          𝑿 ↦ 𝑿<∞          and      𝑋 ↦ Sheff (𝑋)
are mutually inverse equivalences of ∞-categories

                                    Topbc        b,op
                                       ∞ ≃ Pretop∞ .

In light of (3.4.13) we deduce the following variant for Postnikov complete coherent
∞-topoi:
                             post,coh
3.8.10 Corollary. Write Top∞          ⊂ Topcoh
                                           ∞ for the full subcategory spanned by the Post-
                                                             coh
nikov complete coherent ∞-topoi. The constructions 𝑿 ↦ 𝑿<∞       and 𝑋 ↦ Sheff (𝑋)post are
mutually inverse equivalences of ∞-categories
                                    post,coh
                                 Top∞        ≃ Pretopb,op
                                                     ∞ .

    We finish this section by recording the following bounded analogue of Lemma 3.8.4
that we use later, as well as a similar result for functor ∞-categories
3.8.11 Lemma. Let {𝑋𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 be a finite collection of bounded ∞-pretopoi. Then the ∞-pre-
topos given by the product ∏𝑖∈𝐼 𝑋𝑖 in Cat∞,δ1 is a bounded ∞-pretopos.
Proof. For each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 the ∞-category 𝑋𝑖 is δ0 -small, so the product ∏𝑖∈𝐼 𝑋𝑖 is also
δ0 -small. For any integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, an object 𝐹 ∈ ∏𝑖∈𝐼 𝑋𝑖 is 𝑛-truncated if and only if
pr𝑖 (𝐹) ∈ 𝑋𝑖 is 𝑛-truncated for all 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼. Since 𝐼 is finite and every object of each of the
∞-categories {𝑋𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 is truncated by assumption, every object of the product ∏𝑖∈𝐼 𝑋𝑖 is
truncated.
3.8.12 Lemma. Let 𝐶 be an ∞-category and 𝑋 an ∞-pretopos. Then:
(3.8.12.1) The ∞-category Fun(𝐶, 𝑋) is an ∞-pretopos.
(3.8.12.2) If 𝐶 is δ0 -small and has finitely many objects up to equivalence and 𝑋 is bounded,
           then the ∞-pretopos Fun(𝐶, 𝑋) is bounded.
Proof. First, (3.8.12.1) is clear from the definitions and the fact that (co)limits in functor
∞-categories are computed objectwise.
     In the situation of (3.8.12.2), note that since 𝐶 and 𝑋 are δ0 -small, the ∞-pretopos
Fun(𝐶, 𝑋) is δ0 -small. Note that for any integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, an object 𝐹 ∈ Fun(𝐶, 𝑋) is 𝑛-
truncated if and only if 𝐹(𝑐) is 𝑛-truncated for each 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶. So since every object of 𝑋 is
truncated and 𝐶 has finitely many objects up to equivalence, every object of Fun(𝐶, 𝑋)
is also truncated. Hence the ∞-pretopos Fun(𝐶, 𝑋) is bounded.


                                             68
3.9     Coherence of inverse limits
We now recall that bounded coherent ∞-topoi and coherent geometric morphisms are
stable under inverse limits in Top∞ .

3.9.1 Proposition ([SAG, Proposition A.8.3.1]). The ∞-category Pretopb∞ admits fil-
tered colimits and the forgetful functor Pretopb∞ → Cat∞,δ1 preserves filtered colimits.

3.9.2 Proposition ([SAG, Proposition A.8.3.2]). Let 𝑋 ∶ 𝐴 → Pretopb∞ be a filtered
diagram of bounded ∞-pretopoi. Then the natural geometric morphism

                            Sheff (colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑋𝛼 ) → lim𝛼∈𝐴op Sheff (𝑋𝛼 )

is an equivalence in Top∞ .

3.9.3. See [27, Lemma 3.3] for a more general statement about filtered colimits of fini-
tary ∞-sites.
The following is immediate from the previous two propositions and Theorem 3.1.10=
[HTT, Theorem 6.3.3.1].

3.9.4 Corollary ([SAG, Corollary A.8.3.3]). The ∞-category Topbc∞ admits inverse limits
and the inclusion Topbc
                     ∞  → Top  ∞ and  forgetful functor Top bc
                                                            ∞  → Cat∞,δ1 both preserve
inverse limits.

3.10      Coherence & preservation of filtered colimits
The goal of this section is to prove the appropriate ∞-toposic generalization of the fact
that a coherent geometric morphism of 1-topoi preserves filtered colimits (see Corol-
lary 3.10.5).24
    We begin be recalling a basic fact about filtered colimits of truncated objects and
introducing some convenient terminology.
3.10.1 Recollection. Since filtered colimits commute with finite limits in an ∞-topos,
for any ∞-topos 𝑿 and integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, the inclusion 𝑿≤𝑛 ↪ 𝑿 preserves filtered colim-
its. Thus 𝑿≤𝑛 is an ω-accessible localization of 𝑿.
3.10.2 Definition. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. We say that an object 𝑋 ∈ 𝐶 is
almost compact if τ≤𝑛 (𝐶) is a compact object of the 𝑛-category 𝐶≤𝑛 .
     We say that functor 𝐹 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝐷 between presentable ∞-categories almost preserves
filtered colimits if for each integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, the functor 𝐹 ∶ 𝐶≤𝑛 → 𝐷 preserves filtered
colimits.
3.10.3 Lemma. Let (𝑋, 𝜏) be a finitary ∞-site, write 𝑿 ≔ Sh𝜏 (𝑋), and write よ𝜏 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑿
for the sheafified Yoneda embedding. Then for any object 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋, the sheaf よ𝜏 (𝑥) is almost
compact.
  24
     We learned how to simplify and generalize the material in this section from its original form through a
preprint of Chough [25, Theorem 3.4].




                                                    69
Proof. Write 𝑈 ≔ よ𝜏 (𝑥) and let 𝑝∗ ∶ 𝑿/𝑈 → 𝑿 denote the natural étale geometric
morphism. Let 𝑉 ∶ 𝐴 → 𝑿≤𝑛 be a filtered diagram. Then we have natural equivalences

               Map𝑿 (𝑈, colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 ) ≃ Map𝑿 (𝑝! (1𝑿/𝑈 ), colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 )
                                       ≃ Map𝑿/𝑈 (1𝑿/𝑈 , colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑝∗ (𝑉𝛼 )) .

Since 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is coherent (Proposition 3.3.10=[SAG, Proposition A.3.1.3]), the global
sections functor
                           Map𝑿/𝑈 (1𝑿/𝑈 , −) ∶ 𝑿/𝑈 → S
almost preserves filtered colimits [SAG, Proposition A.2.3.1]. Hence we have natural
equivalences

                Map𝑿 (𝑈, colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 ) ≃ colim Map𝑿/𝑈 (1𝑿/𝑈 , 𝑝∗ (𝑉𝛼 ))
                                            𝛼∈𝐴
                                        ≃ colim Map𝑿 (𝑝! (1𝑿/𝑈 ), 𝑉𝛼 )
                                            𝛼∈𝐴
                                        ≃ colim Map𝑿 (𝑈, 𝑉𝛼 ) .
                                            𝛼∈𝐴

3.10.4 Proposition. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ (𝑌, 𝜏𝑌 ) → (𝑋, 𝜏𝑋 ) be a morphism of finitary ∞-sites. Then
the induced functor 𝑓∗ ∶ Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋) → Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌) almost preserves filtered colimits.
Proof. Write 𝑿 ≔ Sh𝜏𝑋 (𝑋), 𝒀 ≔ Sh𝜏𝑌 (𝑌), and よ𝜏𝑋 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑿 and よ𝜏𝑌 ∶ 𝑌 → 𝒀 for
the sheafified Yoneda embeddings. Let 𝑉 ∶ 𝐴 → 𝑿≤𝑛 be a filtered diagram. Since the
essential image of よ𝜏𝑌 generates 𝒀 under colimits, to see that the natural morphism

                         colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑓∗ (𝑉𝛼 ) → 𝑓∗ (colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 )

is an equivalence, it suffices to show that for all 𝑦 ∈ 𝑌, the induced morphism

         Map𝒀 (よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑓∗ (𝑉𝛼 )) → Map𝒀 (よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), 𝑓∗ (colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 ))

is an equivalence. Applying Lemma 3.10.3 to よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦) and 𝑓∗ よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦) ≃ よ𝜏𝑋 (𝑓∗ (𝑦)) we
see that we have equivalences

         Map𝒀 (よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑓∗ (𝑉𝛼 )) ≃ colim Map𝒀 (よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), 𝑓∗ (𝑉𝛼 ))
                                                𝛼∈𝐴
                                           ≃ colim Map𝒀 (𝑓∗ よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), 𝑉𝛼 )
                                                𝛼∈𝐴
                                           ≃ Map𝒀 (𝑓∗ よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 )
                                           ≃ Map𝒀 (よ𝜏𝑌 (𝑦), 𝑓∗ (colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝑉𝛼 )) .

    In light of Theorem 3.8.9=[SAG, Theorem A.7.5.3], Proposition 3.10.4 specializes to
the following.
3.10.5 Corollary. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a coherent geometric morphism between bounded
coherent ∞-topoi. Then the functor 𝑓∗ almost preserves filtered colimits.



                                           70
3.11    Points, Conceptual Completeness, & Deligne Completeness
In this section we discuss points of ∞-topoi as well as the ∞-toposic generalizations of
the Conceptual Completeness Theorem of Makkai–Reyes and Deligne’s Completeness
Theorem.
3.11.1 Notation. For an ∞-topos 𝑿, we write

                         Pt(𝑿) ≔ Fun∗ (S, 𝑿)op ≃ Fun∗ (𝑿, S)

for the ∞-category of points of 𝑿.
    Note that a morphism 𝑥∗′ → 𝑥∗ of Pt(𝑿) is a natural transformation 𝑥∗ → 𝑥∗′ . (The
morphisms are the ‘geometric transformations’ usually preferred in 1-topos theory.) This
choice is compatible with the direction of posets: for instance, when 𝑃 is a noetherian
                  ̃ ≃ 𝑃.
poset, one has Pt(𝑃)
For general ∞-topoi, the passage to its ∞-category of points will lose quite a bit of in-
formation. However, the ∞-toposic version of the Conceptual Completeness Theorem
of Makkai–Reyes [87, Theorem 9.2] tells us that bounded coherent ∞-topoi are to some
extent determined by their ∞-categories of points.
3.11.2 Theorem (Conceptual Completeness; [SAG, Theorem A.9.0.6]). A geometric mor-
phism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 between bounded coherent ∞-topoi is an equivalence if and only if 𝑓∗
is coherent and the induced functor Pt(𝑓∗ ) ∶ Pt(𝑿) → Pt(𝒀) is an equivalence of ∞-cate-
gories.
3.11.3 Definition. An ∞-topos 𝑿 has enough points if a morphism 𝜙 in 𝑿 is an equiv-
alence if and only if for every point 𝑥∗ ∈ Pt(𝑿) the stalk 𝑥∗ 𝜙 is an equivalence.
    In classical topos theory, the Deligne Completeness Theorem [SGA 4ii , Exposé VI,
Proposition 9.0] states that a locally coherent 1-topos has enough points. This is no
longer true in the setting of ∞-topoi, the main obstruction being that ∞-connective
morphisms in an ∞-topos need not be equivalences. For this reason the ∞-categorical
version of Deligne’s theorem takes place in the setting of ∞-topoi where ∞-connective
morphisms are equivalences, i.e., ∞-topoi in which Whitehead’s Theorem is valid.

3.11.4 Recollection. A morphism 𝜙 ∶ 𝑈 → 𝑉 in an ∞-topos 𝑿 is ∞-connective if 𝜙 is
𝑛-connective for each integer 𝑛 ≥ −1.
     Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi. Since the left adjoint 𝑓∗ is
left exact and preserves effective epimorphisms, if 𝜙 is an ∞-connective morphism of
𝒀, then 𝑓∗ (𝜙) is an ∞-connective morphism of 𝑿.

3.11.5 Definition. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos. An object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is hypercomplete if 𝑈 is
local with respect to the class of ∞-connective morphisms in 𝑿. We write 𝑿hyp ⊂ 𝑿
for the full subcategory spanned by the hypercomplete objects of 𝑿. We call 𝑿hyp the
hypercompletion of 𝑿, and we say that 𝑿 is hypercomplete if 𝑿hyp = 𝑿.
3.11.6. The ∞-category 𝑿hyp ⊂ 𝑿 is a left exact localization of 𝑿, hence an ∞-topos
[HTT, p. 699]. Moreover, the ∞-topos 𝑿hyp is hypercomplete [HTT, Lemma 6.5.2.12].


                                           71
3.11.7 Construction (functoriality of hypercompletions). Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geomet-
ric morphism of ∞-topoi. Since 𝑓∗ preserves ∞-connective morphisms, the pushfor-
ward 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 preserves hypercomplete objects, hence restricts to a functor
                                    𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿hyp → 𝒀hyp .
The functor 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿hyp → 𝒀hyp is the right adjoint in a geometric morphism with left
exact left adjoint given by the composite
                                        𝑓∗
                                 𝒀hyp         𝑿     𝑿hyp

of 𝑓∗ with the left adjoint to the inclusion 𝑿hyp ⊂ 𝑿. We denote this geometric mor-
           hyp
phism by 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿hyp → 𝒀hyp .
3.11.8 Warning. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi. The pullback
functor 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝑿 generally does not preserve hypercomplete objects; since every
∞-topos is a left exact localization of a presheaf ∞-topos, if this were true then every
∞-topos would be hypercomplete.
   The hypercompletion 𝑿hyp is characterized by the following universal property.
3.11.9 Proposition ([HTT, Proposition 6.5.2.13]). Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos. Then for every
hypercomplete ∞-topos 𝑯, composition with the inclusion 𝑿hyp ⊂ 𝑿 induces an equiva-
lence
                          Fun∗ (𝑯, 𝑿hyp ) ⥲ Fun∗ (𝑯, 𝑿) .
Consequently, the assignment 𝑿 ↦ 𝑿hyp defines a functor right adjoint to the inclusion
of hypercomplete ∞-topoi into all ∞-topoi.
3.11.10 Example. An ∞-topos with enough points is hypercomplete.
3.11.11 Example. Let 𝑿 be a 1-topos with corresponding 1-localic ∞-topos 𝑿′ . Then
𝑿 has enough points (in the sense of [SGA 4i , Exposé IV, Définition 6.4.1]) if and only
if the hypercomplete ∞-topos (𝑿′ )hyp has enough points.
3.11.12 Example. An ∞-topos 𝑿 is hypercomplete if and only if the pullback functor
𝑝∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝑿post is conservative. In particular, if Postnikov towers converge in 𝑿 (Defi-
nition 3.2.11), then 𝑿 is hypercomplete. However, the converse in false:
3.11.13 Warning. Postnikov towers need not converge in a hypercomplete ∞-topos.
Morel and Voevodsky provide the following counterexample [95, §2.1, Example 1.30].
Let 𝐺 denote the profinite group ∏𝑖≥1 𝐙/2. Write 𝑿 for the ∞-topos of hypersheaves
on the site of finite continuous 𝐺-sets with respect to the topology in which a family of
maps is a covering if and only if it is jointly surjective. Since Γ𝑿∗ ∶ S → 𝑿 preserves con-
nected objects, the constant sheaf 𝑈 ≔ Γ𝑿∗ (∏𝑖≥1 K(𝐙/2, 𝑖)) at the product of Eilenberg–
MacLane spaces K(𝐙/2, 𝑖) is connected. On the other hand, one can show that the limit
of the Postnikov tower of 𝑈 is the product
                              lim τ≤𝑛 𝑈 ≃ ∏ Γ𝑿∗ (K(𝐙/2, 𝑖)) ,
                              𝑛≥0
                                             𝑖≥1

and, moreover, that this object is not connected. In particular, the map 𝑈 → lim𝑛≥0 τ≤𝑛 𝑈
is not an equivalence.


                                              72
   In light of Example 3.11.10, the following is the correct ∞-toposic generalization of
Deligne’s Completeness Theorem.
3.11.14 Theorem (∞-Categorical Deligne Completeness; [SAG, Proposition A.4.0.5]).
An ∞-topos that is locally coherent and hypercomplete has enough points.
3.11.15. Please observe that for an ∞-topos 𝑿, the hypercompletion 𝑿hyp has enough
points if and only if ∞-connectiveness of morphisms in 𝑿 can be checked on stalks.
Indeed, a morphism 𝜙 in 𝑿 is ∞-connective if and only if for every point 𝑥∗ of 𝑿, the
stalk 𝑥∗ 𝜙 is an equivalence in S. The Deligne Completeness Theorem (Theorem 3.11.14=
[SAG, Proposition A.4.0.5]) and Corollary 3.11.17 thus show that ∞-connectiveness in
a locally coherent ∞-topos can be checked on stalks.
    We have already seen that the coherence of an ∞-topos only depends on its hyper-
completion (Lemma 3.4.12). The following proposition gives a more refined assertion
about the relationship between the coherent objects of an ∞-topos and its hypercom-
pletion.
3.11.16 Proposition ([SAG, Proposition A.2.2.2]). Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos, and write
Lhyp ∶ 𝑿 → 𝑿hyp for the left adjoint to the inclusion 𝑿hyp ↪ 𝑿. If 𝑿 is locally 𝑛-coherent
for all 𝑛 ≥ 0, then:
(3.11.16.1) The ∞-topos 𝑿hyp is locally 𝑛-coherent for all 𝑛 ≥ 0.
(3.11.16.2) An object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿hyp is coherent if and only if 𝑈 is coherent when viewed as an
            object of 𝑿.
(3.11.16.3) An object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿 is coherent if and only if Lhyp (𝑈) ∈ 𝑿hyp is coherent.
3.11.17 Corollary. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos. If 𝑿 is (locally) coherent, then the hypercomple-
tion 𝑿hyp of 𝑿 is (locally) coherent.
3.11.18 Example. Let 𝑿 be a bounded coherent ∞-topos. Then since 𝑿 is also locally
coherent (Example 3.3.6), the hypercompletion 𝑿hyp of 𝑿 is coherent and locally coher-
ent.

3.12       Bases for ∞-topoi
Let 𝑊 be a topological space and 𝐵 ⊂ Open(𝑊) a basis for 𝑊. Upon passing to sheaves
of sets, right Kan extension defines an equivalence of 1-topoi

                                Sh(𝐵; Set) ⥲ Sh(𝑊; Set)

with inverse given by restriction of presheaves [86, Proposition B.6.4].
    The analogous statement for sheaves of spaces is false: open subsets of the Hilbert
cube ∏𝑖∈𝐙 [0, 1] homeomorphic to a product [0, 1) × ∏𝑖∈𝐙 [0, 1] form a basis 𝐵 for the
topology on the Hilbert cube, but sheaves of spaces on 𝐵 do not coincide with sheaves
on the Hilbert cube [SAG, Counterexample 20.4.0.1]. The goal of this section is to show
that although right Kan extension need not define an equivalence

(3.12.1)                            Sh(𝐵) → Sh(𝑊) ,


                                            73
the failure of (3.12.1) to be an equivalence is fundamentally infinitary in nature and
(3.12.1) is an equivalence when we restrict to hypercomplete objects.
    We begin by recalling the basics of bases for sites and ∞-sites.
3.12.2 Definition. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site. A basis for the topology 𝜏 on 𝐶 is a full
subcategory 𝐵 ⊂ 𝐶 satisfying the following property: for every object 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶, there exists
a set of morphisms {𝑓𝑖 ∶ 𝑏𝑖 → 𝑐}𝑖∈𝐼 such that 𝑏𝑖 ∈ 𝐵 for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 and the set {𝑓𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼
generates a 𝜏-covering sieve on 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶.
3.12.3. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site and 𝐵 ⊂ 𝐶 a basis for 𝜏. Then there is a unique topology
𝜏|𝐵 on 𝐵 satisfying the following property: for each object 𝑏 ∈ 𝐵, a sieve 𝑆 ⊂ 𝐵/𝑏 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -
covering sieve if and only if the image of 𝑆 under the embedding 𝐵/𝑏 ↪ 𝐶/𝑏 generates a
𝜏 covering sieve of 𝑏 ∈ 𝐶.
    We always regard a basis 𝐵 ⊂ 𝐶 as an ∞-site equipped the topology 𝜏|𝐵 . To simplify
notation, we often write 𝜏 instead of 𝜏|𝐵 .
3.12.4. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site and 𝐵 ⊂ 𝐶 a basis for 𝜏. Then for every object 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶, the
full subcategory
                                 𝐵/𝑐 ≔ 𝐵 ×𝐶 𝐶/𝑐 ⊂ 𝐶/𝑐
is a basis for the topology on 𝐶/𝑐 induced by 𝜏.

3.12.5 Example. Let 𝑊 be a topological space. A full subposet 𝐵 ⊂ Open(𝑊) is a basis
for the standard topology on Open(𝑊) (in the sense of Definition 3.12.2) if and only if
𝐵 defines a basis for the topological space 𝑊 in the usual sense: every open set of 𝑊 can
be written as a union of opens belonging to 𝐵.
3.12.6 Example. Let 𝑃 be a poset. Then the functor 𝑃op → Open(𝑃) defined by 𝑝 ↦ 𝑃≥𝑝
is fully faithful and defines a basis for the topology on the Alexandroff topological space
𝑃. The induced topology on 𝑃op ⊂ Open(𝑃) is the trivial topology.
     The first property of bases for ∞-sites is that a presheaf on 𝐵 is a sheaf if and only if
its right Kan extension along the inclusion 𝐵 ↪ 𝐶 is a sheaf on 𝐶.
3.12.7 Lemma ([6, Proposition A.5; 86, Proposition B.6.6]). Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site and
𝑖 ∶ 𝐵 ↪ 𝐶 a basis for the topology 𝜏 on 𝐶. Then:
(3.12.7.1) A presheaf 𝐹 ∶ 𝐵op → S on 𝐵 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -sheaf if and only if the right Kan extension
           of 𝐹 along 𝑖 ∶ 𝐵op ↪ 𝐶op is a 𝜏-sheaf on 𝐶.
(3.12.7.2) Right Kan extension along 𝑖 defines a fully faithful right adjoint

                                          𝑖∗ ∶ Sh𝜏 (𝐵) ↪ Sh𝜏 (𝐶)

           with left exact left adjoint given by the composite

                                            𝑖∗                 L𝜏
                                Sh𝜏 (𝐶)               PSh(𝐵)        Sh𝜏 (𝐵)

           of presheaf restriction followed by 𝜏|𝐵 -sheafification.


                                                 74
Proof. Note that (3.12.7.2) is an immediate consequence of (3.12.7.1). Write 𝑖∗ 𝐹 for the
right Kan extension of 𝐹 along 𝑖 ∶ 𝐵op ↪ 𝐶op .
    To prove (3.12.7.1), we first show that if 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is a 𝜏-sheaf on 𝐶, then 𝐹 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -sheaf on
𝐵. Let 𝑏 ∈ 𝐵 and let 𝑆𝐵 ⊂ 𝐵/𝑏 be a covering sieve on 𝑏; we need to show that the natural
map
                                   𝜌𝐵𝑏 ∶ 𝐹(𝑏) → limop 𝐹(𝑏′ )
                                                         𝑏′ ∈𝑆𝐵

is an equivalence. Let 𝑆𝐶 ⊂ 𝐶/𝑏 denote the sieve generated by 𝑆𝐵 ⊂ 𝐶/𝑏 . Since 𝑆𝐵 is a
covering sieve for the topology 𝜏|𝐵 on 𝐵, the sieve 𝑆𝐶 is a 𝜏-covering sieve. Now notice
that the map 𝜌𝐵𝑏 factors as a composite

                             𝜌𝐶𝑏                           𝜌′𝑏
           𝐹(𝑏) = 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏)              limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑐′ )                limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏′ ) = limop 𝐹′ (𝑏′ ) .
                                      𝑐′ ∈𝑆𝐶                         𝑏′ ∈𝑆𝐵             𝑏′ ∈𝑆𝐵


The morphism 𝜌𝐶𝑏 is an equivalence because 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is a 𝜏-sheaf on 𝐶 and 𝑆𝐶 is a 𝜏-covering
sieve. The morphism 𝜌′𝑏 is an equivalence because 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is the right Kan extension of 𝐹.
     Now we show that if 𝐹 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -sheaf on 𝐵, then 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is a 𝜏-sheaf on 𝐶. Let 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶 and
let 𝑆𝐶 ⊂ 𝐶/𝑐 be a 𝜏-covering sieve of 𝑐. Define

                                           𝑆𝐵 ≔ 𝑆 ×𝐶 𝐶/𝑐 ⊂ 𝐵/𝑐 .

We need to show that the top horizontal map in the commutative square

                                      𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑐)                    limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑐′ )
                                                            𝑐′ ∈𝑆𝐶



                                   limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏)                 limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏)
                                   𝑏∈𝐵/𝑐                     𝑏∈𝑆𝐵


is an equivalence. The vertical maps are equivalences because 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is the right Kan exten-
sion of its restriction to 𝐵; hence it suffices to show that the lower horizontal map is an
equivalence. To see this, first note that the lower horizonal map can be rewritten as a
limit of maps

(3.12.8)               limop limop 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏′ ) →            lim       op
                                                                         lim op 𝑖∗ 𝐹(𝑏′ ) .
                      𝑏∈𝐵/𝑐 𝑏′ ∈𝐵/𝑏                 [𝑓 ∶ 𝑏→𝑐]∈𝑆𝐵 𝑏′ ∈𝑓∗ 𝑆𝐵


To conclude, note that since 𝑖∗ 𝑖∗ 𝐹 = 𝐹 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -sheaf on 𝐵 and 𝐵 is a basis for (𝐶, 𝜏), the
morphism (3.12.8) is an equivalence.
     For sheaves of sets, Lemma 3.12.7 admits a converse: a presheaf of sets 𝐹 on 𝐶 is a
sheaf if and only if the restriction of 𝐹 to 𝐵 is a sheaf and 𝐹 is the right Kan extension
of its restriction. This fails for sheaves of spaces in general. The goal of the remainder of
this chapter is to show that this is true if we restrict too hypersheaves. The technique is
                hyp                 hyp
to squeeze Sh𝜏 (𝐶) between Sh𝜏 (𝐵) and Sh𝜏 (𝐵) and apply the following observation.



                                                     75
3.12.9 Lemma. Let 𝑿 and 𝒀 be ∞-topoi, and assume that there are fully faithful geometric
morphisms
                                                 𝑓∗         𝑔∗
                                      𝒀hyp             𝑿         𝒀
                              hyp              hyp
and the composite 𝑔∗ 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀 → 𝒀    is the identity. Then 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀hyp ↪ 𝑿hyp and
      hyp    hyp
𝑔∗ ∶ 𝑿 ↪ 𝒀 are mutually inverse equivalences.
Proof. Immediate from the fact that 𝑓∗ and 𝑔∗ preserve hypercomplete objects Con-
struction 3.11.7 and the assumption that 𝑔∗ 𝑓∗ ≃ id𝒀hyp .
    The key technical lemma we need to show that a hypersheaf on 𝐶 is the right Kan
extension of its restriction to 𝐵 is a relative version of [SAG, Lemma 20.4.5.4]. It seems
to have first been noticed by Aoki [6, Lemma A.10].
3.12.10 Lemma. Let 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 be a geometric morphism of ∞-topoi and 𝐵 ⊂ 𝒀
a small full subcategory. Assume that for each object 𝑌 ∈ 𝒀, there exists a morphism
𝑒 ∶ ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 → 𝑌 such that 𝑈𝑖 ∈ 𝐵 for each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 and 𝑓∗ (𝑒) is an effective epimorphism in
𝑿. Then 𝑓∗ (colim𝑈∈𝐵 𝑈) is an ∞-connective object of 𝑿.
Proof. Write 𝑋 ≔ 𝑓∗ (colim𝑈∈𝐵 𝑈). We prove that 𝑋 is 𝑛-connective for each 𝑛 ≥ 0
by induction on 𝑛. For the base case, note that since 𝑓∗ is a left exact left adjoint, the
unique morphism 𝑒 ∶ ∐𝑈∈𝐵 𝑓∗ (𝑈) → 1𝑿 is an effective epimorphism. The effective epi-
morphism 𝑒 factors as a composite

                          ∐ 𝑓∗ (𝑈)                   colim 𝑓∗ (𝑈)    1𝑿 ,
                                                      𝑈∈𝐵
                          𝑈∈𝐵

hence the unique morphism 𝑋 → 1𝑿 is an effective epimorphism (i.e., 𝑋 is 0-connective).
   For the inductive step, we assume that 𝑋 is (𝑛 − 1)-connective and prove that 𝑋 is
(𝑛−1)-connective. That is, we need to show that the diagonal Δ𝑋 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑋 × 𝑋 is (𝑛−1)-
connective. Since 𝑓∗ is a left exact left adjoint and colimits in an ∞-topos are universal,
we can rewrite 𝑋 × 𝑋 as the colimit
                           𝑋×𝑋≃           colim 𝑓∗ (𝑈) × 𝑓∗ (𝑈′ ) .
                                        (𝑈,𝑈′ )∈𝐵×𝐵

Rewriting 𝑋 as an iterated colimit
                                 𝑋≃     colim           colim 𝑓∗ (𝑉) ,
                                      (𝑈,𝑈′ )∈𝐵×𝐵 𝑉∈𝐵/(𝑈×𝑈′ )

we see that we can rewrite the diagonal Δ𝑋 as a colimit of maps
                       𝛿𝑈,𝑈′ ∶    colim 𝑓∗ (𝑉) → 𝑓∗ (𝑈) × 𝑓∗ (𝑈′ ) .
                                 𝑉∈𝐵/(𝑈×𝑈′ )

Thus it suffices to show that each of the maps 𝛿𝑈,𝑈′ is (𝑛 − 1)-connective. This follows
from the inductive hypothesis applied to the geometric morphism
                                 𝑿/(𝑓∗ (𝑈)×𝑓∗ (𝑈′ )) → 𝒀/(𝑈×𝑈′ )
whose left exact left adjoint is given by
                             𝑓∗ ∶ 𝒀/(𝑈×𝑈′ ) → 𝑿/(𝑓∗ (𝑈)×𝑓∗ (𝑈′ )) .


                                                      76
    We are finally ready to prove the main result of this section. The following result has
appeared in work of Porta–Yue Yu under the additional assumption that representable
presheaves are already hypersheaves [103, Proposition 2.22]. We learned of the present
proof from Aoki [6, Appendix A].
3.12.11 Proposition. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site and 𝑖 ∶ 𝐵 ↪ 𝐶 a basis for the topology 𝜏.
Then:
(3.12.11.1) If 𝐹 is a 𝜏-hypersheaf on 𝐶, then 𝐹 is the right Kan extension of its restriction
            𝑖∗ 𝐹 to 𝐵.
(3.12.11.2) Right Kan extension defines an equivalence of hypercomplete ∞-topoi
                                                     hyp              hyp
                                          𝑖∗ ∶ Sh𝜏 (𝐵) ⥲ Sh𝜏 (𝐶)

            with inverse given by presheaf restriction 𝑖∗ .
(3.12.11.3) A presheaf 𝐹 ∶ 𝐶op → S is a 𝜏-hypersheaf if and only if 𝑖∗ 𝐹 is a 𝜏|𝐵 -hypersheaf
            on 𝐵 and 𝐹 is the right Kan extension of 𝑖∗ 𝐹.
Proof. Write L𝜏 ∶ PSh(𝐶) → Sh𝜏 (𝐶) for the 𝜏-sheafification functor and よ ∶ 𝐶 ↪ PSh(𝐶)
for the Yoneda embedding.

3.12.13 Corollary. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site and 𝑖 ∶ 𝐵 ↪ 𝐶 a basis for the topology 𝜏.
If Sh𝜏 (𝐶) is hypercomplete, then Sh𝜏 (𝐵) is hypercomplete and the geometric morphism
𝑖∗ ∶ Sh𝜏 (𝐵) ↪ Sh𝜏 (𝐶) is an equivalence.
3.12.14 Corollary. Let (𝐶, 𝜏) be an ∞-site, 𝑖 ∶ 𝐵 ↪ 𝐶 a basis for the topology 𝜏, and
𝑛 ≥ 0 be an integer. If Sh𝜏 (𝐶) and Sh𝜏 (𝐵) are both 𝑛-localic, then the geometric morphism
𝑖∗ ∶ Sh𝜏 (𝐵) ↪ Sh𝜏 (𝐶) is an equivalence.
3.12.15 Example. Let 𝑃 be a poset. From Example 3.12.6 we see that right Kan extension
along the inclusion 𝑃 ⊂ Open(𝑃)op defined by 𝑝 ↦ 𝑃≥𝑝 defines a fully faithful geometric
morphism
                                    Fun(𝑃, S) ↪ 𝑃̃
that identifies Fun(𝑃, S) with the hypercompletion of 𝑃.    ̃
    In particular, if 𝑃 is a finite poset, then 𝑃̃ is already hypercomplete [HTT, Remark
7.2.4.18; 27, Lemma 3.13], so right Kan extension defines an equivalence

                                       Fun(𝑃, S) ⥲ 𝑃̃ .

See [9, Corollary 2.4] for a direct proof of this fact.
3.12.16 Warning. If 𝑃 is an infinite poset, then 𝑃̃ need not be hypercomplete; see [6,
Example A.13] for a counterexample. Equivalently, if 𝑃 is an infinite poset, then the ∞-
topos Fun(𝑃, S) need not be 𝑛-localic for any 𝑛 ≥ 0.
3.12.17 Remark. See [60, Lemma C.3] for another very useful criterion for checking
that the inclusion of a basis induces an equivalence after passage to sheaves of spaces.




                                               78
4     Shape theory
This chapter is dedicated to shape theory for ∞-topoi. The ideas of shape theory for
higher topoi go back to Grothendieck’s famous letter to Breen [45], and were later de-
veloped by Toën–Vezzosi [128; 129, §3.2; 130, §5.3] and Lurie [HTT, §7.1.6; HA, §A.1;
SAG, §E.2]. We refer the reader to these sources for original accounts of the theory.
    Section 4.1 establishes the basic material we’ll need on protruncated objects. Sec-
tion 4.2 recalls the definition of the shape and explains a few basic properties of the
shape; one of the most important of these is that the protruncated shape of an ∞-topos
and its hypercompletion agree. Section 4.3 is devoted to proving that the protruncated
shape commutes with inverse limits of bouded coherent ∞-topoi (Corollary 4.3.7). This
result provides a computational tool for shapes and will be used repeatedly throughout
the text. Section 4.4 explains how to regard profinite spaces as ∞-topoi following [SAG,
Appendix E].

4.1    Protruncated objects
In this section, we recall some facts about protruncated objects that we’ll need through-
out the text. We also record an interesting observation which does not seem to be in the
literature (Lemma 4.1.6).
4.1.1 Notation. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. For each integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, the pro-𝑛-
truncation functor τ≤𝑛 ∶ Pro(𝐶) → Pro(𝐶≤𝑛 ) is the unique extension of the 𝑛-truncation
functor τ≤𝑛 ∶ 𝐶 → 𝐶≤𝑛 to proöbjects that preserves inverse limits.
4.1.2. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. Then the extension to proöbjects of the func-
tor 𝐶 → Pro(𝐶<∞ ) given by sending an object 𝑋 ∈ 𝐶 to the inverse system given by its
Postnikov tower {τ≤𝑛 (𝑋)}𝑛≥−2 is left adjoint to the inclusion Pro(𝐶<∞ ) ↪ Pro(𝐶). We
call this left adjoint
                              τ<∞ ∶ Pro(𝐶) → Pro(𝐶<∞ )
protruncation. A morphism of proöbjects 𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑌, regarded as left exact accessible
functors 𝐶 → S, becomes an equivalence after protuncation if and only if for every trun-
cated object 𝐾 ∈ 𝐶<∞ , the induced morphism 𝑓(𝐾) ∶ 𝑋(𝐾) → 𝑌(𝐾) is an equivalence.
    Since the truncation functors in an ∞-topos preserve finite products [HTT, Lemma
6.5.1.2], if 𝐶 is an ∞-topos, then the protruncation functor τ<∞ also preserves finite
products
4.1.3 Remark. In the terminology of Artin–Mazur [8, Definition 4.2], morphisms in
the ∞-category Pro(S) of prospaces that induce equivalences after protruncation are
precisely those morphisms that become ♮-isomorphisms in the category Pro(h1 S).
4.1.4 Remark. Isaksen’s strict model structure on pro-simplicial sets [70] presents the
∞-category Pro(S) of prospaces [63, Lemma 3.1]. The model structure that Isaksen de-
fines in [68] is the left Bousfield localization of the strict model structure at the τ<∞ -
equivalences, hence presents the ∞-category Pro(S<∞ ) of protruncated spaces [63, Re-
mark 3.2]. The latter model structure is what is often used étale homotopy theory, for
example in the recent work of Schmidt–Stix [115] on the étale homotopy type and an-
abelian geometry.


                                           79
4.1.5. Let 𝐶 be a presentable ∞-category. The unique functor

                                     mat ∶ Pro(𝐶) → 𝐶

that preserves inverse limits and restricts to the identity 𝐶 → 𝐶 is right adjoint to the
Yoneda embedding よ ∶ 𝐶 ↪ Pro(𝐶) [SAG, Example A.8.1.7]. We call mat the material-
ization functor. Hence we have adjunctions

                                ょ                τ<∞
                          𝐶           Pro(𝐶)            Pro(𝐶<∞ ) .
                               mat

     If Postnikov towers converge in 𝐶 (Definition 3.2.11), then the composite left adjoint
is also fully faithful:

4.1.6 Lemma. Let 𝐶 be presentable ∞-category. If Postnikov towers converge in 𝐶, the
protruncation functor
                              τ<∞ ∶ 𝐶 → Pro(𝐶<∞ )
is fully faithful. Moreover, the essential image of τ<∞ ∶ 𝐶 ↪ Pro(𝐶<∞ ) is the full sub-
category spanned by those protruncated objects 𝑋 such that for each integer 𝑛 ≥ −2, the
pro-𝑛-truncation τ≤𝑛 (𝑋) ∈ Pro(𝐶≤𝑛 ) is a constant pröbject.
Proof. It suffices to show that for any object 𝑋 ∈ 𝐶, the unit morphism 𝑋 → mat τ<∞ (𝑋)
is an equivalence. This follows from the equivalence

                               mat τ<∞ (𝑋) ≃ lim τ≤𝑛 (𝑋)
                                                 𝑛≥−2

and the assumption that Postnikov towers converge in 𝐶.
4.1.7. Composing the fully faithful functor τ<∞ ∶ S ↪ Pro(S<∞ ) with the inclusion
Pro(S<∞ ) ↪ Pro(S) gives another embedding of spaces into prospaces, different from
the Yoneda embedding よ ∶ S ↪ Pro(S): for a space 𝐾, the natural morphism of prospaces
よ(𝐾) → τ<∞ (𝐾) is an equivalence if and only if 𝐾 is truncated. Unlike the Yoneda em-
bedding よ, the functor τ<∞ ∶ S ↪ Pro(S) is neither a left nor a right adjoint.

4.2    Shape theory
We now recall the basics of shape theory for ∞-topoi. The shape is crucial to the study
of Stone ∞-topoi presented in § 4.4. Both shape theory and Stone ∞-topoi are key to
our development of the stratified shape in Part III and stratified étale homotopy type in
Part IV.
4.2.1 Definition. The shape Π∞ ∶ Top∞ → Pro(S) is the left adjoint to the extension to
proöbjects of the fully faithful functor S ↪ Top∞ given by

                                 𝛱 ↦ S/𝛱 ≃ Fun(𝛱, S)

[SAG, §E.2.2]. The shape admits two other useful descriptions:



                                            80
(1) Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos, and write Γ𝑿,! ∶ 𝑿 → Pro(S) for the proëxistent left adjoint
    of Γ𝑿∗ ∶ S → 𝑿. The shape of 𝑿 is equivalent to the prospace Γ𝑿,! (1𝑿 ) [HA, Remark
    A.1.10; 62, §2].
(2) As a left exact accessible functor S → S, the prospace Π∞ (𝑿) is the composite Γ∗ Γ∗
    [HTT, §7.1.6; 62, §2]. Under this identification, the shape assigns to a geometric
    morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 with unit 𝑢 ∶ id𝒀 → 𝑓∗ 𝑓∗ the morphism of prospaces cor-
    responding to
                          Γ𝒀,∗ 𝑢Γ𝒀∗ ∶ Γ𝒀,∗ Γ𝒀∗ → Γ𝒀,∗ 𝑓∗ 𝑓∗ Γ𝒀∗ ≃ Γ𝑿,∗ Γ𝑿∗
       in Pro(S)op ⊂ Fun(S, S).
4.2.2. The functor λ ∶ Pro(S) → Top∞ given by extending the fully faithful functor
S ↪ Top∞ to proöbjects is not itself fully faithful.
    For our first example shape computation, please recall that we write ε ∶ Cat∞ → S
for the left adjoint to the inclusion (Notation 2.2.1).
4.2.3 Example. If 𝐶 is a small ∞-category, then Γ∗ ∶ S → Fun(𝐶, S) admits a genuine
left adjoint Γ! ∶ Fun(𝐶, S) → S given by taking the colimit of a diagram 𝐶 → S.25 The
shape of the ∞-topos Fun(𝐶, S) is thus given by the colimit of the constant diagram at
the terminal object of S:

                          Π∞ (Fun(𝐶, S)) = Γ! (1Fun(𝐶,S) ) = colim𝐶 1S ≃ ε(𝐶) .

       Moreover, the functor ε ∶ Cat∞ → S is equivalent to the composite
                                                Fun(−,S)               Π∞
                                     Cat∞                    Top∞           S.

4.2.4 Definition. A geometric morphism 𝑓∗ ∶ 𝑿 → 𝒀 of ∞-topoi is a shape equivalence
if the induced morphism

                                       Π∞ (𝑓∗ ) ∶ Π∞ (𝑿) → Π∞ (𝒀)

is an equivalence in Pro(S). We say that an ∞-topos 𝑿 has trivial shape if Π∞ (𝑿) is a
terminal object of Pro(S).
4.2.5. Work of Hoyois [62, Proposition 2.6] shows that a geometric morphism 𝑓∗ is
a shape equivalence if and only if 𝑓∗ induces an equivalence of ∞-categories of space-
valued torsors.
4.2.6 Warning. The pullback (in Top∞ ) of a shape equivalence is not generally a shape
equivalence, even when both morphisms are shape equivalences. As an example, con-
sider the space 𝑋 ≔ [0, 1], and its closed subspace 𝑍 ≔ {0} and open complement
                              ̃ 𝑈,
𝑈 ≔ (0, 1]. Then the ∞-topoi 𝑋,  ̃ and 𝑍 ̃ all have trivial shape and the natural inclu-
sions
                            𝑍̃↪ 𝑋 ̃      and      ̃↪𝑋
                                                  𝑈      ̃
are both shape equivalences [HA, Example A.4.5], however the pullback 𝑍   ̃ ×𝑋̃ 𝑈
                                                                                ̃ is the
                ̃
initial ∞-topos ∅, which has empty shape.
  25
       That is to say, presheaf ∞-topoi are locally of constant shape [HA, Definition A.1.5 & Proposition A.1.8].


                                                        81
4.2.7 Notation. Let 𝑛 ≥ −2 be an integer. We write

                               Π𝑛 ≔ τ≤𝑛 ∘ Π∞ ∶ Top∞ → Pro(S≤𝑛 )

for the pro-𝑛-truncated shape (Notation 4.1.1). We write

                            Π<∞ ≔ τ<∞ ∘ Π∞ ∶ Top∞ → Pro(S<∞ )

for the protruncated shape (4.1.2).
4.2.8 Example. Since truncated objects of an ∞-topos are hypercomplete, for any ∞-
topos 𝑿, the natural geometric morphism 𝑿hyp ↪ 𝑿 induces an equivalence

                                      Π<∞ (𝑿hyp ) ⥲ Π<∞ (𝑿)

on protruncated shapes.

4.3       Shapes of inverse limits
This section is dedicated to proving that the protruncated shape preserves limits of in-
verse systems of bounded coherent ∞-topoi and coherent geometric morphisms (Corol-
lary 4.3.7).26 This follows from the more general fact that the protruncated shape pre-
serves limits of inverse systems of ∞-topoi and geometric morphisms in which the push-
forward preserve filtered colimits of uniformly truncated objects. We learned this from
Chough [25, §3]; though Chough’s paper only states this for the profinite shape, his
proof works for the protruncated shape.
    We first fix some useful notation for the next few results. Please also recall Defini-
tion 3.10.2.

4.3.1 Notation. Let 𝑿 ∶ 𝐼 → Top∞ be an inverse diagram of ∞-topoi. For each mor-
phism 𝛼 ∶ 𝑗 → 𝑖 in 𝐼, we write
                                𝑓𝛼,∗ ∶ 𝑿𝑗 → 𝑿𝑖
for the transition morphism. For each 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼, we write

                                          𝜋𝑖,∗ ∶ lim 𝑿𝑖 → 𝑿𝑖
                                                    𝑖∈𝐼

for the projection. In addition, assume for each morphism 𝛼 ∶ 𝑗 → 𝑖 the functor 𝑓𝛼,∗
almost preserves filtered colimits.

object 𝑈 ∈ 𝑿<∞ we have

4.3.6. In particular, the assumptions of Proposition 4.3.5 are satisfied for inverse systems
