12.2.3 Example (knots and primes). Let 𝐾 be a number field, and write O𝐾 ⊂ 𝐾 for
the ring of integers of 𝐾. The profinite Galois category Gal(Spec O𝐾 ) has (isomorphism
classes of) objects the prime ideals of O𝐾 . For each nonzero prime ideal p ∈ Spec O𝐾 ,
the automorphisms of p can be identified with the absolute Galois group Gκ(p) of the
finite field κ(p). Thus the profinite étale shape of Spec O𝐾 is stratified by the various
closed strata, each of which is an embedded profinite circle – i.e., a knot BGκ(p) ≃ B𝐙.̂
The open complement of each BGκ(p) is the profinite classifying space of the profinite
group
                                 Gp ≔ π̂ét
                                         1 (Spec(O𝐾 ) ∖ p) .

Note that Gp is the automorphism group of the maximal Galois extension of 𝐾 that
is ramified at most only at p and the infinite primes. Enveloping each knot is a tubu-
lar neighborhood, given by Gal(Spec 𝐴shp ), so that the deleted tubular neighborhood of
BGκ(p) is a BG𝐾p .


                                             187
12.3         Sieves & cosieves of Galois categories
One can read off various facts about schemes from their Galois categories. In this sec-
tion and the next, we begin to propose a dictionary between schemes and their profi-
nite Galois categories.35 We continue this endeavour in Chapter 14, as the dictionary
is strongest between profinite Galois categories and schemes that admit no nontrivial
universal homeomorphisms (Definition 14.2.2).
     The following is immediate.
12.3.1 Proposition. A monomorphism 𝑈 ↪ 𝑋 of coherent schemes is an open immersion
if and only if the induced functor Gal(𝑈) → Gal(𝑋) is equivalent to the inclusion of a
cosieve.
    Dually, a monomorphism 𝑍 ↪ 𝑋 of coherent schemes is a closed immersion if and
only if Gal(𝑍) → Gal(𝑋) is equivalent to the inclusion of a sieve.
12.3.2 Recollection. An interval in an ∞-category 𝐶 is a full subcategory 𝐷 ⊆ 𝐶 such
that a morphism 𝑑 → 𝑑′ of 𝐷 factors through an object 𝑐 of 𝐶 only if 𝑐 lies in 𝐷.
12.3.3 Corollary. A monomorphism 𝑊 ↪ 𝑋 of coherent schemes is a locally closed im-
mersion if and only if the induced functor Gal(𝑊) → Gal(𝑋) is equivalent to the inclusion
of an interval.
12.3.4 Recollection. Let 𝐶 be an ∞-category. An object 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶 is weakly initial if for
every object 𝑐′ ∈ 𝐶, the space Map𝐶 (𝑐, 𝑐′ ) is nonempty. An object 𝑐′ ∈ 𝐶 is weakly
terminal if 𝑐 is weakly initial in 𝐶op .
12.3.5 Corollary. A coherent scheme 𝑋 is local if and only if Gal(𝑋) contains a weakly
initial object. Dually, a coherent scheme 𝑋 is irreducible if and only if Gal(𝑋) contains a
weakly terminal object.

12.3.6. For any coherent scheme 𝑋 and any point 𝑥0 ∈ 𝑋zar , the Galois category of the
localization is the fiber product

                                       Gal(𝑋(𝑥0 ) ) ≃ Gal(𝑋) ×zar 𝑋𝑥zar
                                                                     0/
                                                                        .
                                                                  𝑋

Dually, for any point 𝑦0 ∈ 𝑋zar , the Galois category of the closure 𝑋(𝑦0 ) of 𝑦0 (with the
reduced subscheme structure, say) is the fiber product

                                       Gal(𝑋(𝑦0 ) ) ≃ Gal(𝑋) ×zar 𝑋/𝑦
                                                                   zar
                                                                     0
                                                                       .
                                                                  𝑋


12.4         Undercategories & overcategories of Galois categories
In this section we extend our dictionary by showing that undercategories correspond to
localizations, while overcategories correspond to normalizations (Corollary 12.4.5).
12.4.1 Notation (strict localization). Let 𝑋 be a scheme and 𝑥 → 𝑋 a point of 𝑋. We
write 𝑥0 ∈ 𝑋zar for the image of 𝑥 in the Zariski topological space of 𝑋 Let κ(𝑥0 )sep ⊇ κ(𝑥)
  35
       This dictionary first appeared in a preprint of the first-named author [13].


                                                       188
denote the separable closure of κ(𝑥0 ) in κ(𝑥). We write Oh𝑋,𝑥0 for the henselization of the
local ring O𝑋,𝑥0 , and write
                                       Oh𝑋,𝑥 ⊇ Oh𝑋,𝑥0
for the unique extension of henselian local rings that on residue fields reduces to the
field extension κ(𝑥0 )sep ⊇ κ(𝑥0 ). We also write

                                    𝑋(𝑥) ≔ Spec(Oh𝑋,𝑥 ) .

We call 𝑋(𝑥) the localization of 𝑋 at 𝑥 (Example 6.7.4). The scheme 𝑋(𝑥) is the limit of
the diagram of étale 𝑋-schemes 𝑈 → 𝑋 equipped with a lift 𝑥 → 𝑈 of 𝑥 → 𝑋 to 𝑈.
     If 𝑥 → 𝑋 is a geometric point, then Oh𝑋,𝑥 is the strict henselization of O𝑋,𝑥0 , and 𝑋(𝑥)
is the strict localization of 𝑋 at 𝑥.
12.4.2 Notation (strict normalization). Let 𝑋 be a scheme, let 𝑦 → 𝑋 is a geometric
point, and let κ(𝑦)alg ⊃ κ(𝑦) be an algebraic closure of κ(𝑦). We write 𝑋(𝑦0 ) ⊂ 𝑋 for the
integral subscheme defined by the Zariski closure of {𝑦0 } with the reduced subscheme
structure. We write 𝑋(𝑦) for the normalization of 𝑋(𝑦0 ) under Spec κ(𝑦)alg . We call 𝑋(𝑦)
the strict normalization of 𝑋 at 𝑦.
    The strict normalization 𝑋(𝑦) can be expressed as an inverse limit of schemes over
  (𝑦0 )
𝑋       as follows. For each intermediate field extension κ(𝑦0 ) ⊂ 𝑘 ⊂ κ(𝑦)alg finite over
κ(𝑦0 ), write 𝑋(𝑦0 ),𝑘 → 𝑋(𝑦0 ) for the normalization of 𝑋(𝑦0 ) under Spec 𝑘. Then 𝑋(𝑦) is
the inverse limit
                                 𝑋(𝑦) ⥲      lim alg 𝑋(𝑦0 ),𝑘
                                         κ(𝑦0 )⊂𝑘⊂κ(𝑦)

of the resulting diagram of normalizations and finite surjective transition morphisms
[121, §3].
12.4.3 Remark. Absolutely integrally closed schemes are integral normal schemes whose
function field is algebraically closed. In other words, an absolutely integrally closed
scheme is a strict normalization 𝑋(𝑦) of a scheme at a geometric point 𝑦 → 𝑋 [7]. This
class of schemes has a number of curious properties:
    → If 𝑍 is absolutely integrally closed, then for any point 𝑧0 ∈ 𝑍zar , the local ring
      O𝑍,𝑧0 is strictly henselian [121, Proposition 2.6].
    → If 𝑍 is absolutely integrally closed, then the étale topos and the Zariski topos of 𝑍
      coincide [121, Corollary 2.5]. Hence Gal(𝑍) ≃ 𝑍zar . In other words, Gal(𝑍) is a
      profinite poset with a terminal object.
    → If 𝑍 is absolutely integrally closed and 𝑊 is irreducible, then any integral mor-
      phism 𝑊 → 𝑍 is radicial [121, Lemma 2.3]. Thus any integral surjection 𝑊 → 𝑍
      is a universal homeomorphism (Recollection 14.1.1).
    → If 𝑍 is absolutely integrally closed, then the poset Gal(𝑍) ≃ 𝑍zar has all finite
      nonempty joins [122, Theorem 2.1].
    Here now is the basic observation, which follows more or less immediately from the
limit descriptions of the strict localization and the strict normalization:


                                             189
12.4.4 Proposition. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme, and let 𝑥alg → 𝑋 and 𝑦alg → 𝑋 be
points of 𝑋 that correspond to algebraic closures of the residue fields of their image points.
Write 𝑥 → 𝑋 and 𝑦 → 𝑌 for the underlying geometric points of 𝑥alg → 𝑋 and 𝑦alg → 𝑋,
respectively. Then the following profinite sets are in (canonical) bijection:
    → The profinite set MapGal(𝑋) (𝑥, 𝑦) of morphisms 𝑥 → 𝑦 in Gal(𝑋).

    → The profinite set Mor𝑋 (𝑦, 𝑋(𝑥) ) of lifts of 𝑦 to the strict localization 𝑋(𝑥) .

    → The profinite set Mor𝑋 (𝑥alg , 𝑋(𝑦) ) of lifts of 𝑥 to the strict normalization 𝑋(𝑦) .
We may thus describe the over- and undercategories of Galois categories. The expression
of the undercategory in terms of the strict localization is originally due to Grothendieck
[SGA 4ii , Exposé VIII, Corollaire 7.6].

12.4.5 Corollary. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme, and let 𝑥 → 𝑋 and 𝑦 → 𝑋 be geometric
points of 𝑋. Then we have

                Gal(𝑋)𝑥/ ≃ Gal(𝑋(𝑥) )         and       Gal(𝑋)/𝑦 ≃ Gal(𝑋(𝑦) ) .

12.4.6 Corollary. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. Then Gal(𝑋) is equivalent to both of the
following full subcategories of 𝑋-schemes:
    → The full subcategory spanned by the strict localizations of 𝑋.
    → The full subcategory spanned by the strict normalizations of 𝑋.

Since Gal(𝑋(𝑦) ) ≃ 𝑋(𝑦),zar , it follows that Galois categories are of a very particular sort:
12.4.7 Corollary. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. For any geometric point 𝑦 → 𝑋, the over-
category Gal(𝑋)/𝑦 is a profinite poset with all finite nonempty joins. In particular, every
morphism of Gal(𝑋) is a monomorphism.

12.5     Recovering the protruncated étale shape
Since Gal(𝑋) is the profinite stratified shape of the spectral ∞-topos 𝑋ét , the fact that the
profinite stratified shape is a delocalization of the protruncated shape (Theorem 10.2.3)
immediately implies the following:
12.5.1 Theorem (Homotopy). Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. Then there is a natural natural
map of prospaces
                           𝜃𝑋 ∶ Πét
                                 ∞ (𝑋) → ε(Gal(𝑋)) .

Moreover, 𝜃𝑋 induces an equivalence on protruncations.
    As a consequence, for each integer 𝑛 ≥ 1 and geometric point 𝑥 → 𝑋, there is a natural
isomorphisms of progroups

                                πét
                                 𝑛 (𝑋, 𝑥) ⥲ π𝑛 (ε(Gal(𝑋)), 𝑥) .

Here πét                                                         ét
      𝑛 (𝑋, 𝑥) is the 𝑛-th homotopy progroup of the étale shape Π∞ (𝑋) of 𝑋 (11.1.7).



                                              190
12.6    Stratified Riemann Existence Theorem
We prove a stratified refinement of the Riemann Existence Theorem of Artin–Mazur–
Friedlander (Theorem 11.5.3), giving a comparison between étale and analytic stratified
homotopy types for schemes of finite type over the field 𝐂 of complex numbers (Corol-
lary 12.6.6).
12.6.1 Construction. Let 𝑋 be a scheme of finite type over 𝐂. Let us recall the geometric
morphism 𝜀∗ ∶ 𝑋an → 𝑋ét of Recollection 11.5.2. We give 𝑋an the profinite stratification
𝑋an → 𝑋zar of Example 1.4.4. With respect to this stratification, 𝜀∗ is an 𝑋zar -stratified
geometric morphism.
   Let 𝑆 be a spectral topological space and let 𝑋zar → 𝑆 be a quasicompact continuous
map. We write
                               𝑆-cons
             (𝑋/𝑆)an ≔ Sheff (𝑋an     )     and      (𝑋/𝑆)ét ≔ Sheff (𝑋ét𝑆-cons )

for the 𝑆-spectrifications of 𝑋an and 𝑋ét , respectively. Since the geometric morphism
𝜀∗ ∶ 𝑋an → 𝑋ét is 𝑋zar -stratified, the pullback functor 𝜀∗ restricts to a morphism of
∞-pretopoi
                                   𝑋ét𝑆-cons → 𝑋an
                                                 𝑆-cons

(Lemma 9.4.9), hence induces an 𝑆-stratified geometric morphism

                                 𝜀∗ ∶ (𝑋/𝑆)an → (𝑋/𝑆)ét .
                    𝑆-cons
   The inclusions 𝑋an      ↪ 𝑋an and 𝑋ét𝑆-cons ↪ 𝑋ét induce natural geometric mor-
phisms
              𝑣𝑆,an
               ∗ ∶ 𝑋an → (𝑋/𝑆)an      and       𝑣𝑆,ét
                                                 ∗ ∶ 𝑋ét → (𝑋/𝑆)ét

that exhibit (𝑋/𝑆)an and (𝑋/𝑆)ét as the 𝑆-spectrifications of 𝑋an and 𝑋ét . These functors
are compatible with 𝜀 in the sense that the squares
                                             𝜀∗
                                    𝑋an             𝑋ét
                                 𝑣𝑆,an
                                  ∗                    𝑣𝑆,ét
                                                        ∗

commute, by construction.
12.6.2. Let 𝑋zar → 𝑃 be a finite constructible stratification. Then the topological space
𝑋an also inherits a stratification 𝑋an → 𝑃, which is conical. The Exodromy Equivalence
for stratified topological spaces (Example 8.4.2), provides an equivalence

                             Exit𝑃 (𝑋an )∧π ≃ Π
                                              ̂(∞,1)
                                                𝑃
                                                     ((𝑋/𝑃)an )

between the profinite completion (in the stratified sense) of the exit-path ∞-category
of 𝑋an and the profinite stratified shape of (𝑋/𝑃)an .
    We now discuss a general nonabelian form of the results identified in [16, 6.1.2].
The key result, which seems to be well-known to experts, is the following ‘nonabelian
derived’ form of the Artin Comparison Theorem [SGA 4iii , Exposé XVI, Théorème 4.1].


                                            191
12.6.3 Theorem. Let 𝑓 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑌 be a morphism of finite type 𝐂-schemes. Then the square
                                                           𝜀∗
                                    (𝑋/𝑋zar )an                    𝑋ét
                                          𝑓∗an                            𝑓∗ét

                                    (𝑌/𝑌zar )an            𝜀∗      𝑌ét

of ∞-topoi satisfies the left basechange condition when restricted to constructible sheaves
on 𝑋ét . That is, for any constructible sheaf 𝐹 ∈ 𝑋ét , the natural basechange morphism

                                          𝜀∗ 𝑓∗ét 𝐹 → 𝑓∗an 𝜀∗ 𝐹

is an equivalence.
12.6.4 Proposition. Let 𝑋 be a scheme of finite type over 𝐂. Then the pullback functor
𝜀∗ ∶ 𝑋ét → 𝑋an restricts to an equivalence on constructible sheaves.
Proof. We induct on the Krull dimension of 𝑋; the claim in obvious for dimension 0.
   Let us assume that 𝑋 is of dimension 𝑛, and that the claim is known for schemes of
dimension < 𝑛. We may also assume that 𝑋 is irreducible. In this case, we may write
𝑋zar as the limit of stratifications of the form 𝑋zar → 𝑆, where

                                𝑆 = 𝑍zar ∪ {∞} ≃ lim 𝑃▹
                                                            𝑃∈FC(𝑍)

for some closed subscheme 𝑍 ⊂ 𝑋 of dimension < 𝑛. Hence it will suffice to show that
for any such stratification 𝑋zar → 𝑆, the 𝑆-stratified geometric morphism

                                 𝜀∗ ∶ (𝑋/𝑆)an → (𝑋/𝑆)ét

is an equivalence. The source is a recollement of (𝑍/𝑍zar )an and (𝑈/∞)an :

                                           𝑖an                     𝑗∗an
                      (𝑍/𝑍zar )an           ∗
                                                    (𝑋/𝑆)an                      (𝑈/∞)an .

Accordingly, the target is a recollement of 𝑍ét and (𝑈/∞)ét :

                                    𝑖ét
                                     ∗                      𝑗∗ét
                          𝑍ét                    (𝑋/𝑆)ét             (𝑈/∞)ét .

Restricted to the closed piece of this recollement, 𝜀∗ is an equivalence by the induction
hypothesis. Restricted to the open piece, 𝜀∗ is an equivalence by the unstratified Riemann
Existence Theorem (Theorem 11.5.3).
    It therefore suffices to prove that the gluing functors agree under these equivalences.
In other words, it suffices to prove that for any lisse sheaf 𝐹 on 𝑈ét , the morphism

                                     𝜀∗ 𝑖∗ét 𝑗∗ét 𝐹 → 𝑖∗an 𝑗∗an 𝜀∗ 𝐹

is an equivalence (Proposition 5.1.16). Now Theorem 12.6.3 applies, completing the
proof.

for the morphism of profinite 𝑋zar -stratified spaces induced by the geometric morphism

12.6.6 Corollary (Stratified Riemann Existence). Let 𝑋 be a scheme of finite type over 𝐂.
Then the natural morphism 𝜀 ∶ Galan (𝑋) → Gal(𝑋) is an equivalence.
    As a corollary, we also obtain:

12.6.7 Corollary. Let 𝑋 be a scheme of finite type over 𝐂 and let 𝑋zar → 𝑃 be a finite
constructible stratification. Then the natural functor

                                 Exit𝑃 (𝑋an ) → Gal(𝑋/𝑃)

exhibits the profinite stratified space Gal(𝑋/𝑃) as the profinite completion of the exit-path
∞-category of 𝑋an → 𝑃.

    Cox [28; 29] also developed a deleted tubular neighborhood for schemes, which
is what appears in Friedlander’s formulation of the van Kampen Theorem. It can be
shown that Cox’s deleted tubular neighborhood and our toposic version have equivalent
protruncated shapes types.




                                            194
13 Extending exodromy: coefficients, stacks, & ℓ-adic
   sheaves
Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. One limitation of our study of constructible sheaves thus
far is that we have effectively restricted our attention to constructible sheaves on 𝑋 with
nonabelian, π-finite coefficients. The first goal of this chapter is to use Theorem 10.1.8
along with some basic facts about the compactness of certain ∞-categories to extend our
Exodromy Theorem to coefficients in a finite ring. Specifically, for any finite ring 𝑅, we
provide an equivalence between the constructible derived ∞-category of étale sheaves
of 𝑅-modules on 𝑋 and the ∞-category of continuous representations of the profinite
1-category Gal(𝑋) with values in Perf(𝑅):
13.0.1 Theorem. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme and let 𝑅 be a finite ring. Then there is an
equivalence of ∞-categories

(13.0.2)                    Dcons (𝑋ét ; 𝑅) ≃ Fun(Gal(𝑋), Perf(𝑅)) .

See Theorem 13.2.11 for an even more general statement.
    However, many of the serious applications of constructible sheaves and their coho-
mology arise with coefficients a more general topological ring, such as 𝐙ℓ , 𝐐ℓ , or 𝐐ℓ .
Consequently, it is desirable to extend our Exodromy Theorem to include these topo-
logical rings. To do this, we have to solve two problems:

(1) Given a topological ring 𝛬, it is not a priori clear how to incorporate the topology
    on 𝛬 in the ∞-category Perf(𝛬). We address this by introducing pyknotic structures
    – called condensed structures by Clausen and Scholze. Pyknotic structures general-
    ize common topological structures in a way that plays well with algebraic structures.
    Using this formalism, if 𝐶 and 𝐷 are pyknotic ∞-categories, we can meaningfully
    speak of the the ∞-category Functs (𝐶, 𝐷) of continuous functors 𝐶 → 𝐷. Criti-
    cally, this extends gives us a way of extending the right-hand side of the equivalence
    (13.0.2) to topological rings; see Lemma 13.6.1.
(2) Constructible sheaves of 𝛬-modules are not generally sheaves for the étale topology,
    but the proétale topology of Bhatt and Scholze [17]. One expects a version of our
    Exodromy Theorem for ∞-topoi that closely resemble the proétale ∞-topos of a
    scheme; we have not been able to obtain such a result. Instead, we do something
    more modest: we identify situations in which the theorem can be extended along
    limits and filtered colimits.

       The formulation and proof of the following pair of results occupies most of this chap-
ter.
13.0.3 Notation. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme.
       → Let 𝛬 be a noetherian ring that is complete with respect to the topology defined by
         an ideal 𝐼 ⊂ 𝛬. We write Dcons (𝑋proét ; 𝛬) for the constructible derived ∞-category
         of proétale sheaves of 𝛬-modules on 𝑋 [17, Definition 6.5.1].


                                              195
    → Let ℓ be a prime number and 𝐸 an algebraic extension of 𝐐ℓ . If 𝑋 is topologically
      noetherian (thus automatically coherent), we write Dcons (𝑋proét ; 𝐸) for the con-
      structible derived ∞-category of proétale sheaves of 𝐸-modules on 𝑋 [17, Defi-
      nition 6.8.8].
13.0.4 Theorem. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme, 𝛬 be a noetherian ring, and 𝐼 ⊂ 𝛬 an ideal.
Assume that 𝛬 is complete with respect to the 𝐼-adic topology and that for each integer
𝑛 ≥ 1, the quotient ring 𝛬/𝐼𝑛 is finite. Then there is an equivalence of ∞-categories

                        Dcons (𝑋proét ; 𝛬) ≃ Functs (Gal(𝑋), Perf(𝛬)) .

   For topologically noetherian schemes, we extend Theorem 13.0.4 to coefficients in
𝐐ℓ or 𝐐ℓ :
13.0.5 Theorem. Let 𝑋 be a topologically noetherian scheme, ℓ be a prime number, and
𝐸 be an algebraic field extension of 𝐐ℓ . Then there is an equivalence of ∞-categories

                        Dcons (𝑋proét ; 𝐸) ≃ Functs (Gal(𝑋), Perf(𝐸)) .

13.0.6 Remark. We need 𝑋 to be topologically noetherian in order to ensure that the
standard notion of a constructible complex of 𝐐ℓ -sheaves is equivalent to the require-
ment that the sheaf is lisse over a finte stratification (see [17, §6.6]).
    At end end of the chapter, we also explain how to extend these Exodromy Equiva-
lences to a large class of stacks.
    Section 13.1 sets up the background about category objects in ∞-topoi that we need
to prove our extensions of the Exodromy Equivalence. Section 13.2 uses the compact-
ness results of category objects that we prove in § 13.1 to prove Theorem 13.0.1. Sec-
tion 13.3 sets up the basics of pyknotic spaces and ∞-categories and explains what we
mean by ‘Functs ’. Armed with this, in Sections 13.4 and 13.5 we explain how to embed
profinite spaces into pyknotic spaces, and profinite stratified spaces into pyknotic ∞-
categories. Section 13.6 shows that functors out of Gal(𝑋) in the ‘pro’ sense agree with
continuous functors in the pyknotic sense. This gives a reinterpretation of the Exodromy
Theorem with finite coefficients in terms of the pyknotic formalism. With all of this in
place, in Section 13.7 we explain how the Exodromy Theorem with profinite coefficients
(Theorem 13.0.4) follows from Exodromy with finite coefficients (see Theorem 13.7.8).
Section 13.8 is dedicated to extending Exodromy with profinite coefficients to coeffi-
cients in an algebraic extension of 𝐐ℓ (Theorem 13.0.5); this step is not entirely formal
and involves an analysis of t-structures in the pyknotic world (see Theorem 13.8.8). Sec-
tion 13.9 ends the chapter by extending the Exodromy Theorem to a large class of stacks
(Proposition 13.9.17).

13.1     Category objects of higher topoi
In preparation for the results of this chapter, it is necessary to develop a little background
on the (co)compactness of category objects in ∞-topoi. First, the cocompactness results
that we prove in this section are what allow us to extend the Exodromy Theorem to
coefficients in a finite discrete ring (Theorem 13.0.1). Second, the compactness results


                                             196
that we prove are what allow us to regard Gal(𝑋) as a pyknotic ∞-category and extend
the coefficients in the Exodromy Theorem from a finite ring to more general topological
rings.
    In order to prove the relevant (co)compactness results, it is technically convenient to
use the formalism of complete Segal objects. The reason for this is that we can often formu-
late criteria for (co)compactness of simplicial objects in terms of the (co)compactness
of their simplices; see Propositions 13.1.12 and 13.1.14.
13.1.1 Definition. Let 𝐷 be an ∞-category with finite limits. We say that a simplicial
object 𝐹 ∶ 𝚫op → 𝐷 is a category object of 𝐷 if 𝐹 satisfies the Segal condition:
(13.1.1.1) For every integer 𝑘 ≥ 1, the natural morphism
                        𝐹𝑘 → 𝐹{0 < 1} × 𝐹{1 < 2} × ⋯ × 𝐹{𝑘 − 1 < 𝑘}
                                        𝐹{1}             𝐹{2}    𝐹{𝑘−1}

           is an equivalence in 𝐷.
We say that a category object 𝐹 ∶ 𝚫op → 𝐷 is a complete Segal object if, in addition, 𝐹
satisfies the following completeness condition:
(13.1.1.2) The natural morphism
                                       𝐹0 → 𝐹3            ×          𝐹1
                                                     𝐹{0<2}×𝐹{1<3}

           is an equivalence in 𝐷.
   Write CO(𝐷) ⊂ Fun(𝚫op , 𝐷) for the full subcategory spanned by the category objects
and CS(𝐷) ⊂ CO(𝐷) for the full subcategory spanned by the complete Segal objects.
13.1.2. Joyal and Tierney [77] showed that the nerve construction defines an equiva-
lence
                                 N ∶ Cat∞ ⥲ CS(S)
from the ∞-category of ∞-categories to the ∞-category of complete Segal spaces. For
each integer 𝑛 ≥ 0, the nerve restricts to an equivalence
                                  N ∶ Cat𝑛 ⥲ CS(S≤𝑛−1 )
between 𝑛-categories and complete Segal objects in (𝑛 − 1)-truncated spaces.
13.1.3. See [SAG, §A.8.2] for more on category objects.
13.1.4. Let 𝐷 be an ∞-category with finite limits. Then the full subcategories
                             CS(𝐷) ⊂ CO(𝐷) ⊂ Fun(𝚫op , 𝐷)
are closed under all limits that exist in 𝐷.
13.1.5. Let 𝑿 be ∞-topos. Since finite limits commute with filtered colimits in ∞-topoi,
the full subcategories
                           CS(𝑿) ⊂ CO(𝑿) ⊂ Fun(𝚫op , 𝑿)
are closed under limits and filtered colimits. In particular CS(𝑿) and CO(𝑿) are pre-
sentable and the incluions into Fun(𝚫op , 𝑿) admit left adjoints.


                                               197
    For future use, we’ll record a few facts about the interaction between ∞-categories
of product-preserving functors and complete Segal objects. We later use this to see that
formation of pyknotic objects and complete Segal objects commute. All are immediate
from the definitions.
13.1.6 Notation. Let 𝐵 and 𝐷 be ∞-categories with finite products. We write

                                   Fun× (𝐵, 𝐷) ⊂ Fun(𝐵, 𝐷)

for the full subcategory spanned by the functors that preserve finite products.
13.1.7 Lemma. Let 𝐵, 𝐶, and 𝐷 be ∞-categories, and assume that 𝐵 and 𝐷 have finite
products. Then the natural equivalence of ∞-categories

                            Fun(𝐵, Fun(𝐶, 𝐷)) ≃ Fun(𝐶, Fun(𝐵, 𝐷))

restricts to an equivalence

                           Fun× (𝐵, Fun(𝐶, 𝐷)) ≃ Fun(𝐶, Fun× (𝐵, 𝐷)) .

13.1.8 Corollary. Let 𝐵 be an ∞-category with products and 𝐷 an ∞-category with finite
limits. Then the natural equivalence of ∞-categories

                      Fun× (𝐵, Fun(𝚫op , 𝐷)) ≃ Fun(𝚫op , Fun× (𝐵, 𝐷))

restrict to equivalences

Fun× (𝐵, CO(𝐷)) ≃ CO(Fun× (𝐵, 𝐷))             and        Fun× (𝐵, CS(𝐷)) ≃ CS(Fun× (𝐵, 𝐷)) .

    We now prove the relevant cocompactness result that we use in § 13.5 in order to
see that profinite stratified spaces embed into pyknotic ∞-categories. The main result
we need is that category objects in π-finite spaces are cocompact as category objects
in profinite spaces. We show this by combining the fact that Sπ ⊂ S∧π consists of co-
compact objects with the fact that a category object in truncated spaces is always right
Kan-extended from a finite subcategory of 𝚫op .
13.1.9 Notation. Let 𝑚 be a positive integer. We write 𝚫≤𝑚 ⊂ 𝚫 for the full subcategory
spanned by the objects [0], [1], … , [𝑚].
13.1.10 Definition. Let 𝐷 be an ∞-category with finite limits and 𝑚 ≥ 1 an integer. An
                                                  op
𝑚-skeletal category object of 𝐷 is a functor 𝐹 ∶ 𝚫≤𝑚 → 𝐷 satisfying the Segal condition:
for every integer 0 ≤ 𝑘 ≤ 𝑚, the natural morphism

                  𝐹𝑘 → 𝐹{0 < 1} × 𝐹{1 < 2} × ⋯ × 𝐹{𝑘 − 1 < 𝑘}
                                    𝐹{1}         𝐹{2}    𝐹{𝑘−1}

is an equivalence in 𝐷. We write
                                                        op
                                   CO≤𝑚 (𝐷) ⊂ Fun(𝚫≤𝑚 , 𝐷)

for the full subcategory spanned by the 𝑚-skeletal category objects.


                                              198
13.1.11. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝑚 ≥ 1 an integer. Then the restriction functor

                                (−)|𝚫op≤𝑚 ∶ CO(𝑿) → CO≤𝑚 (𝑿)

commutes with all limits and filtered colimits.

13.1.12 Proposition. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-category with finite limits, 𝑛 ≥ −2 an integer, and
𝐶 ∈ CO(𝑿) a category object of 𝑿. If the natural map

                                   𝐶{0 < 1} → 𝐶{0} × 𝐶{1}

is 𝑛-truncated and for each 𝐼 ∈ 𝚫, the object 𝐶(𝐼) of 𝑿 is cocompact, then 𝐶 is a cocompact
object of CO(𝑿).

Proof of Proposition 13.1.14. Let 𝐷 ∶ 𝐴 → CO(𝑿) be a cofiltered diagram. By [SAG,
                                                               op
Proposition A.8.2.6], the object 𝐶 is right Kan extended from 𝚫≤𝑛+2 , hence we see that

             colim
                 op
                    MapCO(𝑿) (𝐷𝛼 , 𝐶) ≃ colim
                                            op
                                               MapCO≤𝑛+2 (𝑿) (𝐷𝛼 |𝚫op≤𝑛+2 , 𝐶|𝚫op≤𝑛+2 ) .
             𝛼∈𝐴                            𝛼∈𝐴

13.1.13 Corollary. Every object in the image of the Yoneda embedding CO(Sπ ) ↪ CO(S∧π )
is cocompact.
    We now turn to the compactness of category objects. The formal manipulations in
the proof of the following proposition are very similar to those used in the proof of
Proposition 13.1.12.

13.1.14 Proposition. Let 𝑿 be an ∞-topos and 𝐶 ∈ CO(𝑿) a category object of 𝑿. If
for each 𝐼 ∈ 𝚫, the object 𝐶(𝐼) is almost compact (Definition 3.10.2), then for each integer
𝑛 ≥ −2 the functor
                             MapCO(𝑿) (𝐶, −) ∶ CO(𝑿≤𝑛 ) → S
preserves filtered colimits.
13.1.15. The hypotheses of Proposition 13.1.14 are satisfied if 𝑿 is a locally coherent
∞-topos and for each 𝐼 ∈ 𝚫, the object 𝐶(𝐼) is coherent [SAG, Corollary A.2.3.2].



                                                199

13.2    Exodromy with discrete coefficients
Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme. The original formulation of the Exodromy Theorem for
schemes says that functors Gal(𝑋) → Sπ are the same things as constructible sheaves of
spaces on 𝑋 (Construction 12.1.5). We now prove the analagous claim where Sπ is re-
placed by the ∞-category of perfect complexes over a finite ring 𝑅. The important prop-
erty shared by Sπ and Perf(𝑅) that allows us to reduce the claim to Proposition 13.1.12
is that all of the mapping spaces in these ∞-categories are π-finite.
13.2.1 Definition. We say that an ∞-category 𝐶 is locally π-finite if for all objects 𝑋, 𝑌 ∈
𝐶, the mapping space Map𝐶 (𝑋, 𝑌) is π-finite. We say that a locally π-finite ∞-category
𝐶 is π-finite if 𝐶 has finitely many objects up to equivalence.
13.2.2 Examples.
    → The ∞-category Sπ of π-finite spaces is locally π-finite [SAG, Remark E.2.6.4].
    → For any finite ring 𝑅, the ∞-category Perf(𝑅) of perfect complexes over 𝑅 is locally
      π-finite.
    → If 𝛱 is a π-finite stratified space, then the ∞-category 𝛱 is π-finite
13.2.3 Notation. Let 𝐶 be a locally π-finite ∞-category. We denote by Subπ (𝐶) the
filtered poset of π-finite full subcategories of 𝐶. Note that 𝐶 is the filtered union

                                      𝐶 = colim 𝐶0 .
                                           𝐶0 ∈Subπ (𝐶)


                                             200
13.2.4. Note that if 𝜫 is a profinite stratified space and 𝐶 is a locally π-finite ∞-catego-
ry, then any functor 𝜫 → 𝐶 lands in a π-finite full subcategory of 𝐶. This is because the
profinite set π0 (ι𝜫) is quasicompact, so its image in the discrete set π0 (ι𝐶) is finite. In
particular, the natural functor

                             colim Fun(𝜫, 𝐶0 ) → Fun(𝜫, 𝐶)
                           𝐶0 ∈Subπ (𝐶)

is an equivalence.

    We now introduce the notion of a constructible sheaf with values in a general ∞-
category.
13.2.5 Recollection. Let 𝒀 be an ∞-topos and 𝐷 a presentable ∞-category. A 𝐷-valued
sheaf on 𝒀 is a limit-preserving functor 𝒀op → 𝐷. We write Sh(𝒀; 𝐷) ⊂ Fun(𝒀op , 𝐷) for
the full subcategory spanned by the 𝐷-valued sheaves. Note that Sh(𝒀; 𝐷) is equivalent
to the tensor product of presentable ∞-categories 𝒀 ⊗ 𝐷 [SAG, §1.3.1].
13.2.6 Definition. Let 𝒀 be an ∞-topos, and let 𝐶 be a δ0 -small ∞-category. Then a
sheaf 𝐹 on 𝒀 with values in PSh(𝐶) is said to be a lisse sheaf valued in 𝐶 if and only
if there exist a finite covering ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 1𝒀 , a collection {𝐾𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 of objects of 𝐶, and
equivalences between 𝐹|𝑈𝑖 and the constant sheaf on 𝒀/𝑈𝑖 at 𝐾𝑖 .
     Let 𝑆 be a spectral topological space, and let 𝑿 be an 𝑆-stratified ∞-topos. A sheaf 𝐹
on 𝑿 valued in PSh(𝐶) is said to be a constructible sheaf valued in 𝐶 if and only if there
exists a finite constructible stratification 𝑆 → 𝑃 such that for each element 𝑝 ∈ 𝑃, the
restriction 𝐹|𝑿𝑝 is a lisse sheaf valued in 𝐶. We write

                              Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶) ⊂ Sh(𝑿; PSh(𝐶))

for the full subcategory spanned by the constructible sheaves valued in 𝐶.
13.2.7. By definition, the ∞-category Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶) is the filtered colimit

                           Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶) ≃ colim Cons𝑃 (𝑿; 𝐶)
                                             𝑃∈FC(𝑆)

over the finite constructible stratifications 𝑆 → 𝑃.
    Let 𝑃 be a finite poset, 𝑍 ⊆ 𝑃 a closed subset, and 𝑈 ≔ 𝑃 ∖ 𝑍 the open complement
of 𝑍. Then Cons𝑃 (𝑿; 𝐶) is a recollement of Cons𝑍 (𝑿𝑍 ; 𝐶) and Cons𝑈 (𝑿𝑈 ; 𝐶):

                Cons𝑃 (𝑿; 𝐶) ≃ Cons𝑍 (𝑿𝑍 ; 𝐶)           ↓          Cons𝑈 (𝑿𝑈 ; 𝐶) .
                                                   Cons𝑍 (𝑿𝑍 ;𝐶)

13.2.8 Example. Let 𝑆 be a spectral topological space, and let 𝑿 be an 𝑆-stratified ∞-
topos. Using the identification of the ∞-category S of spaces as sheaves on Sπ with re-
spect to the effective epimorphism topology, we see that we have a natural identification

                                   𝑿𝑆-cons = Cons𝑆 (𝑿; Sπ ) .




                                             201
13.2.9 Example. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme and 𝑅 a finite ring. Note that the derived
∞-category D(𝑅) is compactly generated by Perf(𝑅) and D(𝑅) ⊂ PSh(Perf(𝑅)) is closed
under limits. Hence we have a natural identification
                                                   zar
                            Dcons (𝑋ét ; 𝑅) = Cons𝑋 (𝑋ét ; Perf(𝑅))

of the constructible derived ∞-category of étale sheaves of 𝑅-modules on 𝑋 with the
∞-category of constructible sheaves valued in Perf(𝑅) introduced in Definition 13.2.6.
   Now we turn to extending the Exodromy Theorem from constructible sheaves val-
ued in π-finite spaces, to constructible sheaves valued in any locally π-finite ∞-category.
13.2.10 Lemma. Let 𝐶 be a δ0 -small, locally π-finite ∞-category. Let 𝑆 be a spectral
topological space, and let 𝑿 be an 𝑆-stratified ∞-topos. Then the natural functor

                             colim Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶0 ) → Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶)
                           𝐶0 ∈Subπ (𝐶)

is an equivalence.
Proof. Since both source and target are filtered colimits over finite constructible stratifi-
cations of 𝑆, we are free to assume that 𝑆 = 𝑃 is a finite poset. By inducting on the rank
of 𝑃 and using the recollement decomposition of the source and the target, we may also
assume that 𝑃 is the trivial poset. Now if 𝐹 is a lisse sheaf in 𝐶 on 𝑿, then select a finite
covering ∐𝑖∈𝐼 𝑈𝑖 ↠ 1𝒀 , a collection {𝐾𝑖 }𝑖∈𝐼 of objects of 𝐶, and equivalences between
𝐹|𝑈𝑖 and the constant sheaf on 𝑿/𝑈𝑖 at 𝐾𝑖 . If 𝐶0 ⊆ 𝐶 denotes the full subcategory spanned
by the objects 𝐾𝑖 , then the sheaf takes values in PSh(𝐶0 ) (embedded in PSh(𝐶) via right
Kan extension).
13.2.11 Theorem. Let 𝐶 be a δ0 -small, locally π-finite category. Let 𝑆 be a spectral topo-
logical space, and 𝑿 an 𝑆-stratified ∞-topos. Then there is a natural equivalence
                                  ̂(∞,1)
                              Fun(Π 𝑆
                                         (𝑿), 𝐶) ≃ Cons𝑆 (𝑿; 𝐶) .

Proof. In light of (13.2.4) and Lemma 13.2.10, we may assume that 𝐶 is π-finite.
    Let 𝜫 ≔ Π ̂(∞,1)
                𝑆
                     (𝑿). By Proposition 13.1.14 and the fact that 𝐶 is π-finite, it follows
that for any integer 𝑛 ≥ 0, the natural functor

                     Fun(𝜫, Fun(𝐶op , Sπ,≤𝑛 )) → Fun(𝐶op , Fun(𝜫, Sπ,≤𝑛 ))

is an equivalence. Passing to colimits over 𝑛, we conclude that the natural functor

                       Fun(𝜫, Fun(𝐶op , Sπ )) → Fun(𝐶op , Fun(𝜫, Sπ ))

is an equivalence as well. Applying Fun(𝐶op , −) to the Exodromy Equivalence of Theo-
rem 10.1.8 to obtain an equivalence

                      Fun(𝜫, Fun(𝐶op , Sπ )) ≃ Cons𝑆 (𝑿; Fun(𝐶op , Sπ )) .

Now we conclude by observing that the functors Π̂(∞,1)
                                                 𝑆
                                                       (𝑿) → Fun(𝐶op , Sπ ) that land
in the essential image of the Yoneda embedding correspond under this equivalence to
the constructible sheaves valued in 𝐶.


                                             202
13.2.12 Corollary. Let 𝑋 be a coherent scheme and 𝑅 a finite ring. Then there is a natural
equivalence
                        Dcons (𝑋ét ; 𝑅) ≃ Fun(Gal(𝑋), Perf(𝑅)) .
13.2.13. Attached to any constructible sheaf 𝐹 of 𝑅-complexes on 𝑋, we have an asso-
ciated exodromy representation

                                  ρ𝐹 ∶ Gal(𝑋) → Perf(𝑅)

that is sufficient to reconstruct 𝐹.

13.3     Pyknotic spaces & pyknotic higher categories
In order to upgrade the coefficients in Corollary 13.2.12 from a finite discrete ring to
a more general topological ring, we make use of the pyknotic formalism. In this sec-
tion, we briefly describe the describe elements of the pyknotic formalism that we need
to extend our Exodromy Theorem to ℓ-adic sheaves. For more details on the pyknotic
formalism, we refer the reader to [15; 117; 118; 119].
13.3.1 Construction. Stone duality identifies the category Stn of Stone topological spaces
with the category Pro(Setﬁn ) of profinite sets. The subcategory 𝐸 ⊆ Stn consisting of ef-
fective epimorphisms is an 1-presite structure on Stn. We write eff ≔ 𝜏𝐸 for the resulting
finitary topology, the effective epimorphism topology.
13.3.2 Definition. A pyknotic space is a hypersheaf Stnop → Sδ1 for the effective epi-
morphism topology. We write
                                               hyp
                                 Pyk(S) ≔ Sheff (Stn; Sδ1 )

for the ∞-category of pyknotic spaces.
13.3.3 Warning. The category Stn is δ1 -small, but it is not δ0 -small. Moreover, there
does not exist a cofinal δ0 -small set of covering sieves of an object for the effective epi-
morphism topology. Consequently, when we speak of hypersheaves on Stn for the effec-
tive epimorphism topology, we have to consider hypersheaves valued in δ1 -small spaces.
The result will be a large ∞-topos, that is, a left exact δ1 -accessible localization of an ∞-
category Fun(𝐶op , Sδ1 ) of presheaves of δ1 -small spaces on a δ1 -small ∞-category 𝐶.
    Large ∞-topoi work exactly as do ∞-topoi, except that everything has to be shifted
one universe up. For example, if 𝑿 is bounded and coherent as a large ∞-topos, then
  coh
𝑿<∞   is only δ1 -small.
    An alternative to working with large ∞-topoi is considering instead only the acces-
sible hypersheaves Stnop → Sδ0 . These are what Clausen and Scholze call condensed
spaces. These do not form a ∞-topos (large or small), but in many ways the ∞-category
of accessible sheaves is relatively well behaved.
13.3.4. The large ∞-topos Pyk(S) is hypercomplete, coherent, and locally coherent
[SAG, Propositions A.2.2.2 & A.3.1.3].
    Let us identify two further generating sites for Pyk(S) – one larger and one smaller.


                                            203
13.3.5 Notation. Define full subcategories
                                     Proj ⊂ Comp ⊂ TSpc
as follows:
    → Comp is spanned by the compacta – i.e., compact Hausdorff topological spaces.
    → Proj is spanned by the projective compacta – i.e., compact Hausdorff topological
      spaces that are extremally disconnected [43; 73, Chapter III, §3.7].
    Equivalently, a topological space is a projective compactum if and only if it can be
exhibited as the retract of the Stone–Čech compactification β(𝑆) of some set 𝑆. In par-
ticular, Proj ⊂ Stn.
13.3.6. For every compactum 𝐾, there is a natural surjection β(𝐾disc ) ↠ 𝐾 from the
Stone–Čech compactification of the discrete topological space 𝐾disc with underlying set
𝐾 to 𝐾 (cf. [116, Remark 2.8]). Hence the subcategories Stn ⊂ Comp and Proj ⊂ Comp
are bases for the effective epimorphism topology on Comp (Definition 3.12.2). There-
fore, restriction of presheaves defines equivalences of ∞-categories
                    hyp                    hyp                 hyp
                 Sheff (Comp; Sδ1 ) ⥲ Sheff (Stn; Sδ1 ) ⥲ Sheff (Proj; Sδ1 )
with inverses given by right Kan extension (Proposition 3.12.11).
13.3.7 Warning. Since the 1-sites Comp and Stn have finite limits and the inclusion
Stn ↪ Comp preserves finite limits, Corollary 3.12.14 shows that restriction defines an
equivalence of 1-localic topoi
                            Sheff (Comp; Sδ1 ) ⥲ Sheff (Stn; Sδ1 ) .
    However, as pointed out to us by Clausen and Scholze, since the 1-site Proj of pro-
jective compacta does not have finite limits, and restriction only defines an equivalence
                              hyp                    hyp
                            Sheff (Comp; Sδ1 ) ⥲ Sheff (Proj; Sδ1 )
on topoi of hypersheaves.
13.3.8. Since Proj ⊂ Comp consists of projective objects of Comp, the Čech nerve of
any surjection in Proj is a split simplicial object. Hence by [SAG, Proposition A.3.3.1]
we see that a functor
                                     𝐹 ∶ Projop → Sδ1
is a sheaf with respect to the effective epimorphism topology if and only if 𝐹 carries
coproducts in Proj to products in S. That is to say, the category Pyk(S) is equivalent
to the ∞-category of functors Projop → Sδ1 that carry finite coproducts of projective
compacta to products.
    From this description, it is essentially immediate that the ∞-topos Sheff (Proj; Sδ1 ) is
Postnikov complete, whence we obtain an equivalence
                               hyp
                            Sheff (Comp; Sδ1 ) ≃ Sheff (Proj; Sδ1 )
(cf. [15, §2.4]). That is to say, the ∞-category Pyk(S) is the nonabelian derived ∞-cate-
gory or animation of the category Proj [HTT, §5.5.8; 22, §5.1]


                                             204
    This last description of the ∞-category of pyknotic spaces lets us define pyknotic
objects in any ∞-category with finite products.
13.3.9 Definition. Let 𝐶 be a ∞-category with all finite products. The ∞-category
Pyk(𝐶) of pyknotic objects of 𝐶 is the full subcategory of Fun(Projop , 𝐶) spanned by
those functors that carry finite coproducts of projective compacta to products in 𝐶.

13.3.10 Construction (discrete & indiscrete objects). Let 𝐶 be a δ1 -small presentable
∞-category. The global sections functor Pyk(𝐶) → 𝐶 is given by evaluation at the one-
point compactum ∗. For any pyknotic object 𝑌, we write 𝑌und ≔ 𝑌(∗). We call 𝑌und the
underlying object of 𝑌.
    Left adjoint to this is the constant sheaf functor 𝐶 → Pyk(𝐶) that carries an object
𝑋 ∈ 𝐶 to what we will call the discrete pyknotic object 𝑋disc attached to 𝑋. This pyknotic
object can be described explicitly: 𝑋disc is given by the assignment

                                𝐾 ↦ 𝑋𝐾 ≔ colim ∏ 𝑋 .
                                              𝐼∈Setﬁn
                                                   𝐾/ 𝑖∈𝐼



   The underlying space functor also admits a right adjoint 𝑋 ↦ 𝑋indisc . For an object
𝑋 ∈ 𝐶, the sheaf 𝑋indisc ∶ Projop → 𝐶 is given by the assignment

                                  𝐾 ↦ 𝑋|𝐾| ≔ ∏ 𝑋 ,
                                                 𝑘∈|𝐾|

i.e., the product of copies of 𝑋 indexed by the underlying set |𝐾| of the topological space
𝐾. We call 𝑋indisc the indiscrete pyknotic object attached to 𝑋.
      Both the discrete and indiscrete functors are fully faithful, so that

                              (𝑋disc )und = (𝑋indisc )und = 𝑋 .

Accordingly, we say that a pyknotic object in the essential image of 𝑋 ↦ 𝑋disc is discrete,
and a pyknotic object in the essential image of 𝑋 ↦ 𝑋indisc is indiscrete.

13.3.11 Example. Let 𝛱 → 𝑃 be a π-finite stratified space. Then the pyknotic ∞-cat-
egory 𝛱disc can be identified as above: 𝛱disc (𝐾) ≃ 𝛱𝐾 . Since profinite stratified spaces
embed fully faithfully into ∞-topoi (Proposition 8.8.6 & (8.3.11)), one obtains an equiv-
alence
                                                     ̃ 𝛱)
                             𝛱disc (𝐾) ≃ 𝛱𝐾 ≃ Fun∗ (𝐾,   ̃ ,

natural in 𝐾 and 𝛱.
13.3.12 Warning. The center 𝑋 ↦ 𝑋indisc is not the only point of the ∞-topos Pyk(S).
Let 𝑇 be a topological space. Define pyknotic set P𝑇 by sending 𝐾 to the quotient of the
set continuous maps 𝐾 → 𝑇 by the locally constant maps:

                        P𝑇 (𝐾) ≔ MapTSpc (𝐾, 𝑇)/ MaplcTSpc (𝐾, 𝑇) .

If 𝑇 is nonempty, then the pyknotic set P𝑇 has underlying set ∗; thus if 𝑇 is neither empty
nor ∗, then P𝑇 is a nontrivial pyknotic structure on the point. See [STK, Tag 0991].


                                           205
13.3.13 Example. For any finite set 𝐽, the discrete pyknotic set 𝐽disc is the sheaf
                                    𝐾 ↦ MapProj (𝐽, 𝐾)

represented by 𝐽. If {𝐽𝛼 }𝛼∈𝛬 is an inverse system of finite sets, then the limit
                                          lim 𝐽𝛼disc
                                          𝛼∈𝛬

is the sheaf represented by the Stone topological space lim𝛼∈𝛬 𝐽𝛼 ; this is not discrete. Cf.
(13.4.11).
13.3.14 Example. Write TSpccg ⊂ TSpc for the full subcategory spanned by the com-
pactly generated topological spaces. Then the functor TSpccg → Pyk(Set) given by the
assignment
                              𝑇 ↦ [𝐾 ↦ MapTSpc (𝐾, 𝑇)]
is a fully faithful right adjoint. See [15, Example 2.1.6; 118, Proposition 1.7].
     Now we may speak of pyknotic ∞-categories, which are nothing more than functors
Projop → Cat∞ that carry finite coproduts of projective compacta to the corresponding
finite products of ∞-categories. We can also interpret pyknotic ∞-categories as com-
plete Segal objects in pyknotic spaces:
13.3.15 Example. Corollary 13.1.8 provides equivalences
          Pyk(CO(S)) ≃ CO(Pyk(S))            and          Pyk(Cat∞ ) ≃ CS(Pyk(S)) .
In light of (13.1.2), for each integer 𝑛 ≥ 0, Corollary 13.1.8 provides an equivalence
                               Pyk(Cat𝑛 ) ≃ CS(Pyk(S≤𝑛−1 )) .
13.3.16 Definition. Let 𝐶 and 𝐷 be pyknotic ∞-categories. The ∞-category of contin-
uous functors 𝐶 → 𝐷 is the end

                        Functs (𝐶, 𝐷) ≔ ∫           Fun(𝐶(𝐾), 𝐷(𝐾)) .
                                           𝐾∈Proj

This is part of the natural enrichment of Pyk(Cat∞ ) over Cat∞ .
13.3.17 Example. Let 𝐶 and 𝐷 be pyknotic ∞-categories. As a complete Segal space,
the ∞-category Functs (𝐶, 𝐷) of continuous functors 𝐶 → 𝐷 is given by the assignment

    [𝑛] ↦ MapPyk(Cat∞ ) (𝐶 × [𝑛]disc , 𝐷) = ∫            MapCat∞ ((𝐶 × [𝑛]disc )(𝐾), 𝐷(𝐾)) .

13.4     Profinite spaces as pyknotic spaces
The goal of this section is to show that not only profinite sets embed into Pyk(S), but
profinite spaces actually embed into Pyk(S). The first approach one might have to show-
ing this is to try to show that π-finite spaces are cocompact when regarded as discrete
pyknotic spaces, as this implies that the proëxtension of the discrete functor
                                   (−)disc ∶ Sπ ↪ Pyk(S)


                                             206
is fully faithful. Unfortunately, this approach is destined for failure: finite sets aren’t even
cocompact objects of Pyk(S), as the following counterexample shows.
13.4.1 Counterexample. The finite set {0, 1} with two elements is not cocompact when
regarded as a discrete pyknotic space. Since the embedding of compactly generated topo-
logical spaces into Pyk(Set) preserves limits, and the image of a finite set 𝑆 under the
Yoneda embedding Pro(Setﬁn ) ↪ Pyk(Set) coincides with its image under the embed-
ding TSpccg ↪ Pyk(Set), to see that {0, 1} is not cocompact in Pyk(Set), it suffices to
prove that the discrete topological space {0, 1} is not cocompact in TSpccg . To see this,
let 𝑠 ∶ 𝐍 ↪ 𝐍 be the successor function 𝑛 ↦ 𝑛 + 1 and consider the diagram of discrete
topological spaces
                                     𝑠        𝑠      𝑠
                               ⋯         𝐍       𝐍       𝐍.
We claim that the map of sets

(13.4.2)               colim𝑛 Map(𝐍, {0, 1}) → Map(lim𝑛 𝐍, {0, 1})

is not a bijection. To see this, note that the limit lim𝑛 𝐍 empty, so Map(lim𝑛 𝐍, {0, 1})
has cardinality 1. On the other hand, Map(𝐍, {0, 1}) is the powerset P(𝐍) of 𝐍, and
the colimit colim𝑛 P(𝐍) along the inverse image maps 𝑠−1 ∶ P(𝐍) ↠ P(𝐍) has infinite
cardinality.
    The approach we take to show that profinite spaces embed into Pyk(S) is somewhat
indirect: we show that profinite sets form a basis for the effective epimorphism topology
on S∧π , so that hypersheaves on Pro(Setﬁn ) and S∧π coincide (Proposition 3.12.11). The
Yoneda embedding provides the desired embedding S∧π ↪ Pyk(S).
    In order to get this approach off the ground, we first need to talk about the effective
epimorphism topology on S∧π . The existence of this topology is immediate from [SAG,
Proposition A.3.2.1 & Theorem E.6.3.1].
13.4.3 Proposition. Write 𝐸 ⊆ S∧π for the subcategory of those morphisms 𝑒 ∶ 𝑋 → 𝑌 in
S∧π that can be written as an inverse limit of morphisms 𝑒𝛼 ∶ 𝑋𝛼 → 𝑌𝛼 where each 𝑒𝛼 is an
effective epimorphism of π-finite spaces. Then 𝐸 defines a ∞-presite structure on S∧π . We
write eff ≔ 𝜏𝐸 for the resulting finitary topology, the effective epimorphism topology.
13.4.4. From [SAG, Proposition A.3.3.1] it follows that the effective epimorphism topol-
ogy on S∧π is subcanonical. Moreover, since the Yoneda embedding

                                     S∧π ↪ Sheff (S∧π ; Sδ1 )

preserves δ0 -small limits, truncated objects of a ∞-topos are hypercomplete, and hy-
percomplete objects are closed under limits, the Yoneda embedding factors through
  hyp
Sheff (S∧π ; Sδ1 ).

    Now we show that Pro(Setﬁn ) ⊂ S∧π is a basis for the effective epimorphism topology
(in the sense of Definition 3.12.2). In fact, we show that every object of S∧π admits an
effective epimorphism from a profinite set. This requires a number of preliminaries.



                                              207
13.4.5. Note that for every space 𝑈 there exists an effective epimorphism π0 (𝑈) ↠ 𝑈.
In particular, Setﬁn ⊂ Sπ is a basis for the effective epimorphism topology and every
object admits a cover by a single object of Setﬁn .
    Since we must contend with proöbjects, it isn’t immediate from (13.4.5) that every
profinite space admits an effective epimorphism from a profinite set. To show this, we’ll
use the fact that we can always arrange to index a proöbject by a particularly nice poset.
13.4.6 Definition. We say that a poset 𝐴 is down-finite if for every element 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴, the
set {𝛽 ∈ 𝐴 | 𝛽 ≤ 𝛼} is finite.
13.4.7 Lemma ([SAG, Lemma E.1.6.4]). Let 𝐴′ be a filtered poset. Then there exists a
colimt-cofinal map of posets 𝐴 → 𝐴′ , where 𝐴 is a down-finite filtered poset.
13.4.8 Construction (rank function). If 𝐴 is a down-finite poset, then there exists a
map of posets
                                   rk ∶ 𝐴 → 𝐍
called the rank which is determined by the following requirement: for each 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴, the
number rk(𝛼) is the smallest natural number not equal to rk(𝛽) for 𝛽 < 𝛼 (cf. [HA,
Remark A.5.17]). In particular, rk(𝛼) = 0 if and only if 𝛼 is a minimal element of 𝐴.
13.4.9 Proposition. For every object 𝑋 ∈ S∧π , there exists an cover 𝑌 ↠ 𝑋 for the effective
epimorphism topology on S∧π for which 𝑌 ∈ Pro(Setﬁn ). In particular, Pro(Setﬁn ) ⊂ S∧π is
a basis for the effective epimorphism topology on S∧π .
Proof. To simplify notation, write 𝐶 ≔ Sπ and 𝐷 ≔ Setﬁn . Let {𝑋𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴op be an object
of Pro(𝐶). We without loss of generality assume that 𝐴 is a down-finite filtered poset
(Lemma 13.4.7). We construct a morphism 𝑒 ∶ {𝑌𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴op → {𝑋𝛼 }𝛼∈𝐴op in Pro(𝐶) such
that for each 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴, the morphism 𝑒𝛼 ∶ 𝑌𝛼 → 𝑋𝛼 is an effective epimorphism and 𝑌𝛼 ∈ 𝐷.
We construct this inductively on the rank of elements of 𝐴. For each 𝑛 ∈ 𝐍, write

                                𝐴 ≤𝑛 ≔ {𝛼 ∈ 𝐴 | rk(𝛼) ≤ 𝑛} .

    First, for each element 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴 with rk(𝛼) = 0 (i.e., minimal element of 𝐴), appealing
to (13.4.5), choose an effective epimorphism 𝑒𝛼 ∶ 𝑌𝛼 ↠ 𝑋𝛼 where 𝑌𝛼 ∈ 𝐷.
                                                                          op
    For the induction step, suppose that we have defined a functor 𝑌 ∶ 𝐴 ≤𝑛 → 𝐷 along
                                                                             op
with a natural effective epimorphism 𝑒 ∶ 𝑌 ↠ 𝑋|𝐴op≤𝑛 ; we now extend 𝑌 to 𝐴 ≤𝑛+1 as fol-
lows. For each 𝛼 ∈ 𝐴 with rk(𝛼) = 𝑛+1, consider the pulled-back effective epimorphism

                                      ∐ 𝑋𝛼 × 𝑌𝛽 ↠ 𝑋𝛼 .
                                                 𝑋𝛽
                                    𝛽<𝛼
                                  rk(𝛽)=𝑛

For each 𝛽 < 𝛼 with rk(𝛽) = 𝑛, appealing to (13.4.5) we choose an effective epimor-

composite
                                        ∐𝛽 𝑒𝛽′
                 𝑒𝛼 ∶ 𝑌𝛼 ≔ ∐ 𝑌𝛽′                      ∐ 𝑋𝛼 × 𝑌𝛽       𝑋𝛼 .
                                                            𝑋𝛽
                              𝛽<𝛼                   𝛽<𝛼

                                              op                               op
Then by construction the functor 𝑌 ∶ 𝐴 ≤𝑛 → 𝐷 extends to a functor 𝑌 ∶ 𝐴 ≤𝑛+1 → 𝐷
equipped with a natural effective epimorphism 𝑒 ∶ 𝑌 ↠ 𝑋|𝐴op≤𝑛+1 , as desired.
    As an immediate consequence of Proposition 3.12.11, we obtain the desired equiva-
lence on hypersheaves:

13.4.10 Corollary. Restriction of presheaves along the inclusion Pro(Setﬁn ) ↪ Sπ∧ defines
an equivalence of large ∞-topoi
                                    hyp
                                Sheff (S∧π ; Sδ1 ) ⥲ Pyk(S)

with inverse given by right Kan extension.

13.4.11. We finish this section by showing that the restricted Yoneda embedding

                                     よ ∶ Sπ ↪ Pyk(S)

agrees with the discrete functor Γ∗ ∶ Sπ ↪ Pyk(S). To see this, first note that the global
sections functor Γ∗ ∶ Pyk(S) → S is given by the composite

                                     (−)|Sπ
                           Pyk(S)                  Sheff (Sπ )   ∼   S

of restriction along the inclusion Sπ ↪ S∧π with evaluation at the terminal object. The
inverse equivalence S ⥲ Sheff (Sπ ) is given by sending a space 𝑌 to the functor

                                 MapS (−, 𝑌) ∶ Sop
                                                π →S.

Hence we have natural equivalences

                      MapPyk(S) (よ(𝐾), 𝑋) ≃ 𝑋(𝐾) ≃ 𝑋|Sop
                                                      π
                                                         (𝐾)
                                                   ≃ MapS (𝐾, Γ∗ (𝑋))
                                                   ≃ MapPyk(S) (Γ∗ (𝐾), 𝑋) .

    Since the Yoneda embedding よ ∶ S∧π ↪ Pyk(S) preserves inverse limits, we see that
the Yoneda embedding is the extension of the discrete functor Γ∗ ∶ Sπ ↪ Pyk(S) to
proöbjects.

13.5    Profinite stratified spaces as pyknotic ∞-categories
Our next goal is to show that profinite layered ∞-categories embed into Pyk(Cat∞ ).
We’ll deduce this from the fact that profinite spaces embed into Pyk(S) by regarding
profinite layered ∞-categories as complete Segal objects of S∧π .
   We consider a composite of three functors:
(1) The nerve provides an embedding N ∶ Layπ ↪ CS(Sπ ) of π-finite layered ∞-cat-
    egories into complete Segal objects in π-finite spaces. Extending the nerve to pro-
    öbjects provides an embedding N ∶ Pro(Layπ ) ↪ Pro(CS(Sπ )).


                                               209
(2) The proëxtension of the Yoneda embedding CS(Sπ ) ↪ CS(S∧π ) defines a functor

                                      Pro(CS(Sπ )) → CS(S∧π ) .

    Since the image of Yoneda embedding CS(Sπ ) ↪ CS(S∧π ) consists of cocompact
    objects (Corollary 13.1.13), applying [HTT, Proposition 5.3.5.11] we see that this
    functor is fully faithful.
(3) Corollary 13.4.10 proves an embedding S∧π ↪ Pyk(S). Passing to complete Segal
    objects, we obtain an embedding

                              CS(S∧π ) ↪ CS(Pyk(S)) ≃ Pyk(Cat∞ ) .

   We thus have fully faithful functors

                                  N
(13.5.1)             Pro(Layπ )       Pro(CS(Sπ ))      CS(S∧π )     Pyk(Cat∞ ) .

Since the embedding (13.5.1) preserves inverse limits and agrees with the discrete func-
tor (−)disc ∶ Layπ ↪ Pyk(Cat∞ ) when restricted to π-finite layered ∞-categories (13.4.11),
it is the extension to proöbjects of the discrete functor. Thus we have shown:
13.5.2 Proposition. The functor Pro(Layπ ) → Pyk(Cat∞ ) defined by extending the dis-
crete functor (−)disc ∶ Layπ ↪ Pyk(Cat∞ ) to proöbjects is fully faithful.
    In particular, we may now deduce a key almost-cocompactness result for profinite
stratified spaces, when regarded as pyknotic ∞-categories. The following result plays an
important role in the proof of Theorem 13.8.8.
13.5.3 Corollary. Let 𝜫 be a profinite layered ∞-category, let 𝑛 ≥ 0 be an integer, and
let 𝐷 ∶ 𝐴 → Pyk(Cat𝑛 ) be a filtered diagram. Then the natural functor

                      colim Functs (𝜫, 𝐷𝛼 ) → Functs (𝜫, colim𝛼∈𝐴 𝐷𝛼 )
                       𝛼∈𝐴

is an equivalence.
Proof. In light of Example 13.3.17 and (13.1.15), note that both 𝜫 and 𝜫 × [1] satisfy
the hypotheses of Proposition 13.1.14.
13.5.4 Example. Let 𝜫 be a profinite stratified space. We may now generalize Exam-
ple 13.3.11 as follows. Again since profinite stratified spaces embed fully faithfully into
∞-topoi, it follows that we obtain the following formula for 𝜫 as a pyknotic ∞-category:
                                                   ̃ 𝜫)
                                      𝜫(𝐾) ≃ Fun∗ (𝐾, ̃ ,

for any projective compactum 𝐾, functorially in 𝐾 and 𝜫. Thus, if 𝑿 → 𝑆̃ is a spectral
∞-topos, then one has
                            ̂(∞,1)
                            Π 𝑆                    ̃ 𝑿) ,
                                   (𝑿)(𝐾) ≃ Fun∗ (𝐾,
for any projective compactum 𝐾.


                                             210
   More generally, we may use this formula to define a more refined shape for any
bounded coherent ∞-topos 𝑿: the assignment
                                                  ̃ 𝑿)
                                        𝐾 ↦ Fun∗ (𝐾,
                                      pyk
defines a pyknotic ∞-category Π(∞,1) (𝑿) that we call the pyknotic shape of 𝑿. Following
Lurie’s work on ultracategories [86], it is reasonable to expect that the ∞-topos 𝑿 can
itself be reconstructed from the ∞-category of continuous functors
                                            pyk
